Monopoles And Landau-Ginzburg Models II: Floer Homology
Donghao Wang

Abstract
. This is the second paper in this series. Following the setup of Meng-Taubes, we define the monopole Floer homology for any pair ( Y , ω ) (Y,\omega) , where Y Y is a compact oriented 3-manifold with toroidal boundary and ω \omega is a suitable closed 2-form viewed as a decoration. This construction fits into a (3+1)-topological quantum field theory and generalizes the work of Kronheimer-Mrowka for closed oriented 3-manifolds. By a theorem of Meng-Taubes and Turaev, the Euler characteristic of this Floer homology recovers the Milnor-Turaev torsion invariant of the 3-manifold.



Part I
Introduction

1.1.
Motivations

The monopole Floer homology of a closed oriented 3-manifold as introduced by Kronheimer-Mrowka
[
KM07
]
has greatly influenced the study of 3-manifold topology since its inception. In this paper, we generalize their construction to the case of a compact oriented 3-manifold
Y
with toroidal boundary, which has the potential to recover the knot Floer homology
[
OS04
,
Ras03
]
, including both the hat-version
\widehat{\HFK}_{*}
and the minus-version
\HFK^{-}_{*}
as special cases. This generalization, however, depends on an additional choice of a closed 2-form
\omega
on the 3-manifold
Y
and a geometric datum
\mathfrak{d}
on the boundary
\partial Y
, and therefore should be viewed as an invariant of the pair
(Y,\omega)
relative to this boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}
. As we shall follow closely the setup of Meng-Taubes
[
MT96
]
, the Euler characteristic of this monopole Floer homology group recovers the Milnor-Turaev torsion invariant of
Y

[
MT96
,
Tur98
]
, which is independent of the 2-form
\omega
or the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}
.

The first paper of this series
[
Wan20a
]
focused on the symplectic geometry that underpins this Floer homology: we introduced an infinite dimensional gauged Landau-Ginzburg model for any Riemann surface
(\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})

(1.1)

(M(\Sigma),W_{\lambda},\mathcal{G}(\Sigma))

whose gauged Witten equations on the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
recover the Seiberg-Witten equations on the product manifold
\mathbb{C}\times\Sigma
. Explicitly,
M(\Sigma)
is an infinite dimensional Kähler manifold associated to the surface
\Sigma
, and
W_{\lambda}:M(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{C}
is a holomorphic Morse function, commonly referred to as a superpotential. The Seiberg-Witten equations on the 3-manifold
[0,+\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
forms a downward gradient flowline of the real function
\re W_{\lambda}
on
M(\Sigma)
. The monopole Floer homology group of
(Y,\omega)
is then interpreted as the Lagrangian Floer homology of an infinite dimensional Lagrangian submanifold in
M(\Sigma)
, which is associated to the 3-manifold
Y
using the Seiberg-Witten equations by a work of Nguyen
[
Ngu12
]
, with thimbles of the superpotential
W_{\lambda}:M(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{C}
.

The monopole Floer homology of
(Y,\omega)
, which we denote temporarily by
\HM_{*}(Y,\omega)
, fits into the broader program of constructing the Fukaya-Seidel category of (
1.1
) and developing an infinite dimensional Picard-Lefschetz theory, as proposed first by Donaldson-Thomas
[
DT98
]
and then by Haydys
[
Hay15
]
and Gaiotto-Moore-Witten
[
GMW15
,
GMW17
]
. A more detailed plan for this program has appeared in
[
Wan22
]
in an attempt to lift this monopole Floer homology into an
A_{\infty}
-module over the Fukaya-Seidel category of (
1.1
), providing more refined topological invariants for the 3-manifold
Y
.

The present paper focuses on the delicate analysis that implement this infinite dimensional construction for the underlying chain complexes of the
A_{\infty}
-category/module. The construction of the
A_{\infty}
-structure following
[
Wan22
]
will be explored in a companion paper in the future.

Another motivation of this work is to define Floer-type invariants for knots and links inside a closed 3-manifold using the Seiberg-Witten equations. It has been long believed
[
Man16
]
that the knot Floer homology of a knot
K\subset Z
, as introduced by Ozsváth-Szabó
[
OS04
]
and independently Rasmussen
[
Ras03
]
using Heegaard splittings, encodes some information about the Seiberg-Witten moduli space on
\mathbb{R}_{t}
times the knot complement
Z\setminus N(K)
. By choosing the closed 2-form
\omega
and the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}
properly, this speculation can be made concrete by applying our construction to the complement
Y=Z\setminus N(K)
or
Z\setminus N(K\cup m)
, where
m
is a meridian of the knot
K\subset Z
:

\displaystyle\HM_{*}(Y,\omega)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\cong]{\hskip 8.19447pt?\hskip 8.19447pt}\HFK^{-}_{*}(S^{3},K)

\displaystyle\text{ if }Y=S^{3}\setminus N(K),

\displaystyle\HM_{*}(Y,\omega)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\cong]{\hskip 8.19447pt?\hskip 8.19447pt}\widehat{\HFK}_{*}(S^{3},K)\text{ or }\KHM_{*}(S^{3},K)

\displaystyle\text{ if }Y=S^{3}\setminus N(K\cup m).

While the first isomorphism is mostly conjectural, we shall confirm the second isomorphism in the third paper
[
Wan20b
]
for the hat version of the knot Floer homology.

Some constructions of knot Floer homology already exist in the Seiberg-Witten theory. Motivated by the sutured manifold technique developed by Juhász
[
Juh06
,
Juh08
]
, the monopole knot Floer homology
\KHM_{*}
, which is the analogue of
\widehat{\HFK}_{*}
, was introduced by Kronheimer-Mrowka
[
KM10
]
as the sutured monopole Floer homology of the knot complement with two meridional sutures. By further exploring this idea, Li
[
Li19
]
constructed the analogue of
\HFK^{-}_{*}
in the Seiberg-Witten theory using a direct system of sutures on the knot complement. However, the fact that these invariants fit into a suitable topological quantum field theory was not verified until the foundational work of Juhász
[
Juh16
]
and Li
[
Li18
]
, due to some technical difficulty involved in these constructions.

As our approach is much more geometric, the functoriality of our Floer homology groups can be easily verified for a special class of cobordisms called
strict
. Following the setup of
[
MT96
]
, we complete
(Y,\partial Y)
into a 3-manifold with cylindrical ends using the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}
associated to
\partial Y
. The monopole Floer homology group of
(Y,\omega)
is then defined as an infinite dimensional Morse homology for the perturbed Chern-Simons-Dirac functional on this completion, while the cobordism maps are constructed by counting Seiberg-Witten monopoles on a completion of the strict cobordism, which is reminiscent of the original construction of Kronheimer-Mrowka
[
KM07
]
for the case of closed oriented 3-manifolds. However, this Floer homology group may depend on the choice of
\omega
and the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}
. We give a short discussion on this in Section
1.5
&
1.6
below.

1.2.
Outline of Construction

To state our results, let
Y
be a compact oriented 3-manifold whose boundary
\partial Y\cong\Sigma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\coprod_{1\leq i\leq n}\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}
is a union of 2-tori. Throughout this paper, we assume that
Y
is connected and its boundary
\partial Y
is non-empty. A boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
on
\Sigma
is a triple consisting of

•

a flat metric
g_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
;

•

an imaginary-valued harmonic 1-form
\lambda\in\Omega_{h}^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
;

•

an imaginary-valued harmonic 2-form
\mu\in\Omega_{h}^{2}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
,

which satisfies the conditions
(P0)
(P4)
and
(P5)
in Section
3
. This means in particular that
\lambda|_{\mathbb{T}_{i}^{2}}\neq 0
for all
1\leq i\leq n
. This datum
\mathfrak{d}
specifies the gauged Landau-Ginzburg model (
1.1
) associated to
\Sigma
. The 1-form
\lambda
is used to perturb the superpotential
W_{\lambda}
to make it Morse. Choose a closed 2-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
such that

\omega=\mu+ds\wedge\lambda

in a collar neighborhood
(-1,0]_{s}\times\Sigma\subset Y
. This pair
(Y,\omega)
along with other perturbation datum to be used in the construction is denoted by a thickened letter
\mathbb{Y}
in this paper; see Section
3
for the precise definition. We focus on the
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{std}
on
\Sigma
such that

c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{std})[\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}]=0

on each component
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}\subset\Sigma
. For any relative
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}
on
Y
, which is a
spin^{c}\
structure
\mathfrak{s}
along with an identification of
\mathfrak{s}
with
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{std}
on the boundary
\Sigma
, we follow the setup of Meng-Taubes
[
MT96
]
and associate a finitely generated module over a base ring
\mathcal{R}
:

(1.2)

\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}),

called
the monopole Floer homology group
of
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. This group is constructed as an infinite dimensional Morse homology of the perturbed Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
on the complete Riemannian manifold:

\widehat{Y}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Y\ \bigcup_{\Sigma}\ [0,+\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma,

where the cylindrical end is equipped with the product metric
d^{2}s+g_{\Sigma}
, and the closed 2-form
\omega
is extended constantly on the end, i.e.,

\omega=\mu+ds\wedge\lambda\text{ on }[0,+\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma.

The Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is perturbed by this extension of
\omega
. To think of it another way, the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
on
\Sigma
specifies the geometry of
(\widehat{Y},\omega)
along this cylindrical end; each Seiberg-Witten configuration on
\widehat{Y}
is required to approximate a distinguished critical point of the superpotential
W_{\lambda}:M(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{C}
as
s\to\infty
and hence is always irreducible. The critical points of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
are solutions to the perturbed 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equations on
\widehat{Y}
, while the Floer differential is defined by counting solutions on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
. The set of isomorphism classes of relative
spin^{c}\
structures on
Y
:

\Spincr(Y)

is a torsor over
H^{2}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})
. The desired invariant of
\mathbb{Y}
is obtained by forming the direct sum

(1.3)

\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in\Spincr(Y)}\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}).

This group carries the same formal structure of the monopole Floer homology for closed 3-manifolds: it is bigraded and admits an additional homology grading by homotopy classes of oriented relative 2-plane fields on
Y
(i.e., oriented 2-plane fields that take a standard form near
\Sigma
). If
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}
give rise to the same
spin^{c}\
structure, then their grading sets are the same; see Section
18
for more details. Moreover, a homology orientation of
Y
determines a canonical mod 2 grading of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
. One may take the base ring
\mathcal{R}
to be
\mathbb{Z}
, if
\mu=0
, and if the closed 2-form
\omega
is monotone in the sense of Definition
17.3
, or otherwise a Novikov ring over
\mathbb{Z}
.

1.3.
The Euler Characteristic and Finiteness

By
[
MT96
]
, for any closed oriented 3-manifold
Z
with
b_{1}(Z)\geq 2
, the Euler characteristic of the reduced monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}^{red}(Z)
as defined in
[
KM07
]
(with any generic non-exact perturbation) recovers the Milnor torsion invariant of
Z
. Since we have followed the same setup of Meng-Taubes
[
MT96
]
, the Euler characteristic of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
, which is the same as the signed count of critical points of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
on
\widehat{Y}
, recovers the Seiberg-Witten invariant
\SW(Y,\partial Y)
defined in their paper. In particular, it is independent of the 2-form
\omega
or the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
and recovers the Milnor-Turaev torsion of
(Y,\partial Y)
.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
MT96
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

For any compact oriented 3-manifold
(Y,\partial Y)
with toroidal boundary, the Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
categorifies the Milnor torsion invariant of
(Y,\partial Y)
; in particular,
\chi(\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))
is non-zero only for finitely many relative
spin^{c}\
structures
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in\Spincr(Y)
if
b_{1}(Y)\geq 2
.

Remark 1.2
.

Turaev
[
Tur98
]
later refined this result by showing that
\chi(\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}))
as a map

\Spincr(Y)\to\mathbb{Z}

agrees with the Milnor–Turaev invariant of
(Y,\partial Y)
up to an overall sign ambiguity. The version proved in
[
MT96
]
is slightly weaker: relative
spin^{c}\
structures with the same
c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\in H^{2}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})
are not distinguished. Readers are referred to their original papers for the precise statements.
∎

In light of Theorem
1.1
, it is natural to ask whether
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
enjoys a similar finiteness property when
b_{1}(Y)\geq 2
. Due to the presence of the 2-form
\omega
, this turns out to be a subtler question than the case of closed 3-manifolds, and the answer is unknown in general. We record a result along this line:

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose the harmonic 2-form
\mu\in\Omega^{2}_{h}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
in the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(\Sigma,\mu,\lambda)
is nowhere vanishing on
\Sigma
, i.e.,
\mu|_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}}\neq 0
,
1\leq i\leq n
. Then
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\neq 0
only for finitely many relative
spin^{c}\
structures, and so the monopole Floer homology group
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
is finitely generated over the Novikov Ring
\mathcal{R}
.

We remark that under the assumptions of Theorem
1.3
, the boundary
\Sigma
must be disconnected, since
\int_{\Sigma}\mu=\int_{Y}d\omega=0
; thus
b_{1}(Y)\geq|\Sigma|\geq 2
.

1.4.
The TQFT Property and Invariance

To state the
(3+1)
TQFT property enjoyed by
\HM_{*}
, we introduce the strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}=\Cob_{s}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{d})
associated to a surface
\Sigma
with a fixed boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
. Each object of
\Cob_{s}
is a pair
\mathbb{Y}=(Y,\omega)
consisting of a compact oriented 3-manifold with
\partial Y\cong\Sigma
and a closed 2-form
\omega
compatible with
\mathfrak{d}
. Ignoring many technical constraints, a morphism of
\Cob_{s}
from
\mathbb{Y}_{1}
to
\mathbb{Y}_{2}
, written as

\mathbb{X}=(X,W,\omega_{X}):\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2},\ \mathbb{Y}_{i}=(Y_{i},\omega_{i}),\ i=1,2,

is a 4-manifold with corners

(X,W):(Y_{1},\Sigma)\to(Y_{2},\Sigma)

such that the cobordism
W=[-1,1]_{t}\times\Sigma:\partial Y_{1}\to\partial Y_{2}
between the two boundaries is a standard product (so is the name
strict
). Moreover,
X
is equipped with a closed 2-form
\omega_{X}
compatible with the ones on
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
. This TQFT property can be stated easily, if
\mathfrak{d}
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
1.3
, and if one ignores the issue of orientations and works with the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients.

Theorem 1.4
.

Suppose the harmonic 2-form
\mu\in\Omega^{2}_{h}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
in the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(\Sigma,\mu,\lambda)
is nowhere vanishing on
\Sigma
, and we work with the Novikov ring
\mathcal{R}_{2}
with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients. Then the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}
extends to a covariant functor:

\HM:\Cob_{s}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{d})\to\mathcal{R}_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}

from the strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{d})
to the category of finitely generated
\mathcal{R}_{2}
-modules.

In general, the Floer homology group
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
might be infinitely generated over the base ring, and a completion of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
(as considered in
[
KM07
, Definition 23.1.3]
) is necessary in order to state this functoriality. In this paper, we work instead with the strict
spin^{c}\
cobordism category
\SCob_{s}=\SCob_{s}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{d})
, where each object
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is now equipped with a relative
spin^{c}\
structure, and whose morphism sets are same as those of
\Cob_{s}
. In order to deal with the orientation issue, one has to consider an enlargement
\SCob_{s,b}
of
\SCob_{s}
, which includes a base-point of the configuration space for each object
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\in\SCob_{s}
; see Definition
19.8
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the Novikov ring defined over
\mathbb{Z}
. Then the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}
extends to a covariant functor:

\HM:\SCob_{s,b}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{d})\to\mathcal{R}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}

with a modified composition law: if
\mathbb{X}_{13}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{3}
is the composition of
\mathbb{X}_{12}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
and
\mathbb{X}_{23}:\mathbb{Y}_{2}\to\mathbb{Y}_{3}
, then for any relative
spin^{c}\
structures
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}\in\Spincr(Y_{i}),i=1,3
, the map

\HM\big(\mathbb{X}_{13}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{3},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{3})\big):\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{3},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{3})

induced by
\mathbb{X}_{13}
is equal to the sum

\bigoplus_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in\Spincr(Y_{2})}\HM\big(\mathbb{X}_{23}:(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{3},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{3})\big)\circ\HM\big(\mathbb{X}_{12}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})\big).

which may involve infinitely many non-zeros terms; nevertheless, this sum converges in the topology of
\mathcal{R}
.

As a consequence of Theorem
1.5
, the monopole Floer homology group
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is invariant under the following operations (see Corollary
19.10
and
19.11
for more details):

•

changing of the base point, the interior metric of
Y
and the tame perturbation of the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
;

•

an isotopy to the identification map
\partial Y\cong\Sigma
;

•

replacing
\omega
by
\omega+d_{\widehat{Y}}b
where
b\in\Omega^{1}(\widehat{Y};i\mathbb{R})
is a compactly supported 1-form.

1.5.
A dichotomy I: when the harmonic 2-form
\mu
is nowhere vanishing

In order to obtain a topological invariant, one has to understand how the group
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
depends on the closed 2-form
\omega
and the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
. At this point, a striking dichotomy emerges from the non-vanishing property of the harmonic 2-form
\mu
, which can be seen already from the Finiteness Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
. In this case, one can verify that
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
depends at most on the cohomology class of
\omega
and
*_{\Sigma}\lambda
. The next theorem will be proved in the third paper
[
Wan20b
]
in this series.

Theorem 1.6

(
[
Wan20b
, Theorem 5.2]
)
.

Suppose that the harmonic 2-form
\mu\in\Omega^{2}_{h}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
is nowhere vanishing on
\Sigma
, and its paring with each component
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{j}
is sufficiently small for all
1\leq j\leq n
. Then the isomorphism class of the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
depends only on the 3-manifold
Y
, the cohomology class
[\omega]\in H^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
and
[*_{\Sigma}\lambda]\in H^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
.

It is not clear to the author, however, how the group
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
will be affected, as one changes the class
[\omega]
and
[*_{\Sigma}\lambda]
. In the case of closed 3-manifolds
[
KM07
, Section 31]
, using a closed 2-form as a non-exact perturbation
[
KM07
, Section 29]
, or using balanced perturbations yet with a non-trivial local coefficient system induced by this form, will produce the same reduced monopole Floer homology, so the dependence is understood via this local system. The similar structural result for
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
, however, is unknown and will require further exploration.

Nevertheless, in the third paper
[
Wan20b
]
, we shall establish a series of topological properties of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
, including a Thurston norm & fiberness detection result, which are independent of the choice of
[\omega]
and
[*_{\Sigma}\lambda]
. To certain extent,
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
behaves like the sutured Floer homology of
Y
with an empty set of sutures on the boundary
\partial Y
, despite the presence of the 2-form
\omega
.

It is somewhat disappointing as the cobordism map in Theorem
1.4
is only constructed for strict cobordisms. This failure is also remedied in
[
Wan20b
, Corollary 4.2]
where generalized cobordism maps are constructed. If
W:\partial Y_{1}\to\partial Y_{2}
is the restriction of
\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
between the two boundaries and is also equipped with a closed 2-form

\mathbb{W}=(W,\omega_{W}):(\partial Y_{1},g_{\Sigma_{1}},\lambda_{1},\mu_{1})\to(\partial Y_{2},g_{\Sigma_{2}},\lambda_{2},\mu_{2}),

then there is a map

\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{1})\otimes\HM_{*}(\mathbb{W})\to\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{2})

satisfying a modified composition law. When
\mathbb{W}
is the product cobordism
[-1,1]_{t}\times(\Sigma,g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
, it recovers the monopole Floer functor in Theorem
1.4
by inserting the canonical generator of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{W})\cong\mathcal{R}
. It is hoped that a contact structure on
W
determines a canonical element
[
KM97
,
OS05
,
HKM09
]
of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{W})
(which is still a missing ingredient in our story) and so a map
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{1})\to\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{2})
; this is then analogous to the sutured cobordism category considered in
[
Juh16
]
.

1.6.
A dichotomy II: when the harmonic 2-form
\mu
is somewhere zero

The results stated in the previous section all rely crucially on the property that
\mu
is nowhere vanishing on
\Sigma
; very little is known in the opposite case. This dichotomy can be explained conceptually as follows: the 2-form
\mu
determines a symplectic quotient of the infinite dimensional Kähler manifold
M(\Sigma)
by the gauge action and therefore may change the Fukaya-Seidel category of (
1.1
). In the previous case, this Fukaya-Seidel category is expected to be trivial, cf.
[
Wan20a
, Section 2.2]
, while in the opposite case it may have non-trivial product maps. Thus interesting wall-crossing phenomena will occur as we vary the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
with
\mu
somewhere zero.

In fact, the
\mu=0
case may produce a more canonical invariant for the 3-manifold
(Y,\partial Y)
. The author admits that this paper did
not
provide the most practical construction of the
\mu=0
version of the Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
. For instance, when
\Sigma
is connected, the technical conditions (cf. Section
3
) on the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
says that
\mu=0
and

•

[*_{\Sigma}\lambda]\in\im(H^{1}(Y;i\mathbb{R})\to H^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R}))
;

•

[\lambda]\neq 0\in H^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
is not proportional over
\mathbb{R}
to any integral class.

The second condition forces the flat metric
g_{\Sigma}
to be irrational, which is not practical for any computation. To see why this is needed for our construction, recall that the set of critical values of the superpotential
W_{\lambda}:M(\Sigma)\to\mathbb{C}
forms a lattice in
\mathbb{C}
, which is the image of

(1.4)

H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{C},\ x\mapsto 4\pi^{2}\bigg\langle x\cup\bigg([\frac{*_{\Sigma}\lambda}{2\pi i}]-i[\frac{\lambda}{2\pi i}]\bigg),[\Sigma]\bigg\rangle.

The gauge transformations coming from
Y
corresponds to the subgroup
\im H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\subset H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
, which translate this lattice in the imaginary direction. A relative
spin^{c}\
structure on
Y
is then identified with a lattice point up to this vertical translation; see Figure
1
below. The Seiberg-Witten equations on the 3-manifold
[0,+\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
(the cylindrical end of
\widehat{Y}
) is modeled on the downward gradient equation

(1.5)

p:[0,+\infty)_{s}\to M(\Sigma),\ \partial_{s}p(s)+\nabla\re(W_{\lambda})(p(s))=0,

along which
\im(W_{\lambda})(p(s))
is constant. The irrationality of
g_{\Sigma}
is to ensure that the critical values in (
1.4
) all have distinct imaginary parts. This implies that any finite energy flowlines of
-\nabla\re W_{\lambda}
on
\mathbb{R}_{s}
must be constant, and hence the moduli space on the 3-manifold
\widehat{Y}
is compact.

The most geometric setup one would hope is to identify
\Sigma
isometrically as
(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})_{\theta_{1}}\times(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z})_{\theta_{2}}
by fixing a meridian and a longitude on
\Sigma=\partial Y
with
[\frac{\lambda}{2\pi i}]=\frac{d\theta_{1}}{2\pi}
dual to the meridian and
[\frac{*_{\Sigma}\lambda}{2\pi i}]=\frac{d\theta_{2}}{2\pi}
dual to the longitude. The lattice (
1.4
) then becomes

4\pi^{2}(\mathbb{Z}\oplus i\mathbb{Z}).

A serious non-compactness issue, however, will occur in this case for the Seiberg-Witten moduli space on the 3-manifold
\widehat{Y}
. For
Y=S^{1}\times D^{2}
and the relative
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{k}
on
Y
with
\langle c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{k}),[D^{2}]\rangle=2k+1,k\geq 0
, this moduli space agrees with the vortex moduli space on
D^{2}
:

(1.6)

\Sym^{k}D^{2}\cong\mathbb{C}^{k},

thinking of
D^{2}
as a surface with a cylindrical end. To get rid of the non-compactness issue, one has to modify the Seiberg-Witten equations on
[0,+\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
so that it is modeled on the perturbed equation:

(1.7)

p:[0,+\infty)_{s}\to M(\Sigma),\ \partial_{s}p(s)+\nabla\re(e^{-i\eta}W_{\lambda})(p(s))=0,

where
W_{\lambda}
is multiplied by some
e^{-i\eta}\in S^{1}
with
0<|\eta|\ll 1
. The projection of such a flowline under
W_{\lambda}
follows the direction of
-e^{i\eta}
; see Figure
1
below. The subtlety arises from the fact that this
\eta
must change for different relative
spin^{c}\
structures in order to upgrade the Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
into an
A_{\infty}
-module over the Fukaya-Seidel category of (
1.1
). To verify that this
A_{\infty}
-module is independent of the choice of
\eta^{\prime}s
up to canonical quasi-isomorphisms requires additional technical inputs, which are not addressed in the present paper. In fact, this Floer homology will depend on the sign of
\eta
: for
Y=S^{1}\times D^{2}
,
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{k})
has rank
1
in homology grading
0
if
\eta>0
and
2k
if
\eta<0
, corresponding to the homology and relative homology of the symmetric product (
1.6
) respectively.

\begin{overpic}[Pic1.png]
\put(48.0,19.0){$\eta<0$}
\put(48.0,7.0){$\eta>0$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 1
.
An irrational lattice (left) versus the standard lattice (right)
\subset\mathbb{C}

In the finite dimensional case, this wall-crossing phenomenon (as one changes the sign of
\eta
’s) is understood via Seidel’s generating theorem and has been reformulated in
[
Wan22
]
; see
[
Wan22
, Theorem 1.9 (3)]
. The generalization to the Seiberg-Witten theory will appear in a companion paper in the future.

Remark 1.7
.

By the time of preparing this paper, the framework of
[
Wan22
]
was not available, so we restrict ourself here to the non-practical case (with
\eta=0
and irrational
g_{\Sigma}
) for the proof. Nevertheless, the analytic foundation laid out in this paper will apply also to the general case (with nonzero
\eta
’s) with minor changes following
[
Wan22
]
.
∎

1.7.
Relations with embedded contact homology

The irrational metric
g_{\Sigma}
treated in this paper looks awkward at first sight, but a similar setup already appeared in the context of embedded contact homology. Following an earlier work of Hutchings-Sullivan
[
HS05
]
on periodic Floer homology, Colin-Ghiggini-Honda
[
CGH10
]
introduced a version of embedded contact homology for a contact 3-manifold
(Y,\alpha)
with torus boundary when the boundary
\partial Y
is invariant under the Reeb flow of the contact 1-form
\alpha
. This homology is more canonically defined in the case that
\partial Y
is foliated by this Reeb flow with irrational slope so that
J
-holomorphic curves in the symplectization
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times Y
will not intersect the boundary
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\partial Y
by a blocking lemma; cf.
[
CGH10
, Section 5.2]
. When
\partial Y
is foliated by a circle family of closed Reeb orbits of
\alpha
, this family is then treated as a Morse-Bott singularity and gives arise to a pair of elliptic and hyperbolic Reeb orbits after perturbing
\alpha
.

We expect that this version of embedded contact homology is isomorphic to the monopole Floer homology introduced in this paper at least in the irrational case and if
g_{\Sigma}
is chosen suitably according to
\alpha
. In any case, it is a challenging task to understand the dependence of this Floer homology on the irrational slope of the Reeb flow of
\alpha
on
\partial Y
or on the irrational metric
g_{\Sigma}
.

1.8.
Relations with knot Floer homology: some speculation

The simplest examples of
(Y,\partial Y)
arise from the knot complement for a knot
K\subset S^{3}
. In this case, there exists a unique
spin^{c}\
structure
\mathfrak{s}
on
(Y,\partial Y)
, and
\Spincr(Y)
is a torsor over

(1.8)

H^{1}(\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})/\im H^{1}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}.

A choice of
[\frac{*_{\Sigma}\lambda}{2\pi i}]\in\im(H^{1}(Y;i\mathbb{R})\to H^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R}))
specifies an isomorphism of (
1.8
). The Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
then carries a bi-grading of
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
. The first grading arises from relative
spin^{c}\
structures with

\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}+n)=\{0\}

when
n\gg 1
under (
1.8
), while the second arises from the homology grading by oriented relative 2-plane fields. The group
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
shares the same Euler characteristic and formal structures as the knot Floer homology
\HFK^{-}_{*}(S^{3},K)
. But one ingredient is missing here:
\HFK^{-}_{*}(S^{3},K)
is an
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U]
-module with
\deg U=(-1,-1)
.

If
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
can be lifted into an
A_{\infty}
-module over the Fukaya-Seidel category
\mathcal{A}
of (
1.1
), then by passing to homology,
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
is a module over the algebra
H(\mathcal{A})
, which may supplement the
U
-action in our story. Using the geometric setup from Section
1.6
, this may provide a gauge theoretic construction of
\HFK^{-}_{*}(S^{3},K)
as an
\mathbb{F}_{2}[U]
-module. It is hoped that the original
A_{\infty}
-module (the chain-level invariant) may provide refined information about the knot
K\subset S^{3}
.

On the other hand, pick a meridian
m
of
K\subset S^{3}
, and consider the link complement
Y_{K}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}S^{3}\setminus N(K\cup m)
. By gluing the two boundary components of
Y_{K}
(using a suitable orientation reversing diffeomorphism), we obtain a closed 3-manifold
\tilde{Y}_{K}
. We will establish an internal gluing theorem in the third paper
[
Wan20b
]
to identify
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{K})
with the monopole Floer homology of the closure
\tilde{Y}_{K}
, which is isomorphic to
\KHM_{*}(S^{3},K)
by
[
KM10
]
. This gluing result falls into Case I of the dichotomy and therefore can be easily understood. Interested readers are referred to
[
Wan20a
, Section 2]
for more heuristics on this gluing formula.

1.9.
Organizations

To define the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
and implement the construction sketched in Section
1.2
, we address five analytic problems in this paper, as summarized below. We follow closely the plotline of
[
KM07
]
.

Compactness.
To obtain the correct compactification of moduli spaces on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
, we have to study the equations over the planar end of
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
:

(1.9)

\mathbb{H}^{+}\times\Sigma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma,

where
\mathbb{H}^{+}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,+\infty)_{s}
is furnished with the Euclidean metric. At this point, we make essential use of results from the first paper
[
Wan20a
]
. Our constraints on the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
are intended to make the following properties hold:

•

finite energy solutions are trivial on
\mathbb{C}\times\Sigma
, namely, they have to be
\mathbb{C}
-translation invariant up to gauge
[
Wan20a
, Theorem 1.2 or 8.1]
.

•

finite energy solutions on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
are trivial, namely, they have to be
\mathbb{R}_{s}
-translation invariant up to gauge. This result is due to Taubes; see
[
Tau01
, Proposition 4.4 & 4.7]
or
[
Wan20a
, Proposition 10.1 & 10.3]
for a version that we exploit.

In Part
II
, we first set up the strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}
and derive an energy identity for the Seiberg-Witten equations on
\widehat{Y}
. Combining with the results in
[
Wan20a
]
, we establish the compactness theorem in Section
6
. Part
II
is the counterpart of
[
KM07
, Section 4, 5, 16]
.

Perturbations.
To achieve the regularity of moduli spaces, we apply a further perturbation to the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
confined in the compact region

Y=\{s\leq 0\}\subset\widehat{Y}.

Thus the monopole equations always take a standard form on the planar end
\mathbb{H}^{+}\times\Sigma
. The cylinder functions that we use here are slightly different from those in
[
KM07
, Section 11]
since global gauge fixing conditions never give rise to such perturbations, in the sense of Definition
7.1
. Inspired by holonomy perturbations from instanton Floer homology, we will look at embeddings of
S^{1}\times D^{2}
into
Y
instead. The construction is carried out in details in Part
III
, as the counterpart of
[
KM07
, Section 10, 11]
.

Linear Analysis.
This part is more or less standard. Since
\widehat{Y}
is non-compact, the extended Hessian of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
on
\widehat{Y}
is a self-adjoint operator with essential spectrum, a major distinction of this theory from the case of closed 3-manifolds. Fortunately, this essential spectrum is disjoint from the origin, so it makes sense to speak of the spectrum flow when perturbed by compact operators. We will follow the setup of
[
RS95
]
and summarize relevant results in Part
IV
, as the counterpart of
[
KM07
, Section 17]
.

Unique Continuation.
As our perturbation space is not large enough, we need a stronger unique continuation property to achieve transversality. The non-linear version is stated as follows: if two solutions
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
to the perturbed monopole equations on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
are gauge equivalent on the slice

\{0\}\times Y\text{ with }Y=\{s\leq 0\}\subset\widehat{Y},

then they are gauge equivalent on the whose space. The proof relies on the Carleman estimates from
[
Kim95
]
. Part
V
is the counterpart of
[
KM07
, Section 7, 12, 15]
. The proof of transversality is accomplished in Section
16
.

Orientations.
To work with a Novikov ring
\mathcal{R}
with integral coefficients, we have to orient moduli spaces coherently. For closed 3-manifolds, this is done by first looking at reducible configurations in the blown-up space. See
[
KM07
, Section 20]
for details. In our case, we have to adopt a different approach as configurations are never reducible and the action of the gauge group is free.

Our situation here is similar to that of
[
KM97
]
, where a Riemannian 4-manifold with a conic end is considered, so one may follow the argument of
[
KM97
, Appendix]
to orient moduli spaces. The key ingredients are relative determinant line bundles or
relative orientations
that compare two Fredholm operators. We adopt a more direct approach to this notion without referring to either
K
-theory or the proof of the index theorem
[
AS68
]
. This combinatoric construction is based on a simple proof of the excision principle due to Mrowka and is carried out in Appendix
A
.

Part
VI
is the counterpart of
[
KM07
, Section 20, 22, 28]
. The canonical grading of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y})
by homotopy classes of oriented relative 2-plane fields is introduced in Section
18
. We will first define the monopole Floer homology of
\mathbb{Y}
using
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients in Section
17
, and then address the orientation issue in Section
19
.

Most results and proofs in the present paper are intended to generalize the ones in
[
KM07
]
. Readers are assumed to have a reasonable understanding of the monopole Floer homology of closed 3-manifolds, at least in the case that
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})
is non-torsion.

Remark 1.8
.

The following results, however, are not covered in this paper:

•

the exponential decay of solutions in the time-direction, cf.
[
KM07
, Section 13]
;

•

the gluing theorem, cf.
[
KM07
, Section 18, 19]
;

since they will follow immediately from
[
KM07
]
, once we set up the rest of the theory correctly.
∎

Acknowledgments.
The author would like to thank his advisor Tom Mrowka for introducing him to this subject, for suggesting the present problem, and for his patient help and constant encouragement throughout this project. The author would also like to thank Micheal Hutchings, Chris Gerig, Siqi He, Lante Ma, Jianfeng Lin, Matt Stoffregen, Guangbo Xu, Yuan Yao and Boyu Zhang for helpful conversations. This work is partially supported by NSF through his thesis advisor’s award DMS-1808794.

Part II
Three-Manifolds with Toroidal Boundary

In this part, we introduce the strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}
of compact oriented 3-manifolds with toroidal boundary and study the Seiberg-Witten equations on the completion of these manifolds. Throughout this paper, we use
(\Sigma,g_{\Sigma})
to denote a disjoint union of 2-tori equipped with a flat metric
g_{\Sigma}
. Although most results in the first paper
[
Wan20a
]
do not require the flatness of
g_{\Sigma}
, it is assumed here in order to apply a theorem of Taubes; see Theorem
2.6
below.

For any compact oriented 3-manifold
(Y,\partial Y)
with toroidal boundary
\partial Y\cong\Sigma
, we attach a cylindrical end to obtain a complete Riemannian 3-manifold

\widehat{Y}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Y\ \bigcup_{\Sigma}\ [0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma.

Any strict cobordism between such manifolds
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}

(X,[-1,1]_{t}\times\Sigma):(Y_{1},\partial Y_{1})\to(Y_{2},\partial Y_{2}),

is then associated with a complete Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{X}
with a planar end:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(-\infty,-1]_{t}\times\widehat{Y}_{1}\ \bigcup\ \widehat{X}\ \bigcup\ [1,+\infty)_{t}\times\widehat{Y}_{2}\text{ with }

\displaystyle\widehat{X}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}X\ \bigcup\ [-1,1]_{t}\times[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma.

The manifold
\widehat{X}
is obtained from
X
by completing only in the spatial direction, which gives a cobordism from
Y_{1}
from
Y_{2}
.

Part II
is devoted to the proof of the Compactness Theorem
6.1
for the Seiberg-Witten moduli spaces on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
and
\mathcal{X}
, which is essential to the construction of Floer homology and cobordism maps. The proof in turn relies on three important properties for the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations:

(K1)

there is a uniform upper bound on the analytic energy;

(K2)

finite energy solutions are trivial on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
; in other words, they are gauge equivalent to the unique
\mathbb{C}_{z}
-translation invariant solution on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
; see Theorem
2.4
below.

(K3)

finite energy solutions on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
are trivial; in other words, they are gauge equivalent to the unique
\mathbb{R}_{s}
-translation invariant solution on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
. This result is due to Taubes and requires
g_{\Sigma}
to be flat; see Theorem
2.6
below.

For these properties to hold, a suitable closed 2-form is needed to perturb the Seiberg-Witten equations. In Section
2
, we summarize a list of results from the first paper
[
Wan20a
]
and spell out the requirement for this 2-form, which will then ensure
(K2)
and
(K3)
.

The rest of Part
II
is organized as follows. In Section
3
, we define the strict cobordism category and set up the configuration spaces on
\widehat{Y}
and
\widehat{X}
respectively. In Section
4
, we prove that the quotient configuration space in our case is still Hausdorff and remains a Hilbert manifold after Sobolev completions.

The closed 2-form
\omega
on
Y
is chosen such that the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations on the cylindrical end
[0,+\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
is modeled on the equation (
1.5
), and therefore the energy equation can be derived easily. This is carried out in detail in Section
5
and gives the first property
(K1)
. We remark that in order to derive the similar energy equation for the perturbed version (
1.7
) in Section
1.6
, one must follow the computation in
[
Wan22
, Section 2]
, which will complicate the story significantly.

Finally, the Compactness Theorem
6.1
is stated and proved in Section
6
. The analogue of Theorem
6.1
in the case of Landau-Ginzburg models is
[
Wan22
, Proposition 3.5]
, whose proof has followed the same argument and is technically easier.

2.
Results from the First Paper

In this section, we summarize a list of results from the first paper
[
Wan20a
]
, which are concerned with the geometry of the planar end of
\mathcal{X}
. They are used in an essential way in the proof of the Compactness Theorem
6.1
in Section
6
. We focus on the case that
\Sigma
is connected for their statements.

2.1.
Review

Let
X
be any smooth oriented 4-manifold equipped with a metric. A
spin^{c}\
structure
\mathfrak{s}=(S_{X},\rho_{4})
on
X
is a pair consisting of a Hermitian vector bundle
S_{X}=S^{+}\oplus S^{-}
with
\rank S^{\pm}=2
, called the spin bundle, and a smooth bundle map
\rho_{4}:T^{*}X\to\End(S_{X})
that defines the Clifford multiplication. A Seiberg-Witten configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
on
(X,\mathfrak{s})
then consists of a smooth
spin^{c}\
connection
A
and a smooth section
\Phi
of
S^{+}
. The space of all such configurations, which we denote by
\mathcal{C}(X,\mathfrak{s})
, is acted on by the gauge group
\mathcal{G}(X)=\map(X,S^{1})
by the formula

u\in\mathcal{G}(X):\mathcal{C}(X,\mathfrak{s})\to\mathcal{C}(X,\mathfrak{s}),\ (A,\Phi)\mapsto(A-u^{-1}du,u\Phi).

The Seiberg-Witten equations on the
spin^{c}\
manifold
(X,\mathfrak{s})
are invariant under this gauge action and can be perturbed a closed 2-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(X;i\mathbb{R})
. Let
A^{t}
denote the induced connection of
A
on
\Lambda^{0,1}S^{+}
and
\omega^{+}
the self-dual component of
\omega
. Then the perturbed equations take the form

(2.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r}\frac{1}{2}\rho_{4}(F_{A^{t}}^{+})-(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}-\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})=0,\\
D_{A}^{+}\Phi=0,\end{array}\right.

where
D_{A}^{+}:\Gamma(S^{+})\to\Gamma(S^{-})
is the Dirac operator, and
(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}=\Phi\Phi^{*}-\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|^{2}\otimes\Id_{S^{+}}
denotes the traceless part of the endomorphism
\Phi\Phi^{*}:S^{+}\to S^{+}
,

Let
\Sigma=(\mathbb{T}^{2},g_{\Sigma})
be a 2-torus equipped with a
flat
metric. In the case that
X=\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
a Kähler manifold, furnished with the product metric and oriented by the complex structure, the equations (
2.1
) can be understood more explicitly as follows.

Let
dvol_{\mathbb{C}}
(resp.
dvol_{\Sigma}
) denote the volume form of
\mathbb{C}
(resp.
\Sigma
). The symplectic form on
X
is given by the sum
\omega_{sym}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}dvol_{\mathbb{C}}+dvol_{\Sigma}
. The spin bundle
S^{+}
is acted on by
\rho_{4}(\omega_{sym})
and splits as
L^{+}\oplus L^{-}
such that

\rho_{4}(\omega_{sym})=\begin{pmatrix}-2i&0\\
0&2i\end{pmatrix},

i.e.,
L^{\pm}
is the
\mp 2i
eigen-subbundle of
\rho_{4}(\omega_{sym})
. Write
\Phi=(\Phi_{+},\Phi_{-})
with
\Phi_{\pm}\in\Gamma(X,L^{\pm})
.

We focus on the
spin^{c}\
structure on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
with

c_{1}(S^{+})[\Sigma]=0,

so both
L^{+}
and
L^{-}
are topologically trivial.

Let
z=t+is
be the coordinate function on
\mathbb{C}_{z}
. Then the Clifford multiplication
\rho_{4}:T^{*}X\to\Hom(S,S)
can be constructed by setting:

\displaystyle\rho_{4}(dt)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\Id\\
\Id&0\\
\end{pmatrix},\ \rho_{4}(ds)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma_{1}\\
\sigma_{1}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}:\ S^{+}\oplus S^{-}\to S^{+}\oplus S^{-},

where
\sigma_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}i&0\\
0&-i\\
\end{pmatrix}:S^{+}=L^{+}\oplus L^{-}\to L^{+}\oplus L^{-}
is the first Pauli matrix. If one identifies
L^{+}\cong\mathbb{C}
and
L^{-}\cong\Lambda^{0,1}\Sigma
, then for any
x\in\Sigma
and
w\in T_{x}\Sigma
,
\rho_{4}(w)
is determined by the relation

\rho_{3}(w)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\rho_{4}(dt)^{-1}\cdot\rho_{4}(w)=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\iota(\sqrt{2}w^{0,1})\ \cdot\\
\sqrt{2}w^{0,1}\otimes\cdot&0\\
\end{pmatrix}:S^{+}\to S^{+},

Remark 2.1
.

The Clifford multiplications in dimension
3
and
4
are denoted respectively by
\rho_{3}
and
\rho_{4}
in this paper. Identify
\mathbb{C}_{z}
as
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
, then they are related by

\rho_{3}(w)=\rho_{4}(dt)^{-1}\cdot\rho_{4}(w):S^{+}\to S^{+},

for any
w\in T^{*}(\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma)
. In particular,
\rho_{3}(ds)=\sigma_{1}
.
∎

The symplectic form
\omega_{sym}
is parallel, and so is the splitting
S^{+}=L^{+}\oplus L^{-}
. As a result, any
spin^{c}\
connection
A
then takes a diagonal form

\nabla_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{A_{+}}&0\\
0&\nabla_{A_{-}}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

We also regard
L^{+}=\mathbb{C}
and
L^{-}=\Lambda^{0,1}\Sigma
as bundles over
\Sigma
. Let
\check{B}_{*}=(d,\nabla^{LC})
denote the reference connection on
\mathbb{C}\oplus\Lambda^{0,1}\Sigma\to\Sigma
. A reference connection
A_{*}
on
S^{+}\to X
is then obtained by setting

(2.2)

\nabla_{A_{*}}=dt\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+ds\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+\nabla_{\check{B}_{*}}.

One can easily check that
A_{*}
is a
spin^{c}\
connection. Any
spin^{c}\
connection on
X
then takes the form
A=A_{*}+a
for an imaginary valued 1-form
a\in\Omega^{1}(X;i\mathbb{R})
. Their curvature tensors are related by

F_{A}=F_{A_{*}}+d_{X}a\otimes\Id_{S},\text{ so }F_{A^{t}}=F_{A^{t}_{*}}+2d_{X}a.

2.2.
Point-Like Solutions

Now we recall the list of results from
[
Wan20a
]
which we shall use in the proof of Theorem
6.1
. Let
X=\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
. For our applications, the 2-form perturbation that appears in the Seiberg-Witten equations (
2.1
) will take the special form

\omega\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mu+ds\wedge\lambda

where

•

\lambda\in\Omega_{h}^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
is an imaginary-valued harmonic 1-form on
\Sigma
, and

•

\mu\in\Omega_{h}^{2}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
is an imaginary-valued harmonic 2-form.

Since the metric
g_{\Sigma}
is flat, the 2-form
\omega
is parallel on
X=\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
. The pair
(\lambda,\mu)
is subject to further conditions in order to deduce the properties
(K2)
and
(K3)
from the first paper
[
Wan20a
]
.

Assumption 2.2
.

The pair
(\lambda,\mu)\in\Omega_{h}^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})\times\Omega_{h}^{2}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
is said to be admissible if
\lambda\neq 0
, and if one of the following two conditions holds:

(\mathrm{W}1)

\mu\neq 0
;

(\mathrm{W}2)

\lambda
is not the real multiple of any integral class in
H^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{Z})\subset\Omega_{h}^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
.

The pair
(\lambda,\mu)
is always assumed to be admissible in this paper.

For the rest of this section, we state the main results of
[
Wan20a
]
and explain why Assumption
2.2
is crucial. First, recall the definition of the local energy functional associated to any configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
on
X
.

Definition 2.3

(
[
Wan20a
, Definition 8.3]
)
.

For any region
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}_{z}
and any configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
, define
the local energy functional
of
\gamma
over
\Omega
as

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{1}{4}|F_{A^{t}}|^{2}+|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})|^{2}.\qed

A solution
\gamma
to the Seiberg-Witten equations (
2.1
) is called
point-like
if its global energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;\mathbb{C}_{z})
is finite. There is a constant solution
\gamma_{*}=(A_{*},\Phi_{*})
on the 4-manifold
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
with
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma_{*};\mathbb{C}_{z})=0
. The
spin^{c}\
connection of
\gamma_{*}
is given by the formula (
2.2
), while the spinor
\Phi_{*}
takes the form

(r_{+},\sqrt{2}\lambda^{0,1}r_{-})\in\Gamma(X,\mathbb{C}\oplus\Lambda^{0,1}\Sigma),

where
r_{\pm}
are real numbers subject to the relations:

r_{+}r_{-}=1\text{ and }\frac{i}{2}(|r_{+}|^{2}-|r_{-}|^{2}|\lambda|^{2})=-*_{\Sigma}\mu.

See
[
Wan20a
, Section 7.2]
. In particular,
\Phi_{*}
is parallel with respect to
A_{*}
.

One important consequence of Assumption
2.2
is that
\gamma_{*}
will be the only point-like solution on
X
up to gauge. For practical reasons, we give a more general statement. Let
I_{n}=[n-2,n+2]_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
. Choose a compact domain
\Omega_{0}\subset I_{0}\times[0,\infty)_{s}
with a smooth boundary such that

(2.3)

I_{0}\times[1,3]_{s}\subset\Omega_{0}\subset I_{0}\times[0,4]_{s}.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
S\in\mathbb{R}_{s}
, define
\Omega_{n,S}\subset\mathbb{C}
to be the translated domain

(2.4)

\Omega_{n,S}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(t,s):(t-n,s-S)\in\Omega_{0}\}\subset I_{n}\times[0,\infty)_{s}.

Theorem 2.4

(
[
Wan20a
, Proposition 8.3]
)
.

If
\lambda\neq 0
, then there exists a constant
\epsilon_{*}=\epsilon_{*}(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)>0
with the following significance. If a solution
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
to (
2.1
) on
X=\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
satisfies the estimate

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega_{n,S})<\epsilon_{*}

for all
|n|+|S|\gg 1
, then
\gamma
is gauge equivalent to the constant configuration
\gamma_{*}=(A_{*},\Phi_{*})
. In particular, a point-like solution on
X
is always trivial.

We are also interested in solutions on the planar end
\mathbb{H}^{+}\times\Sigma
with
\mathbb{H}^{+}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,+\infty)_{s}\subset\mathbb{C}_{z}
. The next theorem says that if a solution
\gamma
on
\mathbb{H}^{+}\times\Sigma
is close to
\gamma_{*}
everywhere, then
\gamma
converges to
\gamma_{*}
exponentially in the spatial direction:

Theorem 2.5

(
[
Wan20a
, Theorem 9.1]
)
.

If
\lambda\neq 0
, then there exists constants
\epsilon,\zeta>0
depending only the datum
(g_{\Sigma},\lambda\neq 0,\mu)
with the following significance. Suppose that a configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
solves the Seiberg-Witten equations (
2.1
) on
\mathbb{H}^{+}\times\Sigma
, and
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;\Omega_{n,S})<\epsilon
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
S\geq 0
. Then

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;\Omega_{n,S})<e^{-\zeta S}.

This theorem will be used in Section
6
to control the
L^{2}_{k}
-norm of
\gamma-\gamma_{*}
on the region
\Omega_{n,S}
,
k\geq 2
if
\gamma
lies in the Coulomb slice of
\gamma_{*}
; see Theorem
6.2
.

2.3.
Solutions on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma

Now we study the dimensional reduction of (
2.1
), the Seiberg-Witten equations defined on the 3-manifold
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
:

(2.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r}\frac{1}{2}\rho_{3}(F_{B^{t}})-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}-\rho_{3}(\omega)=0,\\
D_{B}\Psi=0.\end{array}\right.

with
\omega=\mu+ds\wedge\lambda
. The pair
\check{\gamma}=(B,\Psi)
is a configuration on the 3-manifold. To go back to the 4-dimensional case, one takes

A=dt\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+B,\ \Phi(t)=\Psi\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma,

so
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;[0,1]_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s})
comes down to the energy of
(B,\Psi)
:

\mathcal{E}_{an}(B,\Psi;\mathbb{R}_{s})=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma}\frac{1}{4}|F_{B^{t}}|^{2}+|\nabla_{B}\Psi|^{2}+|(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{3}(\omega)|^{2}.

The trivial solution
\check{\gamma}_{*}=(B_{*},\Psi_{*})
of (
2.5
) can be then written as

(2.6)

B_{*}=ds\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+\begin{pmatrix}d&0\\
0&\nabla^{LC}\end{pmatrix},\Psi_{*}=(r_{+},\sqrt{2}\lambda^{0,1}r_{-}),

which has
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\check{\gamma}_{*};\mathbb{R}_{s})=0
. In fact, this is the only solution with finite energy if Assumption
2.2
holds.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
Tau01
, Proposition 4.4 & 4.7]
)
.

If
g_{\Sigma}
is flat and Assumption
2.2
holds, then any solution
\check{\gamma}
of
(\ref{3DDSWEQ})
with
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\check{\gamma};\mathbb{R}_{s})<\infty
is gauge equivalent to the unqiue
\mathbb{R}_{s}
-translation solution
\check{\gamma}_{*}
.

Remark 2.7
.

Theorem
2.6
is due to Taubes and is the main reason to assume the flatness of
g_{\Sigma}
. A short discussion on its proof can be found in
[
Wan20a
, Section 10]
. In fact, Theorem
2.4
&
2.5
also hold for any non-flat metric
g_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
with a slightly different expression of
\mathcal{E}_{an}
; see
[
Wan20a
]
.
∎

3.
The Strict Cobordism Category

Let
\Sigma=\coprod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}
be a union of 2-tori. A boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
on
\Sigma
consists of

•

a flat metric
g_{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
,

•

an imaginary-valued harmonic 1-form
\lambda\in\Omega_{h}^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
,

•

an imaginary-valued harmonic 2-form
\mu\in\Omega_{h}^{2}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
,

such that

(P0)

on each component
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}
, Assumption
2.2
holds for the restriction
(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)\big|_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}}
. In particular,
\lambda|_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}}\neq 0
for all
i
.

To any boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}
on
\Sigma
is associated with a strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{d})
. It is called strict, because objects and morphisms are subject to certain constraints. Roughly speaking, each object of
\Cob_{s}
is a 3-manifold
Y
with
\partial Y\cong\Sigma
, together with a choice of a cylindrical metric
g_{Y}
and a closed 2-form
\omega
compatible with
\mathfrak{d}
. A morphism of
\Cob_{s}
is a 4-manifold with corners, equipped again with a suitable closed 2-form:

(X,W):(Y_{1},\partial Y_{1})\to(Y_{2},\partial Y_{2}),\partial Y_{1}\cong\partial Y_{2}\cong\Sigma.

The restriction of this cobordism between boundaries has to be a product, so
W=[-1,1]_{t}\times\Sigma
. Some of these constraints on objects and morphisms might be dropped in the future by looking at the Seiberg-Witten moduli spaces on 4-manifolds with more complicated geometry. But for now we restrict attention to this smaller category
\Cob_{s}
for the sake of simplicity. Subsection
3.1
and
3.2
below are devoted to the precise definition of
\Cob_{s}
. Once this is done, the configuration spaces on the completed manifolds
\widehat{Y}
and
\widehat{X}
will be introduced in Subsection
3.3
.

3.1.
Objects

For any fixed boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
on
\Sigma
, an object of the strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{d})
is a quintuple
\mathbb{Y}=(Y,\psi,g_{Y},\omega,\{\mathfrak{q}\})
satisfying the following properties:

(P1)

Y
is a compact oriented 3-manifold with boundary, and
\psi:\partial Y\to\Sigma
is an orientation preserving diffeomorphism. The identification map
\psi
will be dropped from our notations when it is clear from the context.

(P2)

g_{Y}
is a metric on
Y
cylindrical near
\Sigma
, i.e.,
g_{Y}
is the product metric

ds^{2}+\psi^{*}g_{\Sigma}

within a collar neighborhood
(-2,0]_{s}\times\partial Y
of
\partial Y
. We form a complete Riemannian
3
-manifold
\widehat{Y}
by attaching cylindrical ends along
\Sigma
:

\widehat{Y}=Y\cup_{\psi}[-1,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma,

whose metric is denoted also by
g_{Y}
.

(P3)

\omega\in\Omega^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
is an imaginary valued
closed
2-form on
Y
such that within the collar neighborhood
[-1,0]_{s}\times\partial Y
,
\omega
restricts to an
s
-independent form

\mu+ds\wedge\lambda,

so
\omega
extends naturally to a closed 2-form on
\widehat{Y}
, denoted also by
\omega
.

(P4)

The 1-form
*_{\Sigma}\lambda
lies in the image

\im(H^{1}(Y;i\mathbb{R})\to H^{1}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})).

(P5)

The 2-form
\mu
lies in the image

\im(H^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})\to H^{2}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})).

(P6)

Choose a cut-off function
\chi_{1}:[0,\infty)_{s}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

\chi_{1}(s)\equiv 1\text{ if }s\geq-1;\ \chi_{1}(s)\equiv 0\text{ if }s\leq-3/2.

Set
\omega_{\lambda}=\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda
and

(3.1)

\overline{\omega}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\omega-\omega_{\lambda}=\omega-\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda.

Then
\overline{\omega}\equiv\mu
on
[-1,0]_{s}\times\partial Y
. Any two such forms
\overline{\omega},\overline{\omega}^{\prime}
are said to be
relatively cohomologous
, if
\omega=\omega^{\prime}+d_{Y}b
for a compactly supported 1-form
b\in\Omega^{1}_{c}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
. For each
[\mu]\in H^{2}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})
, the space of relative cohomology classes is denoted by

H^{2}_{dR}(Y,\partial Y;[\mu])

which is a torsor over
H^{2}_{dR}(Y,\partial Y;[0])\cong H^{2}(Y,\partial Y;i\mathbb{R})
. The class of
\overline{\omega}
is denoted by
[\omega]_{cpt}
. There is a natural map
j^{*}
:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}H^{2}_{dR}(Y,\partial Y;[\mu])&\xrightarrow{j^{*}}&H^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})&\xrightarrow{i^{*}}&H^{2}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R})\\
{[\omega]}_{cpt}&\mapsto&[\omega]&\mapsto&[\mu]\end{array}

sending
[\omega]_{cpt}
to the cohomology class
[\omega]
of
\omega
. Moreover,
i^{*}([\omega])=[\mu]
for the inclusion map
i:\Sigma\to Y
. We refer to
[\omega]\in H^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
as
the period class
, which is independent of
\lambda
and the cut-off function
\chi_{1}
. The closed 2-form
\omega
in
(P3)
can be recovered from (
\lambda,\mu,[\omega]_{cpt}
) up to a relatively exact 2-form.

(P7)

\{\mathfrak{q}\}
is a collection of admissible perturbations (in the sense of Definition
13.3
) of the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
for each relative
spin^{c}\
structures
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

The closed 2-form
\omega
is used to perturb the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional on
\widehat{Y}
, see Definition
3.8
below.
(P0)
will allow us to apply Theorem
2.4
-
2.6
in Section
6
, so the Seiberg-Witten moduli spaces will have the right compactness property. We will address the issue of perturbations in Part
III
, so at present readers may ignore the last property
(P7)
.
∎

The property
(P4)
requires some further explanation: it is used to find a closed 1-form on
\widehat{Y}
that equals
*_{3}(\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda)
on the cylindrical end. It will play an essential role in the energy equation in Section
5
, cf. Theorem
5.4
below.

Lemma 3.2
.

For any object
\mathbb{Y}\in\Cob_{s}
, there exists a smooth 2-form
\omega_{h}
on
\widehat{Y}
such that
*_{3}\omega_{h}
is closed and
\omega_{h}=ds\wedge\lambda
on
[-1,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
. In particular,
\omega_{h}-\omega_{\lambda}\in L^{2}(\widehat{Y})
.

Remark 3.3
.

In Part
VI
, we will define for each object
\mathbb{Y}
and each relative
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}
on
(Y,\partial Y)
(see Subsection
3.3
below) a finitely generated module

\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})

over a Novikov ring
\mathcal{R}
. The isomorphism class of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is independent of

•

the cylindrical metric
g_{Y}
;

•

isotopy of the diffeomorphism
\psi:\partial Y\to\Sigma
;

•

the choice of admissible perturbations in
(P7)
.

Moreover, this module is not altered if we replace
\omega
by
\omega+d_{Y}b
for a compactly supported 1-form
b\in\Omega^{1}_{c}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
. We refer to
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
as the monopole Floer homology of
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, which depends at most on

•

the 3-manifold
(Y,\partial Y)
,

•

the isotopy class of
\psi:\partial Y\to\Sigma
,

•

the boundary datum
\mathfrak{d}=(g_{\Sigma},\lambda,\mu)
and

•

the relative cohomology class
[\omega]_{cpt}\in H^{2}_{dR}(Y,\partial Y;[\mu])
as defined in
(P6)
.

However, the definition of
[\omega]_{cpt}
relies on the cut-off function
\chi_{1}
. This ambiguity is removed by the following fact: the group
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is not affected if one replaces
\omega
by

\omega+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi_{i}^{\circ}(s)ds\wedge\lambda_{i}

where
\lambda_{i}=\lambda|_{\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i};i\mathbb{R})
and
\chi_{i}^{\circ}(s)
is any compactly supported function on
[0,\infty)_{s}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}
. Thus only a suitable quotient class of
[\omega]_{cpt}\in H^{2}(Y,\partial Y;[\mu])
matters, and this class is independent of
\chi_{1}
. See Corollary
19.11
for more details.
∎

3.2.
Morphisms

Having described objects in the strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}(\Sigma,\mathfrak{d})
, we now turn to the morphism spaces in this subsection. Since each object
\mathbb{Y}
is coupled with a closed 2-form
\omega
, so is any morphism in this category. Given two objects
\mathbb{Y}_{i}=(Y_{i},\psi_{i},g_{i},\omega_{i},\mathfrak{q}_{i}),i=1,2
in
\Cob_{s}
, a morphism

\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}

is a quadruple
\mathbb{X}=(X,\psi_{X},W,[\omega_{X}]_{cpt})
satisfying the following properties.

(Q1)

X
is a manifold with corners, i.e.
X
is a space stratified by manifolds

X\supset X_{-1}\supset X_{-2}\supset X_{-3}=\emptyset.

The co-dimensional 1 stratum
X_{-1}
consists of three parts

X_{-1}=(-Y_{1})\cup(Y_{2})\cup W_{X}.

W_{X}
is an oriented 3-manifold with boundary
\partial W_{X}=\partial Y_{1}\cap\partial Y_{2}
,
\partial Y_{i}=Y_{i}\cap W_{X}
and
X_{-2}=\partial Y_{1}\cup\partial Y_{2}
.

(Q2)

W=[-1,1]_{t}\times\Sigma
is the product cobordism from
\Sigma
to itself.

(Q3)

\psi_{X}:W_{X}\to W
is an orientation preserving diffeomorphism compatible with
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
. To be more precise, we require that

\displaystyle\psi_{X}|_{\partial Y_{1}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{1}:\partial Y_{1}\to\{-1\}\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle\psi_{X}|_{\partial Y_{2}}

\displaystyle=\psi_{2}:\partial Y_{2}\to\{1\}\times\Sigma,

and these relations hold also in a collar neighborhood of
\partial W_{X}
. When it is clear from the context, we shall not distinguish
W_{X}
from
W
, and
\psi_{X}
will be dropped from our notations. Such a pair
(X,\psi_{X})
is called
a strict cobordism
from
(Y_{1},\psi_{1})
to
(Y_{2},\psi_{2})
.

(Q4)

The closed 2-form
\omega_{i}
on
Y_{i}
contains a bit more information than the period class
[\omega_{i}]\in H^{2}(Y_{i};i\mathbb{R})
. Note first that

\omega_{1}|_{\Sigma}=\omega_{2}|_{\Sigma}=\mu\in\Omega^{2}_{h}(\Sigma;i\mathbb{R}),

so the triple
(\omega_{1},\mu,\omega_{2})
determines a class
[\alpha]
in
H^{2}((-Y_{1})\cup W\cup Y_{2};i\mathbb{R})
.
[\alpha]
is required to lie in the image

\im\bigg(m^{*}_{0}:H^{2}(X;i\mathbb{R})\to H^{2}((-Y_{1})\cup W\cup Y_{2});i\mathbb{R})\bigg),

where
m_{0}:(-Y_{1})\cup W\cup Y_{2}\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion map, and let
[\omega_{X}]
be a lift of
[\alpha]
. As a result,
[\omega_{X}]
generates all cohomology classes in the right diagram below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(Y_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\omega_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mu_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(W)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\omega_{X}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{b}^{*}[\omega_{X}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(Y_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\omega_{2}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mu_{2}]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{b}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{b}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}^{*}}

(Q5)

There exists a closed 2-form
\overline{\omega}_{X}\in\Omega^{2}(X;i\mathbb{R})
on
X
with the following properties:

•

\overline{\omega}_{X}
realizes the class
[\omega_{X}]\in H^{2}(X;i\mathbb{R})
;

•

\overline{\omega}_{X}=\overline{\omega}_{i}
(see
(P6)
) within a collar neighborhood of
Y_{i}\subset X_{-1}
for
i=1,2
;

•

\overline{\omega}_{X}=\mu
, within a collar neighborhood of
W\subset X_{-1}
.

The existence of such a form
\overline{\omega}_{X}
is guaranteed by the cohomological condition in
(Q4)
. Finally, set
\omega_{\lambda}=\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda
and

\omega_{X}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\overline{\omega}_{X}+\omega_{\lambda}=\overline{\omega}_{X}+\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda\text{ on }X.

(Q6)

For any two closed forms
\omega_{X}
and
\omega_{X}^{\prime}
satisfying the condition in
•
‣
(Q5)
, they are said to be equivalent if
\omega_{X}^{\prime}-\omega_{X}=da
for a compactly supported smooth 1-form
a\in\Omega^{1}(X;i\mathbb{R})
. Denote by
[\omega_{X}]_{cpt}
the equivalence classes of
\omega_{X}
.

Example 3.4
.

The product cobordism
\mathbb{X}=[-1,1]_{t}\times\mathbb{Y}:\mathbb{Y}\to\mathbb{Y}
. In this case,
X=[-1,1]_{t}\times Y
and
\psi_{X}=\Id_{[-1,1]_{t}}\times\psi
is the product map. We obtain
\omega_{X}
as the pull-back of the 2-form
\omega
from
Y
.
∎

Example 3.5
.

Take
\mathbb{Y}_{1},\mathbb{Y}_{2}\in\Cob_{s}
with
Y_{1}=Y_{2}=Y
and
\psi_{1}
isotopic to
\psi_{2}
. Suppose in addition that
\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}=d_{Y}b
for a compactly supported 1-from
b\in\Omega^{1}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
, then one may construct a cobordism
\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
as follows. Let
X=[-1,1]_{t}\times Y
and
\psi_{X}
be an isotopy from
\psi_{1}
to
\psi_{2}
. Set
\omega_{X}=d_{X}(\chi(t)b)+\omega_{1}
, where
\chi(t)
is any cut-off function such that

\chi(t)\equiv 0\text{ if }t\leq-1/2;\ \chi(t)\equiv 1\text{ if }t\geq 1/2.\qed

Similar to the definition of
\widehat{Y}
, for each strict cobordism
X:Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
, we obtain a cobordism between
\widehat{Y}_{1}
and
\widehat{Y}_{2}
by attaching cylindrical ends to
X
:

\widehat{X}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}X\cup_{\psi_{X}}[-1,1]_{t}\times[-1,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma:\widehat{Y}_{1}\to\widehat{Y}_{2}.

A planar metric
g_{X}
on
X
is a metric compatible with the corner structure. We insist that the metric
g_{W}
of
W=[-1,1]_{t}\times\Sigma
is the product one

v^{2}(t)dt^{2}+g_{\Sigma}

for some function
v:[-1,1]_{t}\to[0,\infty)
such that
v(t)\equiv 1
for
1-|t|\ll 1
. This flexibility allows us to compose strict cobordisms equipped with planar metrics, but we shall always take
v(t)\equiv 1
in the sequel for the sake of simplicity. We require the planar metric
g_{X}
be the product metric

d^{2}t+d^{2}s+g_{\Sigma}

in a neighborhood
(-\epsilon,0]_{t}\times(-1,0]_{s}\times X_{-2}
of the co-dimension
2
stratum
X_{-2}=(-\Sigma)\cup\Sigma
. Also,
g_{X}
needs to be cylindrical near the co-dimensional 1 stratum
X_{-1}
:

\displaystyle g_{X}|_{[-1,-1+\epsilon)\times Y_{1}}

\displaystyle=d^{2}t+g_{1},

\displaystyle g_{X}|_{(1-\epsilon,1]\times Y_{2}}

\displaystyle=d^{2}t+g_{2},

\displaystyle g_{X}|_{[-1,1]_{t}\times(-1,0]_{s}\times\Sigma}

\displaystyle=d^{2}s+g_{W}=d^{2}t+d^{2}s+g_{\Sigma}.

Such a metric extends to a cylindrical metric on
\widehat{X}
compatible with that of
(-\widehat{Y}_{1})\cup\widehat{Y}_{2}
. When it is clear from the context, we also use
g_{X}
to denote this extended metric on
\widehat{X}
.

Although a planar metric
g_{X}
of
X
is
not
encoded in a morphism
\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
, it is needed to define the cobordism map induced by
\mathbb{X}
(and so the
functor
\HM_{*}
in Theorem
1.5
). This cobordism map will be independent of the choice of
g_{X}
.

Similar to Lemma
3.2
, one can find a co-closed 2-form
\omega_{X,h}
on
\widehat{X}
extending
\omega_{\lambda}=\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda
. In this case, we also insist a Dirichlet boundary condition for
*_{4}\omega_{X,h}
. This property is crucial for the energy equations in Section
5
, cf. Theorem
5.1
.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any morphism
\mathbb{X}\in\Cob_{s}
, there exists a co-closed 2-form
\omega_{X,h}
on
\widehat{X}
such that
\omega_{X,h}=ds\wedge\lambda
on
[-1,1]_{t}\times[-1,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
and

(3.2)

*_{4}\omega_{X,h}\big|_{(-\widehat{Y}_{1})\cup\widehat{Y}_{2}}=0.

In particular,
\omega_{X,h}-\omega_{\lambda}\in L^{2}(\widehat{X})
.

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

It suffices to verify that the class

[dt\wedge*_{\Sigma}\lambda]\in H^{2}(W,\partial W;i\mathbb{R})

lies in the image
\im\big(H^{2}(X,Y_{1}\cup Y_{2};i\mathbb{R})\to H^{2}(W,\partial W;i\mathbb{R})\big)
with
W=[-1,1]_{t}\times\Sigma
. By the property
(P4)
, take
z
to be a lift of
[*_{\Sigma}\lambda]
in
H^{1}(Y_{1};i\mathbb{R})
. In the diagram below, all cohomology groups take value in
i\mathbb{R}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(Y_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(Y_{1})\oplus H^{1}(Y_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(X,Y_{1}\cup Y_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\{-1\}\times\Sigma)\oplus H^{1}(\{1\}\times\Sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(W,\partial W)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z\mapsto(z,0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[*_{\Sigma}\lambda]\mapsto([*_{\Sigma}\lambda],0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

Alternatively, one may construct the form
\omega_{X,h}
by hands using the co-closed form
\omega_{1,h}\in\Omega^{1}(\widehat{Y}_{1};i\mathbb{R})
in Lemma
3.2
. Take a smooth function
h:[-1,1]_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
h
is supported on
[-1,-1+\epsilon]
,
h(t)\equiv 1
for
t\in[-1,-1+\epsilon/2]
, and

\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}h(t)dt=1.

Then there is another function
f:[-1,1]_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
with
f(1)=f(-1)=0
and
f^{\prime}(t)=h(t)-1
. Finally, set
*_{4}\omega_{X,h}=h(t)dt\wedge*_{3}\omega_{1,h}-d_{X}(f(t)\chi_{1}(s)*_{\Sigma}\lambda)
.
∎

3.3.
Relative
spin^{c}\
Structures and Configuration Spaces

Let
\mathfrak{s}_{std}=(S_{std},\rho_{std,3})
be the standard
spin^{c}\
structure on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
as described in Section
2
with

S_{std}=\mathbb{C}\oplus\Lambda^{0,1}\Sigma.

For any object
\mathbb{Y}=(Y,\psi,g_{Y},\omega,\mathfrak{q})\in\Cob_{s}
, a relative
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}=(\mathfrak{s},\varphi)
is a pair consisting of a
spin^{c}\
structure
\mathfrak{s}=(S,\rho_{3})
on
Y
and an isomorphism of
spin^{c}\
structures

\varphi:(S,\rho_{3})|_{\partial Y}\to\psi^{*}\mathfrak{s}_{std}|_{\partial Y}

near the boundary compatible with
\psi
. The set of isomorphism classes of relative
spin^{c}\
structures on
Y
, denoted by

\Spincr(Y),

is a torsor over
H^{2}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})
. There is a natural forgetful map from
\Spincr(Y)
to the set of isomorphism classes of
spin^{c}\
structures:

\Spincr(Y)\to\Spinc(Y),\ \widehat{\mathfrak{s}}=(\mathfrak{s},\varphi)\mapsto\mathfrak{s},

whose fiber is acted on freely and transitively by
H^{1}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})/\im(H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{Z}))
reflecting the change of boundary trivializations. Any
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in\Spincr(Y)
extends to a relative
spin^{c}\
structure on
\widehat{Y}
(interpreted suitably), denoted also by
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}
.

Let
(B_{*},\Psi_{*})
be the translation invariant configuration on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
such that the restriction

(3.3)

(B_{*},\Psi_{*})|_{\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}}

on each component
\mathbb{T}^{2}_{i}\subset\Sigma
is defined by the formula (
2.6
) for all
1\leq i\leq n
. Take
(B_{0},\Psi_{0})
to be a smooth configuration on
\widehat{Y}
which agrees with
(B_{*},\Phi_{*})
on the cylindrical end
[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
. Recall from
(P3)
that the closed 2-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
defined on
Y
extends to a closed 2-form on the completion
\widehat{Y}
by setting

\omega|_{[-1,\infty)\times\Sigma}=\mu+ds\wedge\lambda,

and
[\omega]\in H^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
is the period class of
\omega
. Now consider the configuration space for any
k>\frac{1}{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})=\{(B,\Psi):(b,\psi)=(B,\Psi)-(B_{0},\Psi_{0})\in L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Y};iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S)\}.

Remark 3.7
.

Since
\widehat{Y}
is non-compact, the condition that
(b,\psi)\in L^{2}_{k}
says that the section
(b,\psi)
decays mildly along the cylindrical end of
\widehat{Y}
. It turns out that this decay is always exponential for solutions to the Seiberg-Witten equations, cf. Theorem
6.2
below.
∎

Definition 3.8
.

The perturbed Chern-Simons-Dirac functional on
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is defined as

(3.4)

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(B,\Psi)=-\frac{1}{8}\int_{\widehat{Y}}(B^{t}-B_{0}^{t})\wedge(F_{B^{t}}+F_{B_{0}^{t}})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\widehat{Y}}\langle D_{B}\Psi,\Psi\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\widehat{Y}}(B^{t}-B_{0}^{t})\wedge\omega.\qed

Remark 3.9
.

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is the analogue of the gauged action functional
\mathcal{A}_{H}
in the context of gauged Witten equations, see
[
Wan20a
, Definition 4.1]
.
∎

The configuration space
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is acted on freely by the gauge group

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})=\{u:\widehat{Y}\to S^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}:u-1\in L^{2}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y},\mathbb{C})\},

via the formula:

u(B,\Psi)=(B-u^{-1}du,u\Psi).

The Lie algebra of
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}
is
\Lie(\mathcal{G}_{k+1})=L^{2}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y};i\mathbb{R}).
The exponential map
f\mapsto e^{f}
is surjective onto the identity component
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}^{e}
of
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}
; they fit to a short exact sequence:

0\to\mathcal{G}_{k+1}^{e}\to\mathcal{G}_{k+1}\to\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{k+1})\cong H^{1}(Y,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\to 0.

The Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is not fully gauge-invariant in general:

Lemma 3.10

(Compare
[
KM07
, Section 29]
)
.

For any
\gamma=(B,\Psi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
, we have

\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(u\cdot\gamma)-\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\gamma)=(2\pi^{2}[u]\cup c_{1}(S)-2\pi i[u]\cup[\omega])[Y,\partial Y],

where
[u]=[\frac{u^{-1}du}{2\pi i}]\in H^{1}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})
is the relative cohomology class determined by
u
and
[\omega]
is the period class of
\omega
.

The tangent space at each
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is naturally identified with
L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Y},iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S)
. We compute the gradient of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
with respect to the
L^{2}
inner product:

(3.5)

\grad\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(B,\Psi)=(\frac{1}{2}*_{3}F_{B^{t}}+\rho_{3}^{-1}(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}-*_{3}\omega,D_{B}\Psi).

A configuration
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is a critical point of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
if and only if it solves the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations on
\widehat{Y}
.

Definition 3.11
.

For any object
\mathbb{Y}=(Y,\psi,g_{Y},\omega,\mathfrak{q})\in\Cob_{s}
, the Seiberg-Witten map defined on
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is given by (ignoring the perturbation
\mathfrak{q}
for a moment)

\mathfrak{F}_{\omega}(B,\Psi)=(\frac{1}{2}\rho_{3}(F_{B^{t}}-2\omega)-(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0},D_{B}\Psi).

and the equation

(3.6)

\mathfrak{F}_{\omega}(B,\Psi)=0

is called the 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equations.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

By Theorem
2.6
, the reference configuration
(B_{*},\Psi_{*})
defined in (
3.3
) is the unique
\mathbb{R}_{s}
-translation invariant solution of (
3.6
) on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
up to gauge.
∎

The downward gradient flowline equation of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}

\frac{d}{dt}(B(t),\Psi(t))=-\grad\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(B(t),\Psi(t))

can be cast into the 4-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equations:

(3.7)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r}\frac{1}{2}\rho_{4}(F_{A^{t}}^{+}-2\omega^{+}_{X})-(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}=0,\\
D_{A}^{+}\Phi=0,\end{array}\right.

on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
with
A=\frac{d}{dt}+B(t),\Phi=\Psi(t)
and
\omega_{X}=\pi^{*}\omega
where
\pi:\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}\to\widehat{Y}
is the projection map. This corresponds to the product cobordism
[-1,1]_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
in Example
3.4
.

In general, let
(A_{*},\Phi_{*})
be the
\mathbb{C}
-translation-invariant solution on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
with

(3.8)

A_{*}=dt\otimes\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+B_{*},\Phi_{*}(t)=\Psi_{*},z=t+is.

Let
\mathbb{X}=(X,\psi_{X},W,[\omega_{X}]_{cpt}):\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
be a morphism in
\Cob_{s}
and suppose
\widehat{X}:\widehat{Y}_{1}\to\widehat{Y}_{2}
extends to a
relative

spin^{c}\
cobordism:

(3.9)

(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\widehat{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}).

Remark 3.13
.

For a
relative

spin^{c}\
cobordism, we insist that identification maps

(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})|_{\widehat{Y}_{i}}\cong(\widehat{Y}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}),i=1,2

are implicitly baked in the definition.
∎

Let
(A_{0},\Phi_{0})
be a reference configuration on
\widehat{X}
whose restriction on
[-1,1]_{t}\times[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
agrees with
(A_{*},\Phi_{*})
. For each
k\geq 1
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})=\{(A,\Phi):(a,\phi)=(A,\Phi)-(A_{0},\Phi_{0})\in L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{X},iT^{*}\widehat{X}\oplus S^{+})\}.

In this case, we take
\omega_{X}\in\Omega^{2}(\widehat{X};i\mathbb{R})
to be the closed 2-form constructed in
(Q5)
and extended constantly over the cylindrical end
[-1,1]_{t}\times[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
; so for some
\epsilon>0
,

•

\omega_{X}=\omega_{1}
on
\widehat{Y}_{1}\times[-1,-1+\epsilon)_{t}
;

•

\omega_{X}=\omega_{2}
on
\widehat{Y}_{2}\times(1-\epsilon,1]_{t}
;

•

\omega_{X}=\mu+ds\wedge\lambda
on
[-1,1]_{t}\times[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
.

Then the left hand side of (
3.7
) defines a smooth map:

(3.10)

\mathfrak{F}_{X}:\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{X},i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})\oplus S^{-})

called the Seiberg-Witten map on
\widehat{X}
. For
0\leq j\leq k
, let
\mathcal{V}_{j}
be the trivial vector bundle with fiber
L^{2}_{j}(i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})\oplus S^{-})
over
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
:
