\mathcal{V}_{j}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}_{j}(i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})\oplus S^{-})\times\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}).

The Seiberg-Witten map
\mathfrak{F}_{X}
defines a smooth section of
\mathcal{V}_{k-1}\to\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
.

3.4.
Strict
spin^{c}\
cobordisms

Now let us introduce the strict
spin^{c}\
cobordism category
\SCob_{s}
, which plays the central role in Theorem
1.5
:

•

each object of
\SCob_{s}
is a pair
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
where
\mathbb{Y}
is an object of
\Cob_{s}
, and
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in\Spincr(Y)
is a relative
spin^{c}\
structure on
Y
;

•

for any objects
(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})
and
(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
,

\Hom_{\SCob_{s}}((\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1}),(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}))=\Hom_{\Cob_{s}}(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\mathbb{Y}_{2}).

3.5.
Homotopy Classes of Paths

To define the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
for each object
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\in\SCob_{s}
, we will look at the moduli spaces of the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
) on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and define a Floer chain complex:

\Ch_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});

The underlying idea is an infinite dimensional Morse theory in the quotient configuration space:

\mathcal{B}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{C}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})/\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(Y).

For any
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, the relative homotopy classes of paths
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
is a torsor over

\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{b}])\cong\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{k+1})\cong H^{1}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z}).

Moreover, for any
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
, the relative loop space
\Omega_{[\gamma]}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
in the class
[\gamma]
is simply connected, since

\pi_{2}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{b}])\cong\pi_{1}(\mathcal{G}_{k+1})=\{0\}.

There are three additional ways to think of a path
\check{\gamma}:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{B}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
with
\check{\gamma}(-1)=\mathfrak{a}
and
\check{\gamma}(1)=\mathfrak{b}
, and we shall use them interchangeably:

(1)

a path
\check{\gamma}_{1}:[-1,1]\to\mathcal{C}_{k}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
that connects
\mathfrak{a}
and
u\cdot\mathfrak{b}
for some
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
;

(2)

a configuration
\gamma
on the 4-manifold
I\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
with
I=[-1,1]_{t}
such that
\gamma|_{\{-1\}\times\widehat{Y}}=\mathfrak{a}
and

\gamma|_{\{1\}\times\widehat{Y}}=u\cdot\mathfrak{b}
for some
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
;

(3)

a configuration
\gamma^{\prime}
for a relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism

(\widehat{X}=I\times\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\to(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})

such that
\gamma|_{\{-1\}\times\widehat{Y}}=\mathfrak{a}
and
\gamma|_{\{1\}\times\widehat{Y}}=\mathfrak{b}
. Indeed, all such relative
spin^{c}\
structures on
I\times\widehat{Y}
form a torsor over

\displaystyle H^{2}(I\times Y,\partial(I\times Y);\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\cong H^{1}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})\otimes H^{1}(I,\partial I;\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{1}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z}).

The last standpoint makes it easier to think about a general morphism
\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
. To make
\HM_{*}
into a functor from
\SCob_{s}
to
\mathcal{R}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}
as in Theorem
1.5
, we attach cylindrical ends to
\widehat{X}
and obtain a complete Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{X}
:

\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigg((-\infty,-1]_{t}\times\widehat{Y}_{1}\bigg)\cup\widehat{X}\cup\bigg([1,\infty)_{t}\times\widehat{Y}_{2}\bigg).

The closed 2-form
\omega_{X}
extend over
\mathcal{X}
by setting

(3.11)

\omega_{X}=\omega_{1}\text{ on }(-\infty,-1]_{t}\times Y_{1};\ \omega_{X}=\omega_{2}\text{ on }[1,\infty)_{t}\times Y_{2}.

The goal of this paper is to analyze the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
) on
\mathcal{X}
and construct a chain map:

(3.12)

\Ch_{*}(\mathbb{X}):\Ch_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to\Ch_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})

that is independent of the choice of

•

the planar metric
g_{X}
compatible with
(g_{Y_{1}},g_{Y_{2}},g_{\Sigma})
;

•

the closed 2-form
\omega_{X}\in\omega^{2}(X;i\mathbb{R})
in the class
[\omega_{X}]_{cpt}
;

•

any auxiliary perturbation of (
3.7
) defined in Subsection
14.1
;

up to chain homotopy. To do so, we have to take into account of all isomorphism classes of relative
spin^{c}\
cobordisms:

\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\text{all possible }\eqref{E9.8}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})\}\text{ modulo isomorphisms}

which is a torsor over
H^{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
. Indeed, any two relative
spin^{c}\
cobordisms
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X,1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X,2}
that cover the 4-manifold
X
with corners are related by a complex line bundle
L_{12}\to X
:

\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X,2}=\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X,1}\otimes L_{12},

and a trivialization
L_{12}\cong\mathbb{C}
is specified along
\partial X
. Some of elements of
\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
may arise from different underlying
spin^{c}\
structures, but they all contribute to the chain map (
3.12
) and will not be separated from each other. For any
\mathfrak{a}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}),\ i=1,2
, an element of
\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
can be viewed a homotopy class of
\mathbb{X}
-paths that connect
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{2}
.

4.
The Quotient Configuration Space and Slices

Configurations in
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
are required to converge to a fixed limit in the spatial direction, so by definition, they are never reducible, i.e.
\Psi
or
\Phi\not\equiv 0
. This prevents us from finding a global slice of the gauge action as in
[
KM07
, Section 9.6]
over the non-compact manifold
\widehat{Y}
or
\widehat{X}
. Nevertheless, local slices always exists. In this section, we prove that:

Proposition 4.1
.

For either
(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{M})=(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
or
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
, the quotient space

\mathcal{B}_{k}(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{M})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{C}_{k}(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{M})/\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(M)

is a Hilbert manifold when
2(k+1)>\dim M
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

It is clear from the formula

(uv-1)=(u-1)(v-1)+(u-1)+(v-1),\ \forall u,v\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(M)

that
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(M)
is a Hilbert Lie group when
2(k+1)>\dim M
. Following the book
[
KM07
, Section 9]
, we base the argument on a general principle:

Lemma 4.2

(
[
Pal68
]
,
[
KM07
]
Lemma 9.3.2)
.

Suppose a Hilbert Lie group
G
acts smoothly and freely on a Hilbert manifold
C
, and the quotient space
C/G
is Hausdorff. Suppose that at each
c\in C
, the differential

d_{c}:T_{e}G\to T_{c}G

has closed range, then
C/G
is also a Hilbert manifold.

It remains to verify the condition of Lemma
4.2
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For either
(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{M})=(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
or
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
, the quotient configuration space
\mathcal{B}_{k}(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{M})
is Hausdorff.

Proof.

Suppose we have a sequence of configurations
\gamma_{n}=(A_{n},\Phi_{n})\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(M,s)
and a sequence of gauge transformations
u_{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(M)
such that

\gamma_{n}\to\gamma\text{ and }u_{n}\cdot\gamma_{n}\to\gamma^{\prime}

for some
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
and
\gamma^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime})
. We wish to show that
u\cdot\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}
for some
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(M)
. We prove that
v_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}1-u_{n}
has uniformly bounded
L^{2}_{k+1}
norm, so there is a weakly converging subsequence among
\{v_{n}\}
. Let
v
be the weak limit and define
u\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}1-v
.

We begin with the
L^{2}
-norm of
v_{n}
. Since
\|v_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq 2
,
|v_{n}|_{2}^{2}
contributes to a bounded integral over any compact region of
M
. It suffices to estimate
|v_{n}|_{2}^{2}
over the cylindrical end of
M
. Note that

\displaystyle\|v_{n}\Phi\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|(1-u_{n})\Phi\|_{2}\leq\|\Phi-\Phi^{\prime}\|_{2}+\|\Phi^{\prime}-u_{n}\Phi_{n}\|_{2}+\|u_{n}(\Phi_{n}-\Phi)\|_{2},

which is uniformly bounded. As
s\to\infty
,
\Phi
approximates the standard spinor and is non-vanishing everywhere. It follows that
\|v_{n}\|_{2}\leq C
for some uniform
C>0
.

To deal with derivatives of
v_{n}
, let
w_{n}=u_{n}^{-1}du_{n}
. Then
\|w_{n}\|_{L^{2}_{k}}\leq\|u_{n}\cdot\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{n}\|_{L^{2}_{k}}\leq\|\gamma-\gamma^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{k}}+1
when
n\gg 1
. The estimate for
\|\nabla^{l}v_{n}\|_{L^{2}}\ (1\leq l\leq k+1)
now follows from the relation

\nabla v_{n}=\nabla u_{n}=w_{n}-v_{n}\cdot w_{n}

and an induction argument. If we already know
2k>\dim M
, then
L^{2}_{k}
is a Banach algebra itself; otherwise, the first a few steps in the induction requires special treatments. For instance, if
\dim M=3
and
k=1
, then we have to bound

\|\nabla v_{n}\|_{p}\text{ for }2\leq p\leq 6\text{ and }\|\nabla^{2}v_{n}\|_{2}.

If
\dim M=4
and
k=2
, then we have to bound

\|\nabla v_{n}\|_{p}\text{ for }2\leq p<\infty,\ \|\nabla^{2}v_{n}\|_{p}\text{ for }2\leq p\leq 4\text{ and }\|\nabla^{3}v_{n}\|_{2}.

For the Sobolev embedding theorem on cylinders, see
[
KM07
, Section 13.2]
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{T}_{k}
be the tangent space of
\mathcal{C}_{k}(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{M})
. For each configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{M})
, let
\textbf{d}_{\gamma}
be the map obtained by linearizing the action of
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(M)
, extended to lower Sobolev regularities
(0\leq j\leq k)
:

\displaystyle\textbf{d}_{\gamma}:L^{2}_{j+1}(M;i\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}_{j}(M,iT^{*}M\oplus S^{+})=\mathcal{T}_{j,\gamma}

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto(-df,f\Phi).

Let
\mathcal{J}_{j,\gamma}\subset\mathcal{T}_{j,\gamma}
be the image of
\textbf{d}_{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j,\gamma}
be the
L^{2}
-orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{J}_{j,\gamma}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{j,\gamma}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{v\in\mathcal{T}_{j,\gamma}:\langle v,\textbf{d}_{\gamma}(f)\rangle_{L^{2}(M)}=0,\forall f\in L^{2}_{j+1}(M;i\mathbb{R})\}

\displaystyle=\{v=(\delta a,\delta\phi)\in L^{2}_{j}(M,iT^{*}M\oplus S^{+}):\textbf{d}_{\gamma}^{*}(v)=0,\langle a,\vec{n}\rangle=0\text{ at }\partial M\}

where
\vec{n}
is the outward normal vector at
\partial M
and

\displaystyle\textbf{d}_{\gamma}^{*}:L^{2}_{j}(M,iT^{*}M\oplus S^{+})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}_{j-1}(M;i\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle(\delta a,\delta\phi)

\displaystyle\mapsto-d^{*}\delta a+i\re\langle i\Phi,\delta\phi\rangle.

is the formal adjoint of
\textbf{d}_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 4.4

(cf.
[
KM07
, Proposition 9.3.4]
)
.

As
\gamma
varies over
\mathcal{C}_{k}(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{M})
,
\mathcal{J}_{j,\gamma}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j,\gamma}
form complementary closed sub-bundles of
\mathcal{T}_{j}
, and we have a smooth decomposition

\mathcal{T}_{j}|_{\mathcal{C}_{k}(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})}=\mathcal{J}_{j}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{j},0\leq j\leq k.

In particular,
T\mathcal{C}_{k}(M,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})=\mathcal{T}_{k}=\mathcal{J}_{k}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{k}
.

Proposition
4.1
now follows from Lemma
4.3
and
4.4
.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

For any
v=(\delta a,\delta\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{j,\gamma}
, we need to find the unique element
f\in L^{2}_{j+1}(M;i\mathbb{R})
such that
v-\textbf{d}_{\gamma}(f)\in\mathcal{K}_{j,\gamma}
. Such an element solves the Neumann boundary value problem:

(4.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\Delta_{M}f+|\Phi|^{2}f&=-\textbf{d}_{\gamma}^{*}(v)\\
\langle df,\vec{n}\rangle&=\langle\delta a,\vec{n}\rangle\text{ at }\partial M.\end{array}\right.

The left hand side of
(
4.1
)
forms a Fredholm operator (
1\leq j\leq k
):

(4.2)

(\Delta_{M}+|\Phi|^{2},\frac{\partial}{\partial\vec{n}}\bigg|_{\partial M}):L^{2}_{j+1}(M;i\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}_{j-1}(M;i\mathbb{R})\times L^{2}_{j+1/2}(\partial M;i\mathbb{R})

which is in fact invertible. If
M
is compact, this follows from
[
Tay11
, Proposition 7.5]
. In general, one may start with the special case when

(M,\Phi)=(\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma,\Psi_{*})\text{ or }([-1,1]\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma,\Phi_{*})

using Fourier transformation on the real line
\mathbb{R}_{s}
and the positivity of
|\Psi_{*}|^{2}
. To show (
4.2
) is Fredholm, apply the parametrix patching argument. To compute the index of
4.2
, note that the restriction map

\frac{\partial}{\partial\vec{n}}\bigg|_{\partial M}:L^{2}_{j+1}(M.i\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}_{j+1/2}(\partial M;i\mathbb{R})

is surjective, and the operator

\Delta_{M}+|\Phi|^{2}:\{f\in L^{2}_{2}(M;i\mathbb{R}):\langle df,\vec{n}\rangle=0\}\to L^{2}(M;i\mathbb{R})

is positive and self-adjoint. This proves that the operator (
4.2
) is invertible.

Alternatively, one may follow the proof of
[
Tay11
, Proposition 7.5]
. Details are left as exercises.
∎

We record the next proposition for convenience:

Proposition 4.5
.

Over the configuration space
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, the gradient (
3.5
) of the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
defines a smooth section of
\mathcal{K}_{k-1}\to\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
when
k\geq 1
.

5.
Energy Equations

This section is devoted to the energy equations of the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
) on
\widehat{X}
, which will play an important role in the proof of the Compactness Theorem
6.1
in Section
6
. In particular, it gives property
(K1)
. The main results of this section are Theorem
5.1
and Proposition
5.4
.

5.1.
The 4-Dimensional Case

Following the book
[
KM07
, Section 4]
, we prove an energy equation associated to the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
):

Theorem 5.1

(cf.
[
KM07
, P.593]
)
.

For any morphism
\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
in the strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}
, choose a planar metric
g_{X}
on
X
and consider a relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\widehat{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
. Then for any configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
, the
L^{2}
-norm of the Seiberg-Witten map
\mathfrak{F}_{X}(A,\Phi)
can be expressed as

\int_{\widehat{X}}|\mathfrak{F}_{X}(A,\Phi)|^{2}=\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi)-\mathcal{E}_{top}(A,\Phi),

where

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{\widehat{X}}\frac{1}{4}|F_{A^{t}}|^{2}+|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{4}(\omega^{+}_{X})|^{2}+\frac{s}{4}|\Phi|^{2}-\langle F_{A^{t}},\overline{\omega}_{X}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{\widehat{X}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega_{\lambda}-\omega_{X,h}\rangle-\int_{\widehat{X}}F_{A^{t}_{0}}\wedge*_{4}\omega_{X,h},

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{top}(A,\Phi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1}}(B_{1},\Psi_{1})-2\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{2}}(B_{2},\Psi_{2})+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\widehat{X}}F_{A^{t}_{0}}\wedge F_{A^{t}_{0}}-\int_{\widehat{X}}F_{A^{t}_{0}}\wedge\omega_{X},

and
(B_{i},\Psi_{i})=(A,\Phi)|_{\widehat{Y}_{i}}
are restrictions of
\gamma
at
\widehat{Y}_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Here,
\omega_{X}=\overline{\omega}_{X}+\omega_{\lambda}
is the closed 2-form constructed in
(Q5)
with
\omega_{\lambda}=\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda
. The co-closed 2-form
\omega_{X,h}
is subject to the Neumann boundary condition and the constraint that
\omega_{\lambda}-\omega_{X,h}\in L^{2}(\widehat{X})
. Its existence is guaranteed by Lemma
3.6
.

Remark 5.2
.

Let us explain why (
5.1
) is a useful expression. Errors terms in the second line of
(\ref{an})
are bounded below by

-\frac{1}{16}\|F_{A^{t}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\widehat{X})}-C(A_{0},\omega_{X},g_{X})

for some constant
C(A_{0},\omega_{X},g_{X})>0
.

The first line of (
5.1
) is consistent with the
local energy functional

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega)
in Definition
2.3
. Indeed, over the cylindrical end
I\times[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
, (
5.1
) becomes (with
I=[-1,1]_{t})
:

(5.3)

\displaystyle\int_{I\times[0,\infty)_{s}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{1}{4}|F_{A^{t}}|^{2}+|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})|^{2}-\langle F_{A^{t}}^{\Sigma},\mu\rangle

where
\omega=\mu+ds\wedge\lambda
. The last term in (
5.3
)

-\int_{\Sigma}\langle F_{A^{t}}^{\Sigma},\mu\rangle

is always zero. Indeed, if we write
a=A-A_{0}\in L^{2}(\widehat{X},iT^{*}\widehat{X})
, then
F_{A^{t}}^{\Sigma}=2d_{\Sigma}a
is an exact form on the surface
\Sigma
. Since
\mu
is harmonic on
\Sigma
, their inner product is always zero. Hence, (
5.3
) has a definite sign. The integral in (
5.1
) over the compact region
X=\{s\leq 0\}\subset\widehat{X}
can be treated in the usual way. We summarize this remark into a lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
5.1
, there exists a constant
C_{2}(A_{0},\omega_{X},g_{X})
independent of
(A,\Phi)
such that

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi)+C_{2}>\int_{\widehat{X}}\frac{1}{8}|F_{A^{t}}|^{2}+|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{4}(\omega^{+}_{X})|^{2}+\frac{s}{4}|\Phi|^{2}.

Proof.

Note that

|\int_{\widehat{X}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\overline{\omega}_{X}\rangle|=|\int_{X}\langle F_{A^{t}},\overline{\omega}_{X}\rangle|\leq\frac{1}{16}\|F_{A^{t}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\widehat{X})}+C_{3}(A_{0},\omega_{X},g_{X}).\qed

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

Let
\gamma_{0}=(A_{0},\Phi_{0})
be the reference configuration in
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
. For convenience, take its restrictions at the boundary

(B_{i0},\Phi_{i0})=\gamma_{0}|_{\widehat{Y}_{i}}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y}_{i})

as reference configurations in the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{i}}
for
i=1,2
. It suffices to prove the theorem when the section

(a,\phi)=(A,\Phi)-(A_{0},\Phi_{0})\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(\widehat{X},iT^{*}\widehat{X}\oplus S),

is compactly support, and the rest will follow by continuity. Let
X_{S}=\{s\leq S\}\subset\widehat{X}
be the truncated manifold and
Y_{i,S}=\widehat{Y}_{i}\cap X_{S}
. The boundary of
X_{S}
consist of three parts:

-Y_{1,S},Y_{2,S}\text{ and }\{S\}\times W=[-1,1]_{t}\times\{S\}\times\Sigma.

Since
(a,\phi)
is compactly supported, we may discard any boundary integrals over
\{S\}\times W\subset\partial X_{S}
when
S\gg 1
. By the Lichnerowicz-Weizenböck formula
[
KM07
, (4.15)]
, we have

(5.4)

\displaystyle\int_{X_{S}}|D_{A}^{+}\Phi|^{2}=\int_{X_{S}}|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\rho_{4}(F_{A^{t}}^{+})\Phi,\Phi\rangle+\frac{s}{4}|\Phi|^{2}

\displaystyle-\int_{Y_{1,S}}\langle D_{B_{1}}\Phi_{1},\Phi_{1}\rangle+\int_{Y_{2,S}}\langle D_{B_{2}}\Phi_{2},\Phi_{2}\rangle.

Now consider the first equation of (
3.7
):

\displaystyle\int_{X_{S}}|\frac{1}{2}\rho_{4}(F_{A^{t}}^{+}-2\omega^{+}_{X})-(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{S}}\frac{1}{4}|F_{A^{t}}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\langle\rho_{4}(F_{A^{t}}^{+})\Phi,\Phi\rangle+|(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{4}(\omega^{+}_{X})|^{2}

(5.5)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{S}}F_{A^{t}}\wedge F_{A^{t}}-2\int_{X_{S}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega_{X}^{+}\rangle.

Only the second line requires some further work. Note that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{S}}F_{A^{t}}\wedge F_{A^{t}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\int_{X_{S}}F_{A^{t}_{0}}\wedge F_{A^{t}_{0}}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\partial X_{S}}a\wedge(F_{A^{t}}+F_{A_{0}^{t}}).

Finally, using the relation
\omega_{X}=\overline{\omega}_{X}+\omega_{\lambda}
, we compute

\displaystyle 2\int_{X_{S}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega_{X}^{+}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{S}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega_{X}+*_{4}\omega_{X}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{S}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\overline{\omega}_{X}\rangle+\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega_{\lambda}\rangle+\langle F_{A^{t}_{0}},*_{4}\omega_{X}\rangle+\langle 2da,*_{4}\omega_{X}\rangle

\displaystyle=J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}+J_{4}.

J_{1}
and
J_{3}
already show up in (
5.1
) and (
5.2
). As for
J_{2}
and
J_{4}
, note that

\displaystyle J_{4}

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\partial X_{S}}a\wedge\omega_{X}=\int_{Y_{1,S}}(B_{1}^{t}-B^{t}_{10})\wedge\omega_{1}+\int_{Y_{2,S}}(B_{2}^{t}-B^{t}_{20})\wedge\omega_{2},

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{S}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega_{\lambda}\rangle=-\int_{X_{S}}F_{A^{t}}\wedge*_{4}\omega_{X,h}+\int_{X_{S}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega_{\lambda}-\omega_{X,h}\rangle.

Since
*_{4}\omega_{X,h}
is closed, the first term in
J_{2}
is a pairing in cohomology:

[\frac{i}{2\pi}F_{A^{t}}]\cup[\frac{i}{2\pi}*_{4}\omega_{X,h}]\in H^{4}(X,\partial X)\xleftarrow{\cup}H^{2}(X,Z)\otimes H^{2}(X,Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}),

so one may replace
A
by
A_{0}
. Now the energy identity follows by adding (
5.4
) and (
5.5
) together.
∎

5.2.
The 3-Manifold Case

Let
I=[t_{1},t_{2}]_{t}
. In the special case when
\mathbb{X}=I\times\mathbb{Y}:\mathbb{Y}\to\mathbb{Y}
is the product morphism, Theorem
5.1
takes a simpler form.

The 4-manifold
\widehat{X}=I\times\widehat{Y}
is furnished with the product metric. Let
\omega_{X}=\pi^{*}\omega
be the pull-back of
\omega
where
\pi:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{Y}
is the projection map. Any
spin^{c}\
connection
A
on
\widehat{X}
can be written as

(5.6)

A=\frac{d}{dt}+B(t)+c(t)dt\otimes\Id_{S}.

where
B(t)
is a path of
spin^{c}\
connections on
(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and
c(t)\in L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Y};i\mathbb{R})
. Any configuration
\gamma\in(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
gives rise to a path
\check{\gamma}(t)=(B(t),\Psi(t))
in
\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
by setting

\Psi(t)=\Phi|_{\{t\}\times\widehat{Y}}.

Moreover,
\gamma
solves the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
) on
\widehat{X}
if and only if the path
(\check{\gamma}(t),c(t))
forms a downward gradient flowline of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
:

\frac{d}{dt}\gamma(t)=-\grad\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\gamma(t))-\bd_{\gamma(t)}c(t).

Let
A_{0}=\frac{d}{dt}+B_{0}
be the reference connection on
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})=I\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. The curvature form
F_{A_{0}^{t}}
does not involve any
dt
-component, so
F_{A_{0}^{t}}\wedge F_{A^{t}_{0}}\equiv 0
.

Proposition 5.4
.

For any configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
on
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})=I\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, the
L^{2}
-norm of the Seiberg-Witten map
\mathfrak{F}_{X}(A,\Phi)
can be expressed as

\int_{\widehat{X}}|\mathfrak{F}_{X}(A,\Phi)|^{2}=\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi)-\mathcal{E}_{top}(A,\Phi)

where
\mathcal{E}_{top}(A,\Phi)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}(t_{1}))-2\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}(t_{2}))
and

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{I}\|\frac{d}{dt}\check{\gamma}(t)+d_{\check{\gamma}(t)}c(t)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\widehat{Y})}+\|\grad\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}(t))\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\widehat{Y})}

\displaystyle=\int_{I\times\widehat{Y}}\frac{1}{4}|F_{A^{t}}|^{2}+|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})|^{2}+\frac{s}{4}|\Phi|^{2}-\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega\rangle.

The last term can be written as

\int_{I\times\widehat{Y}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega\rangle=\int_{I\times\widehat{Y}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\overline{\omega}\rangle+\int_{I\times\widehat{Y}}\langle F_{A^{t}},\omega_{\lambda}-\omega_{h}\rangle-|I|\int_{\widehat{Y}}F_{B_{0}^{t}}\wedge*_{3}\omega_{h},

where
\omega=\overline{\omega}+\omega_{\lambda}
and
\omega_{\lambda}=\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda
. The co-closed 2-form
\omega_{h}
is constructed by Lemma
3.2
such that
\omega_{\lambda}-\omega_{h}\in L^{2}(\widehat{Y})
. In particular, for any
(B,\Psi)\in\mathcal{C}_{1}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
,

\displaystyle\|\grad\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(B,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(\widehat{Y})}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\widehat{Y}}\frac{1}{4}|F_{B^{t}}|^{2}+|\nabla_{B}\Psi|^{2}+|(\Psi\Psi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{3}(\omega)|^{2}+\frac{s}{4}|\Psi|^{2}-\langle F_{B^{t}},\omega\rangle.

6.
Compactness

6.1.
Statements

With all machinery developed so far, we are ready to state and prove the compactness theorem for the (unperturbed) Seiberg-Witten equations on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
. The result easily generalizes to a complete Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{X}
induced from a morphism
\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
in
\Cob_{s}
. Nevertheless, we will focus on the first case for the sake of simplicity. The analogous results for perturbed equations will be addressed in Section
9
, after we set up tame perturbations in the next part. Now let

\gamma_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(A_{0},\Phi_{0})\text{ with }A=\frac{d}{dt}+B_{0},\ \Phi(t)=\Psi_{0},

be the reference configuration on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
, then it agrees with the standard configuration
(A_{*},\Phi_{*})
over the planar end
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
. For any
k\geq 2
, define

\mathcal{C}_{k,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))=\{(A,\Phi):(A,\Phi)\big|_{I\times\widehat{Y}}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(I\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})),\forall\text{ finite interval }I\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}\}

and
\mathcal{G}_{k+1,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))
in a similar manner. We will set up the Fredholm theory of moduli spaces in a different way in Section
13
. For now, let us stick to these loosely defined spaces.

For any
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k,loc}
and
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
, define the analytic energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;I)
over the interval
I
to be the integral of (
5.7
) over
I\times\widehat{Y}
and

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma,\mathbb{R}_{t}).

One standard assumption below is the finiteness of the total energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}
. Since
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;I)
is alway non-negative, it implies that

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;I)<\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;\mathbb{R}_{t})<\infty\text{ for any }I\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}.

The primary result of this section is the compactness theorem.

Theorem 6.1
.

Suppose
\{\gamma_{n}=(A_{n},\Phi_{n})\}\subset\mathcal{C}_{k,loc}
is a sequence of solutions to the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
) on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
and their analytic energy

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma_{n})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma_{n},\mathbb{R}_{t})<C

is uniformly bounded by a positive constant
C>0
. Then we can find a sequence of gauge transformations
u_{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})
with the following properties. For a subsequence
\{\gamma_{n}^{\prime}\}
of
\{u_{n}(\gamma_{n})\}
and any finite interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
, the restriction of each
\gamma_{n}^{\prime}
on
I\times\widehat{Y}

\gamma_{n}^{\prime}|_{I\times\widehat{Y}}

lies in
\mathcal{C}_{l}(I\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))
. In addition, they converge in
L^{2}_{l}(I\times\widehat{Y})
-topology for any
l\geq 2
.

The main difficulty is to deal with the cylindrical end of
\widehat{Y}
and the proof relies on the exponential decay of
L^{2}_{l}
-norms. To state the result, recall that
\Omega_{n,S}\ (n\in\mathbb{Z},\ S\in\mathbb{R}_{s})
defined in (
2.4
) is a bounded sub-domain of
\mathbb{C}
with smooth boundary, which is centered at
(n,S)\in\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
.

Theorem 6.2
.

For any
C>0
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, there exists constants
\zeta(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}),M_{l}(C,\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})>0
with the following significance. For any solution
\gamma=(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})
to the Seiberg-Witten equations
(\ref{4DSWEQ})
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
with analytic energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi)<C
, we can find a gauge transformation
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})
such that

(6.1)

\|u(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{l,A_{0}}(\Omega_{n,S}\times\Sigma)}\leq M_{l}e^{-\zeta S},

for any
l\geq 1
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
S\geq 0
. Here
\gamma_{0}
is the reference configuration in
\mathcal{C}_{k,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})
.

Theorem
6.1
is an easy corollary of Theorem
6.2
.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

It suffices to prove the case when
I=[-2,2]
. The rest will follow by a patching argument (cf.
[
KM07
, Section 13.6]
). By Theorem
6.2
, for any
\gamma_{n}
in that sequence, we may assume the exponential decay (
6.1
) holds for
\gamma_{n}-\gamma_{0}
. Take
S\gg 1
and let
Y_{S}=\{s\leq S\}
be the truncated 3-manifold.

With the energy equation in Proposition
5.4
, the classical compactness theorem
[
KM07
, Theorem 5.2.1]
implies that a subsequence of
\{\gamma_{n}\}
converges smoothly (up to gauge) in the interior of the compact manifold
I\times Y_{S}.
Suppose
\{u_{n}:I\times Y_{S}\to S^{1}\}
is the sequence of gauge transformations, then the restriction

u_{n}:I\times[S-1,S]_{s}\times\Sigma\to S^{1}

must lie in the same homotopy class when
n\gg 1
(by (
6.1
)). We may correct
\{u_{n}\}
so their restrictions lie in the trivial homotopy class. By a patching argument, we extend
u_{n}
over the whole space
I\times\widehat{Y}
by setting
u_{n}\equiv 1
when
s\geq S+1
. By Theorem
6.2
, a subsequence of
\{u_{n}(\gamma_{n})\}
converges in fact in
L^{2}_{l}
-topology on
[-2+\epsilon,2-\epsilon]\times\widehat{Y}
for some small
\epsilon>0
. This completes the proof of the theorem (some details are left to the readers).
∎

The proof of Theorem
6.2
will dominate the rest of the section.

6.2.
Decay of Local Energy Functional

Recall from Definition
2.3
that the local energy functional of
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
over
\Omega_{n,S}\subset\mathbb{H}^{+}
is defined as

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{1}{4}|F_{A^{t}}|^{2}+|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}+\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})|^{2}.

with
\omega=\mu+ds\wedge\lambda
. We wish to first get an estimate on
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega_{n,S})
for a solution
(A,\Phi)
to (
3.7
) on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
when
S\gg 1
. The main results are as follows.

Theorem 6.3
.

For any
C,\epsilon>0
, there exists a constant
R_{0}(\epsilon,C,\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})>0
with the following significance. For any solution
(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})
to the Seiberg-Witten equations
(\ref{4DSWEQ})
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
with analytic energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi)<C
and any
S>R_{0}
, we have

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega_{n,S})<\epsilon.

The uniform decay in Theorem
6.3
can be improved into exponential decay using Theorem
2.5
:

Theorem 6.4
.

For any
C>0
, there exists constants
\zeta(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}),M_{0}(C,\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})>0
with the following significance. For any solution
(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})
to the Seiberg-Witten equations
(\ref{4DSWEQ})
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
with analytic energy
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi)<C
, any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
S>0
,

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega_{n,S})<M_{0}e^{-\zeta S}.

The proof of Theorem
6.3
will dominate the rest of Subsection
6.2
and it relies on Theorem
2.4
and
2.6
in an essential way. Let us first state a lemma in which we set
\Omega_{S}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Omega_{0,S}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
J=[-3,3]\supset I=[-2,2]
. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists constants
R_{0}(\widehat{Y},\epsilon),\eta(\widehat{Y},\epsilon)>0
with the following significance. For any solution
(A,\Phi)
to the Seiberg-Witten equations
(\ref{4DSWEQ})
on
J\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
with
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;J)<\eta
and any
S>R_{0}
, we must have

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega_{S})<\epsilon.

Proof.

Suppose on the contrary that there exists a sequence
\{(A_{n},\Phi_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
of solutions to the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
) on
J\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, a sequence of numbers
\eta_{n}\to 0
and
R_{n}\to\infty
such that

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;J)<\eta_{n}\text{ and }\mathcal{E}_{an}(A_{n},\Phi_{n};\Omega_{R_{n}})\geq\epsilon.

By Proposition
5.4
and Lemma
5.3
,

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A_{n},\Phi_{n};J\times[0,\infty)_{s})\leq C_{2}^{\prime}

for some uniform constant
C_{2}^{\prime}>0
. Let
\beta_{n}=(A_{n}^{\prime},\Phi_{n}^{\prime})(t,s)=(A_{n},\Phi_{n})(t,s-R_{n})
be the translated configuration defined on
J\times[-R_{n},R_{n}]\times\Sigma
. Since we have a uniform bound on

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{n};J\times[-R_{n},R_{n}]),

the classical compactness theorem
[
KM07
, Theorem 5.2.1]
ensures that there is a subsequence of
\{\beta_{n}\}
that converges in
\mathcal{C}_{loc}^{\infty}
topology to a solution
\beta_{\infty}=(A_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty})
on
J\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
. On the other hand, if we write
\beta_{\infty}
as

(\check{\gamma}(t),c(t))=(B(t),\Psi(t),c(t)),

then Proposition
5.4
implies

\partial_{t}\check{\gamma}(t)+\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}c(t)=-\grad\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}(t))=0,

since
\eta_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. By making
\beta_{\infty}
into temporal gauge (i.e
c(t)\equiv 0
), we conclude that
\check{\gamma}(t)
is independent of
t\in I
and solves the 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.6
) or (
2.5
).

This is the place where the property
(P0)
is used. By Theorem
2.6
, up to gauge,
\gamma(t)
has to be
\mathbb{R}_{s}
-translation invariant, so

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{\infty};I\times[-3,3])=0.

This contradicts the assumption that
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A_{n},\Phi_{n};\Omega_{R_{n}})\geq\epsilon
for each
n
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

Suppose on the contrary that there exists a sequence

\{\beta_{m}=(A_{m},\Phi_{m})\}_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{C}_{k,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})

of solutions to the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
) on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, a sequence of integers
n_{m}\geq 0
and numbers
R_{m}\to\infty
such that

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{m})<C\text{ and }\mathcal{E}_{an}(A_{m},\Phi_{m},\Omega_{n_{m},R_{m}})\geq\epsilon.

Let
J_{n}=[n-3,n+3]
for each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. For each
m
, define the significant set of
\beta_{m}
as

K_{m}=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{m},J_{n})>\eta\},

where
\eta=\eta(\epsilon,\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is the constant obtained in Lemma
6.5
. Then
n_{m}\in K_{m}
. Since there is a uniform upper bound on
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{m},\mathbb{R}_{t})
, we know that

|K_{m}|<C_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}6C/\eta.

By passing to a subsequence, we assume
|K_{m}|
are the same for all
m
. Place elements of
K_{m}
in the increasing order:

a_{1}^{m}<a_{2}^{m}<\cdots<a_{k}^{m},\ k=|K_{m}|.

By passing to a further subsequence, we require that
\lim_{m\to\infty}|a^{m}_{i+1}-a^{m}_{i}|
exists (either finite or infinite) for each
1\leq i\leq k
and it is infinite precisely when
i
is one of

i_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-1<i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{l}<i_{l+1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}k.

Let
N=\max_{0\leq j\leq l,m\geq 0}|a^{m}_{i_{j+1}}-a^{m}_{i_{j}+1}|
. Now consider the translated configuration

\beta_{m}^{\prime}=(A_{m}^{\prime},\Phi_{m}^{\prime})\text{ with }(A_{m}^{\prime},\Phi_{m}^{\prime})(t,s)=(A_{m},\Phi_{m})(t-n_{m},s-R_{m})

defined on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[-R_{m},R_{m}]\times\Sigma
. What we have shown so far implies that

•

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{m}^{\prime},[-N,N]_{t}\times[-R_{m},R_{m}]_{s})
is bounded above by a constant
C_{2}
independent of
\beta_{m}^{\prime}
. This follows from energy equations and the assumption that
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{m})\leq C
.

•

For any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|j|\geq N
and any
S\in\mathbb{R}_{s}
,
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta^{\prime}_{m},\Omega_{j,S})<\epsilon
when
m\gg 1
. Indeed, by the choice of
N
, when
m\gg 1
,
n_{m}+j\not\in K_{m}
and
R_{m}\gg R_{0}-S
. Now apply lemma
6.5

By the classical compactness theorem
[
KM07
, Theorem 5.2.1]
, up to gauge, a subsequence of
\{\beta_{m}^{\prime}\}
will converge in
\mathcal{C}_{loc}^{\infty}
-topology to a solution
\beta_{\infty}=(A_{\infty},\Phi_{\infty})
defined on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
. Moreover, we have the following estimates on its analytic energy:

•

For some large constant
M>0
,
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{\infty},\Omega_{j,S})<\epsilon
whenever
|j|>N
or
|S|>M
;

•

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{\infty},[-N,N]_{t}\times[-M,M]_{s})<\infty
;

•

\mathcal{E}_{an}(\beta_{\infty},\Omega_{0,0})\geq\epsilon
.

Now we draw a contradiction from Theorem
2.4
which rules out such solutions.
∎

6.3.
Decay of
L^{2}_{k}
-norm

Having addressed the exponential decay of the local energy functional

\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega_{n,S})

in Theorem
6.4
, let us estimate the
L^{2}_{k}
-norm of
(A,\Phi)
over the sub-domain
\Omega_{n,S}
in terms of
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega_{n,S})
. Aside from Remark
5.2
, this is the second reason why the local energy functional is useful. For the sake of simplicity, let us state the results for the compact domain

\Omega_{0}\subset[-2,2]_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\subset\mathbb{C}

defined in (
2.3
). Let
M=\Omega_{0}\times\Sigma
. Recall that
\gamma_{*}=(A_{*},\Phi_{*})
defined by (
3.8
) is the standard configuration on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
. For any smooth
\gamma=(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}(M)
, set
(a,\phi)=\gamma-\gamma_{*}
and consider the gauge fixing condition

(6.2)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\bd_{\gamma_{*}}^{*}(a,\phi)&\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}-d^{*}a+i\re\langle\phi,i\Phi_{*}\rangle=0\\
\langle a,\vec{n}\rangle&=0\text{ at }\partial M.\end{array}\right.

The proof of Theorem
6.2
requires three additional lemmas, summarized as follows:

•

Lemma
6.6
: put
\gamma
into the Coulomb-Neumann gauge slice of
\gamma_{*}
;

•

Lemma
6.7
: once
\gamma
is in the slice, estimate the
L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}
-norm of
(a,\phi)=\gamma-\gamma_{*}
in terms of
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;\Omega_{0})
;

•

Lemma
6.8
: once
\gamma
is in the slice, estimate the
L^{2}_{l,A_{*}}
-norm of
(a,\phi)=\gamma-\gamma_{*}
in terms of
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma;\Omega_{0})
for any
l\geq 1
.

Lemma 6.6
.

There exist constants
\epsilon_{0},C_{0}>0
with the following significance. For any configuration
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}(\Omega_{0}\times\Sigma)
with

(6.3)

\|\gamma-\gamma_{*}\|_{L^{2}_{2,A_{*}}(M)}<\epsilon_{0}

then we can find a smooth function
f:M\to i\mathbb{R}
such that
e^{f}\cdot\gamma
satisfies the Coulomb-Neumann gauge fixing condition (
6.2
). Moreover,

\|e^{f}\cdot\gamma-\gamma_{*}\|_{L^{2}_{2,A_{*}}(M)}\leq C_{0}\|\gamma-\gamma_{*}\|_{L^{2}_{2,A_{*}}(M)}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{2}
be the subspace of
\mathcal{T}_{2,\gamma_{*}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}_{2}(M,iT^{*}M\oplus S^{+})
subject to the gauge fixing condition (
6.2
). Consider the non-linear map:

\displaystyle U:L_{3}^{2}(M;i\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{K}_{2}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{T}_{2,\gamma_{*}}

\displaystyle(f,(a,\phi))

\displaystyle=(a-df,(e^{f}-1)\cdot\Phi_{*}+e^{f}\cdot\phi).

The linearized operator
\mathcal{D}_{0}U
of
U
at
(0,(0,0))
is invertible. Now our lemma follows from the implicit function theorem.
∎

Suppose now that
\gamma
already lies in the Coulomb-Neumann gauge slice of
\gamma_{*}
. The next step is to estimate
\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}}
in terms of the local energy functional
\mathcal{E}_{an}(A,\Phi;\Omega_{0})
.

Lemma 6.7
.

There exist constants
\epsilon_{1},C_{1}>0
with the following significance. For any
\gamma
subject to the gauge fixing condition (
6.2
), if
\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}}<\epsilon_{1}
, then

\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}}^{2}\leq C_{1}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma,\Omega_{0}).

Proof.

Consider the non-linear operator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(a,\phi)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}_{1}+\mathcal{F}_{2}\text{ where}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{1}(a,\phi)

\displaystyle=(da,\nabla_{A_{*}}\phi+a\otimes\Phi_{*},(\Phi_{*}\phi^{*}+\phi\Phi_{*}^{*})_{0},\bd^{*}_{\gamma_{*}}(a,\phi)),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{2}(a,\phi)

\displaystyle=(0,a\otimes\phi,(\phi\phi^{*})_{0},0),

so
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is the linear part of
\mathcal{F}
and
\|\mathcal{F}(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{2}=\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma,\Omega_{0})
by Definition
2.3
. Using the identity

|(\Phi_{*}\phi^{*}+\phi\Phi_{*}^{*})_{0}|^{2}+|\im\langle\phi,\Phi_{*}\rangle|^{2}=|\Phi_{*}|^{2}|\phi|^{2},

we calculate that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}_{1}(a,\phi)\|^{2}_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle=\|da\|_{2}^{2}+\|d^{*}a\|_{2}^{2}+\|\nabla_{A_{*}}\phi\|_{2}^{2}+\|a\otimes\Phi_{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\||\phi||\Phi_{*}|\|_{2}^{2}+K_{3}\text{ where }

\displaystyle K_{3}

\displaystyle=2\re\int_{M}\langle\nabla_{A_{*}}\phi,a\otimes\Phi_{*}\rangle-\langle\phi,(d^{*}a)\Phi_{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=2\re\int_{M}d^{*}(\langle\phi,\Phi_{*}\rangle\cdot a)+\langle a\otimes\phi,\nabla_{A_{*}}\Phi_{*}\rangle=0.

In the last step, we used the facts that
\Phi_{*}
is
\nabla_{A_{*}}
-parallel and
\langle a,\vec{n}\rangle=0
at
\partial M
. Hence,

\|\mathcal{F}_{1}(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}(M)}\geq c_{1}\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}},

for some
c_{1}>0
. Finally,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|\mathcal{F}_{1}\|_{2}-\|\mathcal{F}_{2}\|_{2}\geq c_{1}\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}}-m_{3}\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}}^{2}\geq\frac{c_{1}}{2}\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}}

if
\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}}\leq c_{1}/2m_{3}
, where
m_{3}
is the constant that appears in the Sobolev embedding
L^{2}_{1}\times L^{2}_{1}\to L^{4}
.
∎

Now we come to estimate the
L^{2}_{k}
-norm of
(a,\phi)
. Consider a closed subset
\Omega_{0}^{\prime}\subset\Omega_{0}
with a smooth boundary such that

[-1,1]_{t}\times[1,3]\subset(\Omega_{0}^{\prime})^{\circ}\subset\Omega_{0}^{\prime}\subset(\Omega_{0})^{\circ}.

Lemma 6.8
.

There exist constants
\epsilon_{k},C_{k}>0
for each
k\geq 1
with the following significance. For any smooth solution
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}(M)
to the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
), if
\gamma
is subject to the gauge fixing condition (
6.2
) and
\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}(M)}<\epsilon_{k}
, then

\|(a,\phi)\|_{L^{2}_{k,A_{*}}(\Omega_{0}^{\prime}\times\Sigma)}^{2}\leq C_{k}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma,\Omega_{0}).

Proof.

The case when
k=1
is settled in Lemma
6.7
. For
k>1
, this follows from the standard bootstrapping argument
[
KM07
, P.107]
. To illustrate, consider the case when
1<k<2
. Take a cut-off function
\chi_{4}
such that

\chi_{4}\equiv 1\text{ on }\Omega_{0}^{\prime};\ \text{supp}\chi_{4}\subset(\Omega_{0})^{\circ}.

The section
v\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(a,\phi)\in C^{\infty}(M,iT^{*}M\oplus S)
is subject to a non-linear elliptic equation:

Dv+v\#v=0

where
\#
stands for a certain bilinear form that involves only point-wise multiplication. By Gårding’s inequality, for any
0<\eta<1
,

\displaystyle\|\chi_{4}v\|_{L^{2}_{1+\eta}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\|D(\chi_{4}v)\|_{L^{2}_{\eta}(M)}+\|v\|_{2}\leq m_{4}\|v\|_{L^{2}_{1}}+\|(\chi_{4}v)\#v\|_{L^{2}_{\eta}}

\displaystyle\leq m_{4}\|v\|_{L^{2}_{1}}+m_{5}\|\chi_{4}v\|_{L^{2}_{1+\eta}}\|v\|_{L^{2}_{1}}

If
\|v\|_{L^{2}_{1}}<1/(2m_{5})
, then we use the rearrangement argument to show that

\|v\|_{L^{2}_{1+\eta}(\Omega_{0}^{\prime}\times\Sigma)}\leq\|\chi_{4}v\|_{L^{2}_{1+\eta}(M)}\leq 2m_{4}\|v\|_{L^{2}_{1}}\leq 2m_{4}\sqrt{C_{1}}\cdot\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma,\Omega_{0})},

so we set
\epsilon_{1+\eta}=\min\{\epsilon_{1},1/(2m_{5})\}
. In the last step, we used Lemma
6.7
to estimate
\|v\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}}
in terms of
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma,\Omega_{0})
. When
k\geq 2
, we need more cut-off functions to separate
\Omega_{0}^{\prime}
from
\Omega_{0}
and use inductions. In fact, we can take

\epsilon_{k}=\min\{\epsilon_{1},1/(2m_{5})\}

for any
k>1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

We divide the proof into three steps. Lemma
6.6
and
6.8
will be used only in the last step. In
Step
1 and
Step
2, we arrange so that the assumptions of these lemmas can be satisfied.

Step
1. By the classical compactness theorem
[
KM07
, Theorem 5.2.1]
, for any
\epsilon>0
, we can find a constant
\eta(\epsilon)>0
with the following property. Under the assumption of Theorem
6.2
, if
\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma,\Omega_{0})<\eta(\epsilon)
, then there exists a gauge transformation
u^{\prime}:\Omega_{0}\to S^{1}
such that

\|u^{\prime}(\gamma)-\gamma_{*}\|_{L^{2}_{2}(\Omega_{0}^{\prime}\times\Sigma)}<\epsilon.

At this point, we have no controls of the function
\eta:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Step
2. We wish to find a gauge transformation
u_{1}\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})
such that

(6.4)

\|u_{1}(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{2,A_{*}}(\Omega_{n,S}\times\Sigma)}<\min\{\epsilon_{0},\frac{\epsilon_{l}}{C_{0}}\}.

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
S\gg 1
, where
\epsilon_{0}
and
\epsilon_{l}
are positive constants constructed in Lemma
6.6
and
6.8
. (
6.4
) is provided by the uniform
L^{\infty}
decay of the local energy functional. Let
S=m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be an integer and apply
Step
1 to the domain

\Omega_{n,m},\forall n\in\mathbb{Z},m>R_{0}(\eta(\epsilon),C),

where
R_{0}
is the constant obtained in Theorem
6.3
. We find gauge transformations
u_{n,m}\in\mathcal{G}^{e}(\Omega_{n,m}\times\Sigma)
such that

\|u_{n,m}(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{2}(\Omega_{n,m}^{\prime}\times\Sigma)}<\epsilon.

Here
\Omega_{n,m}^{\prime}
is the translated domain of
\Omega_{0}^{\prime}\subset\Omega_{0}
:

\Omega_{n,m}^{\prime}=\{(t,s):(t-n,s-m)\in\Omega_{0}^{\prime}\}\subset\Omega_{n,m}.

The collection of domains
\{(\Omega_{n,m}^{\prime})^{\circ}\}
still forms an open cover of
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times[R_{0}+1)_{s}\times\Sigma
. By a patching argument (cf.
[
KM07
, Section 13.6]
), we can find a global gauge transformation
u_{1}
such that

\|u_{1}(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{1}(\Omega_{n,m}\times\Sigma)}<N_{1}\epsilon.

for a constant
N_{1}>0
. Then one may achieve (
6.4
) by starting with
\epsilon
small enough.

Step
3. Now apply Lemma
6.6
to
u_{1}(\gamma)
on each
\Omega_{n,m}
with
m>R_{0}
. We find some smooth functions
f_{n,m}:\Omega_{n,m}\times\Sigma\to i\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\|e^{f_{n,m}}\cdot u_{1}(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{1,A_{*}}(\Omega_{n,m}\times\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\leq\|e^{f_{n,m}}\cdot u_{1}(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{2,A_{*}}(\Omega_{n,m}\times\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\|u_{1}(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|_{L^{2}_{2,A_{*}}(\Omega_{n,m}\times\Sigma)}\leq\epsilon_{l}.

and
e^{f_{n,m}}\cdot u_{1}(\gamma)
lies in the Coulomb gauge slice (
6.2
) of
\gamma_{*}
. Using Lemma
6.8
and Theorem
6.4
, we estimate the
L^{2}_{l,A_{*}}
-norm of the resulting configuration:

\|e^{f_{n,m}}\cdot u_{1}(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{l,A_{*}}(\Omega_{n,m}^{\prime}\times\Sigma)}\leq C_{l}\cdot\mathcal{E}_{an}(\gamma,\Omega_{n,m})\leq C_{l}M_{0}e^{-\zeta m}.

Finally, using the patching argument once again, we find a global gauge transformation
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1,loc}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y})
such that

\|u(\gamma)-\gamma_{0}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{l,A_{*}}(\Omega_{n,m}\times\Sigma)}\leq N_{2}C_{l}M_{0}e^{-\zeta m}.

for a constant
N_{2}>0
. This completes the proof of Theorem
6.2
.
∎

Part III
Perturbations

In order to make the moduli spaces on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
smooth and define the Floer homology of the 3-manifold
(Y,\partial Y=\Sigma)
, a suitable perturbation
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+f
of the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is needed. We follow the construction of tame perturbations in
[
KM07
, Section 10-11]
. However, there is one distinct feature of our situation, which requires some technical tricks to deal with:

(
\star
)

We want the perturbation supported within
a compact region
of
\widehat{Y}
so that the Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.7
) defined on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
remains unperturbed on the planar end
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{+}\times\Sigma
, and Theorem
2.5
is applicable.

Hence, the error term
f
must factorize through the restriction map to the truncated manifold
Y_{n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{s\leq n\}\subset\widehat{Y}
for some
n\geq 0
:

\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\to\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(Y_{n},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}).

As a result, the perturbation space is not large enough to separate all tangent vectors and points of
\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
as in
[
KM07
, Proposition 11.2.1]
. Nevertheless, we can still achieve the transversality of moduli spaces on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
, even with this smaller perturbation space. In fact, one may even require that
n=0
, so
Y_{n}=Y=\{s\leq 0\}
.

Part
III
is organized as follows. In Section
7
, we introduce the so-called tame perturbations (Definition
7.3
) and state the formal mapping properties that they enjoy.

In Section
8
, we take up the task to construct tame perturbations. The separation properties are examined carefully in Subsection
8.2
. The Banach space
\mathcal{P}
of tame perturbations is constructed in Subsection
8.5
.

Section
9
is devoted to the compactness theorems for perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations. Since tame perturbations are made compactly supported, the proofs in Section
6
apply verbatim to this case.

7.
Abstract Perturbations

The perturbation that we deal with is a continuous section
(k>1)

\mathfrak{q}:\mathcal{C}_{k-\frac{1}{2}}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\to\mathcal{T}_{0}

where
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is the
L^{2}
-completion of the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
introduced in Section
4
. The perturbation
\mathfrak{q}
is required to be the formal gradient of a
\mathcal{G}_{k+1/2}(\widehat{Y})
-invariant continuous function
f:\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\to\mathbb{R}
, and we write
\mathfrak{q}=\grad f
. This means that

f(\check{\gamma}(1))-f(\check{\gamma}(0))=\int_{0}^{1}\langle\dot{\check{\gamma}},\mathfrak{q}(\check{\gamma}(t))\rangle_{L^{2}}dt

for any smooth path
\check{\gamma}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. Take

\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+f

to be the perturbed Chern-Simons-Dirac functional. Let
I=[t_{1},t_{2}]
and
\widehat{Z}
be the product
spin^{c}\
manifold
I\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. The down-ward gradient flowline equation of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
becomes

(7.1)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\check{\gamma}(t)

\displaystyle=-\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}(t))-\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}c(t)

\displaystyle=-\grad\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}(t))-\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}c(t)-\mathfrak{q}(\check{\gamma}(t)),

where
\check{\gamma}(t)=(B(t),\Psi(t))
is a underlying path in
\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and

(7.2)

A=\frac{d}{dt}+B(t)+c(t)dt\otimes\Id_{S},\ \Phi|_{\{t\}\times Y}=\Psi(t)

is the corresponding 4-dimensional configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
in
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Z})
. In this way, the continuous section
\mathfrak{q}
extends to a section of the trivial bundle
\mathcal{V}_{0}
over
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Z})
:

(7.3)

\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}:\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Z})\to\mathcal{V}_{0}=L^{2}(\widehat{Z},i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})\oplus S^{-})\times\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Z})

by sending
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
to
\mathfrak{q}(\check{\gamma}(t))
at each time slice
t\in I
. Here we use the 3-dimensional Clifford multiplication
\rho_{3}
to identify the bundle
iT^{*}\widehat{Y}
with
i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})
over
\widehat{Z}
. We wish that this section
\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}
extends to a smooth section of
\mathcal{V}_{k}\to\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Z})
for any
k\geq 2
, so (
7.1
) is cast into the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equation
\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}}=0
where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z}}+\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}:\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Z})\to\mathcal{V}_{k-1},

and
\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z}}
is defined as in (
3.7
).

We do not have a canonical
L^{2}_{j}
norm on the space
\Gamma(\widehat{Z},i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})\oplus S^{-})
. For each
\gamma=(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Z})
, we define a norm at the fiber
\mathcal{V}_{j}|_{\gamma}
using
A
as the covariant derivative, i.e.

\|v\|_{L^{2}_{j,A}}^{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\sum_{n=0}^{j}\|\nabla_{A}^{n}v\|^{2}

for any
v\in\mathcal{V}_{j}|_{\gamma}
. This family of norms on
\mathcal{V}_{j}
is equivariant under the gauge action of
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Z})
. Similarly, we define the
L^{2}_{j,A}
norm on
\mathcal{T}_{j}\to\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Z})
. Then the
l
-th derivative of
\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}
at
\gamma
is a bounded multi-linear map:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}^{l}\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}

\displaystyle\in\Mult^{l}\big({\bigtimes}_{l}L^{2}_{k,A}(\widehat{Z},iT^{*}\widehat{Z}\otimes S^{+}),L^{2}_{k,A}(i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})\oplus S^{-})\big)

\displaystyle=\Mult^{l}({\bigtimes}_{l}\mathcal{T}_{k},\mathcal{V}_{k}).

The bundle map
\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}^{l}\mathfrak{q}
might not be a local operator: it does not necessarily send compactly supported sections on
\widehat{Y}
to another section with the same or smaller support. However, this is a property enjoyed by derivatives
\mathcal{D}^{l}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z}}
of the unperturbed Seiberg-Witten map
\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z}}
, which motivates the next definition:

Definition 7.1
.

For any closed subset
\Omega\subset\widehat{Y}
, a perturbation
\mathfrak{q}
is said to be supported on
\Omega
if
\text{supp}\ \mathfrak{q}(\check{\gamma})\subset\Omega
for any
\check{\gamma}\in\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and

\mathfrak{q}(\check{\gamma}_{1})=\mathfrak{q}(\check{\gamma}_{2})

for any configurations
\check{\gamma}_{1},\check{\gamma}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
such that
\check{\gamma}_{1}=\check{\gamma}_{2}
on
\Omega
.
∎

We are primarily interested in the case when
\Omega=Y_{n}=\{s\leq n\}
for some
n\geq 0
. It turns out that the choice of the integer
n
is inconsequential for the Floer homology, so we may safely set
n=0
and focus on the case when
\Omega=Y
.

Remark 7.2
.

One may even take
\Omega=[0,1]_{s}\times\Sigma\subset\widehat{Y}
and the construction in Section
8
would be simplified if one uses the gauge fixing condition along each fiber
\{s\}\times\Sigma
.
∎

For technical reasons, we also need completions of bundles and the configuration space with respect to other Sobolev norms
L^{p}_{k}
with
p\neq 2
. Let

\mathcal{C}_{k}^{(p)},\ \mathcal{T}_{k}^{(p)},\ \mathcal{V}_{k}^{(p)}

be the resulting space and bundles when
k\geq 1
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
. Note that
\mathcal{C}_{k}^{(2)}(\widehat{Z})=\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Z})
and so on.

Let us state the constraints on the perturbation
\mathfrak{q}=\grad f
.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
Y^{\prime}
be a smooth co-dimension
0
submanifold of
\widehat{Y}
with possibly non-empty boundary. We usually take
Y^{\prime}
to be either
Y=\{s\leq 0\}
or
\widehat{Y}
. For each integer
k\geq 2
, a perturbation
\mathfrak{q}
given as a section

\mathfrak{q}:\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\to\mathcal{T}_{0}.

is called
k
-tame in
Y^{\prime}
if it is the formal gradient of a continuous
\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y})
-invariant function
f
on
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})
such that

(A1)

the corresponding 4-dimensional perturbation
\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}
defines an element:

\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{j}(\widehat{Z}),\mathcal{V}_{j})

for any integer
j\in[2,k]
;

(A2)

When
p>3
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}
also defines an element in

C^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{j}^{(p)}(\widehat{Z}),\mathcal{V}_{j}^{(p)})

for any integer
j\in[1,k]
;

(A3)

\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}
extends to a continuous map:

\mathcal{C}_{1}(\widehat{Z})\to\mathcal{V}^{(m)}_{0}

for any
2\leq m<4
.

(A4)

for each integer
j\in[-k,k]
, the first derivative

\mathcal{D}\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Z}),\Hom(T\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Z}),\mathcal{V}_{k}))

extends to a smooth map

\mathcal{D}\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Z}),\Hom(\mathcal{T}_{j},\mathcal{V}_{j}));

(A5)

for any
(B,\Psi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y})
, the
L^{2}_{k}
-section
\mathfrak{q}(B,\Psi)
is supported on
Y^{\prime}
:

\text{supp}\ \mathfrak{q}(B,\Psi)\subset Y^{\prime}.

Moreover, there exists a constant
m_{2}>0
such that

\|\mathfrak{q}(B,\Psi)\|_{L^{2}(Y^{\prime})}\leq m_{2}(\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}(Y^{\prime})}+1),

for any
(B,\Psi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y})
.

(A6)

For any
0\leq\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}
,
\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}
extends to a continuous map

\mathcal{C}_{1-\epsilon}(\widehat{Z})\to\mathcal{V}_{0}.

(A7)

the 3-dimensional perturbation
\mathfrak{q}
defines a
C^{1}
-section

\mathfrak{q}:\mathcal{C}_{1}(\widehat{Y})\to\mathcal{T}_{0}.

We simply say that
\mathfrak{q}
is tame in
Y^{\prime}
if
\mathfrak{q}
is
k
-tame in
Y^{\prime}
for any
k\geq 2
. We may not mention the support
Y^{\prime}
when
Y^{\prime}=\widehat{Y}
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

When
Y^{\prime}=\widehat{Y}
, Definition
7.3
agrees with
[
KM07
, Definition 10.5.1]
, with some minor changes in properties
(A2)
(A3)
(A5)
(A6)
. Our construction of tame perturbations in Section
8
ends up with weaker mapping properties, in exchange for having them compactly supported.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

Let us briefly explain where these properties will be used:

•

(A1)
(A2)
(A3)
(A6)
will be used in the compactness theorem for the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations, i.e. Theorem
9.5
. They give intermediate steps in the bootstrapping arguments;

•

(A5)
is used in the energy equation for the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations, i.e. Proposition
9.1
;

•

(A4)
is relevant with the linear theory in Part
IV
;

•

(A7)
will be used in the proof of the exponential decay result in time direction, which we will not actually work out in this paper, cf.
[
KM07
, Section 13.4]
, in particular
[
KM07
, Lemma 13.4.3]
.∎

8.
Constructing Tame Perturbations

8.1.
Cylinder Functions

The construction of cylinder functions in the book
[
KM07
, Section 11]
involves a global gauge slice, which prevents perturbations being local. Instead, we adopt a variation that is reminiscent of the holonomy perturbations in instanton Floer homology to achieve our goal.

First, we fix a smooth embedding of
S^{1}\times D^{2}
into
\widehat{Y}
, where
D^{2}=B(0,1)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the unit disk:

\iota:S^{1}\times D^{2}\to\widehat{Y}.

To find such an
\iota
, one may first embed the core
S^{1}\times\{0\}
into
\widehat{Y}
and extend this map to a tubular neighborhood of the image. We pull back the metric and the spin bundle
S\to\widehat{Y}
via
\iota
. The induced Riemannian metric
g_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\iota^{*}g_{Y}
might not agree with the product metric

g_{std}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\iota^{*}g_{\widehat{Y}}|_{S^{1}\times\{0\}}+g_{D^{2}},

on
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, where
g_{D^{2}}
is the standard Euclidean metric of
D^{2}
. They are related by a smooth symmetric bundle map
K:T^{*}(S^{1}\times D^{2})\to T^{*}(S^{1}\times D^{2})
(with respect to
g_{std}
) such that

\langle b_{1},b_{2}\rangle_{1}=\langle K(b_{1}),b_{2}\rangle_{std}.

for any co-vectors
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
. The volume forms of
g_{1}
and
g_{std}
differ by a smooth positive function
\eta>0
:

dvol_{1}=\eta\cdot dvol_{std}.

It is only important to know that
K
and
\eta
are smooth; the Clifford multiplication
\rho_{3}
is never needed for the purpose of perturbations.

Let
(B_{0},\Psi_{0})
be the reference configuration in
\mathcal{C}_{k}(Y)
. For any
(B,\Psi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y})
, take the difference

(b,\psi)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(B,\Psi)-(B_{0},\Psi_{0})\in L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Y},iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S).

There are three classes of perturbations to be considered. The first two concern the imaginary valued 1-form
b
. The last one deals with the spin section
\Psi
.

(B1)

For any compactly supported 1-form
c\in\Omega^{1}_{c}(S^{1}\times D^{2},i\mathbb{R})
, define

\displaystyle r_{c}:\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(b,\psi)

\displaystyle\mapsto\int_{S^{1}\times D^{2}}b\wedge d\bar{c}

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{1}\times D^{2}}\langle b,*_{1}dc\rangle_{g_{1}}dvol_{1}=\int_{S^{1}\times D^{2}}\langle b,*_{std}dc\rangle_{g_{std}}dvol_{std},

where
*_{1}
and
*_{std}
stand for the Hodge star operators of
g_{1}
and
g_{std}
respectively. The formal gradient of
r_{c}
is

\grad r_{c}=*_{1}dc,

while using
g_{std}
we obtain

\grad_{std}r_{c}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}*_{std}dc=\eta K(\grad r_{c}).

(B2)

Fix a compactly supported 2-form
\nu\in\Omega_{c}^{1}(D^{2},i\mathbb{R})
on the disk
D^{2}
with

\int_{D^{2}}\nu=i,

and define

\displaystyle r_{\nu}:\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle(b,\psi)

\displaystyle\mapsto\int_{S^{1}\times D^{2}}b\wedge\pi^{*}\overline{\nu},

where
\pi:S^{1}\times D^{2}\to D^{2}
is the projection map. Unlike
r_{c}
,
r_{\nu}
is not fully gauge-invariant. For any
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
,

r_{\nu}(u(b,\psi))-r_{\nu}(b,\psi)=-2\pi\deg(u\circ\iota:S^{1}\times\{0\}\to S^{1})\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

Hence,
r_{\nu}
descends to a circle valued function

[r_{\nu}]:\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y})\to\mathbb{R}/(2\pi\alpha\mathbb{Z})

where
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is the multiplicity of
\iota_{*}([S^{1}\times\{0\}])
in
H_{1}(Y,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
, i.e
\iota_{*}([S^{1}\times\{0\}])
is
\alpha
times a primitive class in
H_{1}(Y,\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
. Using the Euclidean metric of
D^{2}
, one may conveniently set

\nu=i\chi_{2}(z)dvol_{D^{2}}

where
\chi_{2}
is a cut-off function on
D^{2}
with
\chi_{2}(z)\equiv 1
when
|z|\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

(B3)

Fix a gauge transformation
u_{1}:\widehat{Y}\to S^{1}
with the following properties:

•

u_{1}
is smooth on
\widehat{Y}
;

•

The composition
u_{1}\circ\iota:S^{1}\times\{0\}\to S^{1}
is harmonic and has degree
\alpha
.

•

u_{1}\circ\iota:S^{1}\times D^{2}\to S^{1}
is constant in
D^{2}
.

Let the transformation
u_{1}
act on the bundle
\mathbb{R}_{x}\times S\to\mathbb{R}_{x}\times(S^{1}\times D^{2})
by the formula:

u^{n}_{1}(x,\Phi)\mapsto(x-2\pi n\alpha,u^{n}_{1}\Phi).

Passing to the quotient space, we obtain a bundle
\mathbb{S}
over
(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\alpha\mathbb{Z})\times(S^{1}\times D^{2})
. If
\Upsilon
is a compactly supported smooth section of
\mathbb{S}
, let
\tilde{\Upsilon}
denote its lift as a section of
\mathbb{R}_{x}\times S\to\mathbb{R}_{x}\times(S^{1}\times D^{2})
. Then
\tilde{\Upsilon}
is an equivariant section, as

\tilde{\Upsilon}(x-2\pi n\alpha,\theta,z)=u^{n}_{1}\tilde{\Upsilon}(x,\theta,z)

for any
(\theta,z)\in S^{1}\times D^{2}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}_{x}
. Let
b_{z}=b|_{S^{1}\times\{z\}}
be the restriction of the 1-form
b
over the
S^{1}
-fiber at
z\in D^{2}
. Using the product metric
g_{std}
, we write

b_{z}=b^{1}_{z}+b^{h}_{z}

in terms of the Hodge decomposition along each fiber
S^{1}\times\{z\}
with

b^{1}_{z}\text{ exact and }b^{h}_{z}\text{ harmonic }(\text{the coexact part }b^{2}_{z}=0).

Let
d_{S^{1}}^{*}
be the adjoint of the exterior differential
d_{S^{1}}
over
S^{1}\times\{0\}
and

G:C^{\infty}(S^{1},i\mathbb{R})\to C^{\infty}(S^{1},i\mathbb{R})

be the Green operator. Then the exact part
b_{z}^{1}
can be explicitly written as

b_{z}^{1}=d_{S^{1}}Gd_{S^{1}}^{*}b_{z},

and
b^{h}_{z}
stands for the harmonic part of
b_{z}
. It is tempting to form the map:

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\dagger}:\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})

\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}(S^{1}\times D^{2},S)

\displaystyle(b,\psi)

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{-Gd^{*}_{S^{1}}b_{z}}\tilde{\Upsilon}(r_{\nu}(b),\theta,z)\text{ on }S^{1}\times\{z\},

which is
equivariant
under the action of
u^{n}_{1}
. However,
\Upsilon^{\dagger}
is
not
equivariant under the action of the full gauge group
\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y})
(compare
[
KM07
, P.173]
). In fact,
\Upsilon^{\dagger}
is invariant under
\Map(D^{2},S^{1})
, the space of gauge transformations that are constant along each fiber
S^{1}\times\{z\}
.

To circumvent this problem, let
\Psi_{z}
and
\Upsilon^{\dagger}_{z}
be the restriction of
\Psi
and
\Upsilon^{\dagger}
along the fiber
S^{1}\times\{z\}
for any
z\in D^{2}
. Fix an
S^{1}
-invariant function
h:\mathbb{C}_{w}\to\mathbb{R}
. For instance, set

h(w)=\chi_{3}(|w|^{2}),\forall w\in\mathbb{C},

for some cut-off function
\chi_{3}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

\chi_{3}(t)\equiv 1\text{ if }t\leq 1;\ \chi_{3}(t)\equiv 0\text{ if }t\geq 2.

Then the composition
h(\sigma(z)):\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})\to\mathbb{R}
is fully gauge invariant, where

\displaystyle\sigma(z)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{S^{1}\times\{z\}}\langle\Psi_{z},\Upsilon^{\dagger}_{z}\rangle.

Finally, define

q_{\Upsilon}(b,\psi)=\int_{D^{2}}h(\sigma(z))\chi_{2}(z)dvol_{D^{2}},

where
\chi_{2}
is the cut-off function on
D^{2}
defined in
(B2)
.

By choosing a finite collection of 1-forms
c_{1},\cdots,c_{n}
and smooth sections
\Upsilon_{1},\cdots,\Upsilon_{m}
of
\mathbb{S}
, we obtain a map

\Xi=(r_{c_{1}},\cdots,r_{c_{n}},[r_{\nu}],q_{\Upsilon_{1}},\cdots,q_{\Upsilon_{m}}):\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\alpha\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Definition 8.1
.

A function
f
defined on
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})
is called
a cylinder function
if it arises as the composition
g\circ\Xi
where

•

the map
\Xi:\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\alpha\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
is defined as above, using any compactly supported forms
c_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq n)
defined on
S^{1}\times D^{2}
and compactly supported sections
\Upsilon_{j}\ (1\leq j\leq m)
on
(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\alpha\mathbb{Z})\times(S^{1}\times D^{2})
, for any
n,m\geq 0
;

•

the function

g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\alpha\mathbb{Z})\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}

is any smooth function with compact support.

A cylindrical function is fully gauge invariant.
∎

Theorem 8.2
.

For any cylinder function
f:\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})\to\mathbb{R}
, its formal gradient

\grad f:\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})\to\mathcal{T}_{0}

is a perturbation tame in
Y^{\prime}=\im\iota
, in the sense of Definition
7.3
, where
\iota:S^{1}\times D^{2}\hookrightarrow\widehat{Y}
is the embedding used to define
f
.

We will prove Theorem
8.2
in Subsection
8.4
.

8.2.
Cylinder Functions and Embeddings

In this subsection, we examine the separating property of cylinder functions. The main results are Proposition
8.4
and
8.6
.

Fix an embedding
\iota:S^{1}\times D^{2}\hookrightarrow\widehat{Y}
, and define

\Cylin(\iota)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{f:f\text{ is a cylinder function defined via }\iota\}.

It is reasonable to ask: to what extend elements of
\Cylin(\iota)
separate points and tangent vectors of
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})
. Apparently, if
(B_{1},\Psi_{1})
is identical to
(B_{2},\Psi_{2})
over the image of
\iota
up to gauge, then they can not be separated by any element of
\Cylin(\iota)
, because only local information is employed when defining cylinder functions. In addition, they can not be separated if
B_{1}=B_{2}
and

e^{i\theta(z)}\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{2}

for some smooth function
\theta:D^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
as the function
h(\sigma(z))
defined in
(B3)
is fully gauge invariant. In fact, this is the worst case that can happen:

Proposition 8.3
.

Take
\gamma_{i}=(B_{i},\Psi_{i})\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})\ (i=1,2)
. Suppose for any cylinder function
f\in\Cylin(\iota)
, we always have

f(\gamma_{1})=f(\gamma_{2}),

then there exists a gauge transformation
v\in\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y})
and some function
\theta:B(0,1/3)\to\mathbb{R}
such that

v(B_{1})=B_{2},\ e^{i\theta(z)}v\cdot\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{2}

over the smaller solid torus
\iota(S^{1}\times B(0,1/3))
. The function
\theta
might not be continuous because of the zero locus of
\Psi_{1}
.

Proof.

Take
(b_{i},\psi_{i})=(B_{i},\Psi_{i})-(B_{0},\Psi_{0})
and set

\delta b=b_{2}-b_{1}.

By our assumptions,
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
can not be separated by any functions of classes
(B1)
(B2)
and
(B3)
. First, we claim that
\delta b
is closed on
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, since

0=r_{c}(b_{2})-r_{c}(b_{1})=r_{c}(\delta b)=\int_{S^{1}\times D^{2}}\delta b\wedge d\bar{c}=\int_{S^{1}\times D^{2}}d(\delta b)\wedge\bar{c}

for any compactly supported 1-form
c
. Moreover,

r_{\nu}(\delta b)=r_{\nu}(b_{2})-r_{\nu}(b_{1})=2\pi n\alpha\in\mathbb{R}

for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, since
[r_{\nu}](b_{1})=[r_{\nu}](b_{2})
. Using the gauge transformation
u_{1}
from
(B3)
, we may place
\gamma_{1}
by

u_{1}^{-n}(\gamma_{1})

to make
r_{\nu}(b_{2})-r_{\nu}(b_{1})
zero. From now on, let us assume
r_{\nu}(\delta b)=0
.

This allows us to conclude that
\delta b
is exact on
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, so
\delta b=d\xi
for some function
\xi:S^{1}\times D^{2}\to i\mathbb{R}
. By cutting off
\xi
outside
B(0,2/3)
, we extend
\xi
to the whole manifold
\widehat{Y}
(by zero outside of
\im\iota
). Finally, replace
\gamma_{1}
by
e^{-\xi}\cdot\gamma_{1}
.

It remains to show that
\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{2}
along the core
S^{1}\times\{0\}
up to an overall phase
e^{i\theta}\in S^{1}
when
\delta b=0
on
S^{1}\times B(0,1/2)
. Let

\Psi_{1,0},\Psi_{2,0}

be their restriction along the core
S^{1}\times\{0\}
. If they do not generate the same complex plane in
\Gamma(S^{1}\times\{0\},S)
, then we can always find a section
\Upsilon_{0}\in\Gamma(S^{1}\times\{0\},S)
such that

\Psi_{1,0}\perp\Upsilon_{0}\text{ and }\Psi_{2,0}\not\perp\Upsilon_{0}

or the other way around. Extending
\Upsilon_{0}
to a section
\Upsilon
of

\mathbb{S}\to(\mathbb{R}/2\pi\alpha\mathbb{Z})\times S^{1}\times D^{2},

supported near
\{r_{\nu}(b_{1})\}\times S^{1}\times\{0\}
will result in a function
q_{\Upsilon}
of class
(B3)
that separates
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
.

When
\Psi_{1,0}
and
\Psi_{2,0}
do generate the same complex plane, but
\|\Psi_{1,0}\|_{L^{2}(S^{1})}\neq\|\Psi_{2,0}\|_{L^{2}(S^{1})}
, one can construct
\Upsilon
in a similar way.

We obtain the function
\theta:B(0,1/3)\to\mathbb{R}
, by applying the same argument to the fiber
S^{1}\times\{z\}
for any
z\in B(0,1/3)
.
∎

Hence, it is necessary to take into account all possible embeddings of
S^{1}\times D^{2}
into
\widehat{Y}
in order to obtain the desired separating property:

Proposition 8.4
.

Recall that
Y=\{s\leq 0\}\subset\widehat{Y}
. Let

\Cylin(Y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigcup_{\im\iota\subset Y}\Cylin(\iota)

be the union of all possible cylinder functions with
\im\iota\subset Y
. If
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})
can not be separated by any element in
\Cylin(Y)
, then there is a gauge transformation
v\in\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y})
that identifies
\gamma_{1}
with
\gamma_{2}
over
Y
, i.e.

v(\gamma_{1})=\gamma_{2}\text{ on }Y.

Proof.

Again, take
(b_{i},\psi_{i})=(B_{i},\Psi_{i})-(B_{0},\Psi_{0})
and set

\delta b=b_{2}-b_{1}.

By the proof of Proposition
8.3
, we deduce that
\delta b
is closed over
Y
, and there is a gauge transformation
v\in\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y})
such that
v(B_{1})=B_{2}
. The remaining step is to verify

v\cdot\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}

up to a global constant
e^{i\theta}\in S^{1}
. By Proposition
8.3
, the equality
|\Phi_{1}|=|\Phi_{2}|
holds point-wise on
Y
, and

e^{i\theta(y)}v\cdot\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}

for some function
\theta:Y^{\circ}\to\mathbb{R}
defined in the interior of
Y
. Suppose for some
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y^{\circ}
,
\Phi_{1}(y_{1}),\Phi_{1}(y_{2})\neq 0
. Choose an embedding
S^{1}\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow Y
that passes
y_{1},\ y_{2}
and extend it into an embedding of the solid torus:

\iota:S^{1}\times D^{2}\to Y\subset\widehat{Y}.

By Proposition
8.3
, the function
e^{i\theta}
has to be constant along the core
S^{1}\times\{0\}
, so
e^{i\theta(y_{1})}=e^{i\theta(y_{2})}
. This allows us to modify
\theta
to be a constant function
\theta\equiv\theta_{0}
, so

e^{i\theta_{0}}v\cdot\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{2}.\qed

Now we state the infinitesimal version of Proposition
8.3
and
8.4
concerning the separating property of tangent vectors. They are essential for the proof of transversality in Section
16
. Proposition
8.6
is a direct consequence of Proposition
8.5
, so we focus on the proof of the latter.

Proposition 8.5
.

Take
\gamma=(B,\Psi)\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})
and
V=(\delta b,\delta\psi)\in T_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})
. For a fixed embedding
\iota:S^{1}\times D^{2}\hookrightarrow\widehat{Y}
and any
f\in\Cylin(\iota)
, suppose we always have

df(V)=0,

then either

•

there exists some
\xi\in\Lie(\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y}))
and some function
\theta:B(0,1/3)\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(\delta b,\delta\psi)=(-d\xi,(\xi+i\theta(z))\Psi)

over the smaller solid torus
\iota(S^{1}\times B(0,1/3))
; or

•

\Psi\equiv 0
on
S^{1}\times\{z\}
for some
z\in B(0,1/3)
.

Proposition 8.6
.

Suppose for some
\gamma=(B,\Psi)\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})
and some tangent vector
V\in T_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}(\widehat{Y})
, we always have

df(V)=0

for any
f\in\Cylin(Y)
. Then either

•

\Psi\equiv 0
on
Y
, or

•

for some
\xi\in\Lie(\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y}))
,
V
is generated by the infinitesimal action of
\xi
over
Y
, i.e.

V=(-d\xi,\xi\Psi)\text{ on }Y.

Proof of Proposition
8.5
.

Since
V=(\delta b,\delta\psi)
can not be separated by any functions in classes
(B1)
(B2)
,
\delta b
has to be an exact 1-form on
S^{1}\times D^{2}
, so
\delta b=-d\xi
for some
\xi:S^{1}\times D^{2}\to i\mathbb{R}
. Since this problem is linear and the vector
(-d\xi,\xi\Psi)
can not be separated, it remains to deal with the case when
\delta b=0
and show

\delta\psi=i\theta(z)\Psi

on
S^{1}\times B(0,1/3)
for some function
\theta:B(0,1/3)\to\mathbb{R}
. For a fixed section
\Upsilon
of
\mathbb{S}
, consider functions
\sigma,\sigma_{1}:D^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
:

\displaystyle\sigma(z)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{S^{1}\times\{z\}}\langle\Psi_{z},\Upsilon^{\dagger}_{z}\rangle,\ \sigma_{1}(z)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int_{S^{1}\times\{z\}}\langle\delta\psi_{z},\Upsilon^{\dagger}_{z}\rangle.

Then the differential of
q_{\Upsilon}
along
V=(0,\delta\psi)
can be computed directly as

dq_{\Upsilon}(0,\delta\psi)=\int_{D^{2}}2\chi_{2}(z)\chi_{3}^{\prime}(|\sigma_{1}|^{2})\re(\sigma(z)\overline{\sigma_{1}}(z))dvol_{D^{2}},

where
\chi_{3}
is the cut-off function used to define the
S^{1}
-invariant function
h
in
(B3)
. For any
z\in B(0,1/3)
, if
\Psi_{z}
and
\delta\psi_{z}
do not lie in the same complex direction in
\Gamma(S^{1}\times\{z\},S)
, then for some section
\Upsilon^{\dagger}_{z}\in\Gamma(\{r_{\nu}(b)\}\times S^{1}\times\{z\},S)
,
\re(\sigma(z)\overline{\sigma_{1}}(z))
is non-zero (it suffices to verify this statement for two vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
). By properly extending
\Upsilon^{\dagger}_{z}
to a section
\Upsilon
of
\mathbb{S}
, we can make
dq_{\Upsilon}(0,\delta\psi)\neq 0
.

Finally, if
\Psi_{z}\not\equiv 0
and
\delta\psi_{z}=w\Psi_{z}
for some
w\in\mathbb{C}
, then
w
has to be imaginary for the same reason. This proves the existence of
\theta(z)\in\mathbb{R}
when
\Psi_{z}\not\equiv 0
.
∎

8.3.
Estimates of Perturbations on Cylinders

In this subsection, we take up the proof of Theorem
8.2
. Unlike the case of closed 3-manifolds (cf.
[
KM07
, Section 11.3]
), gradients and Hessians of
f
