, then a solution
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b})
has to reducible globally, which is absurd. So the condition of Lemma
13.5
is fulfilled.
∎

Proof of Lemma
13.5
.

Consider a smooth function
\xi\in L^{2}_{k+1}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R})
and the section

V_{\xi}=(0,\bd_{\gamma}\xi)\in L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S).

Since
e^{r\xi}\cdot\gamma
also solves the equation
\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}}=0
for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
, taking the derivatives yields

\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}}(\bd_{\gamma}\xi)=0,

so the vector

(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}})V_{\xi}=(\bd_{\gamma}^{*}\bd_{\gamma}\xi,0,0)\in L^{2}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S)

is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to
U
. Since the composition
\bd_{\gamma}^{*}\bd_{\gamma}:L^{2}_{2}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R})\to L^{2}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R})
is an invertible operator and
L^{2}_{k+1}
is dense in
L^{2}_{2}
,
\delta c^{\prime}(t)=0
. Now (
13.6
) is reduced to a pair of equations:

(13.7)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}^{*}(\delta b^{\prime}(t),\delta\psi^{\prime}(t)),

(13.8)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}(\delta b^{\prime}(t),\delta\psi^{\prime}(t))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{\check{\gamma}(t)}\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\delta b^{\prime}(t),\delta\psi^{\prime}(t)).

For the second clause of Lemma
13.5
, suppose on the contrary that
U(t)
can not be separated for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
. By Proposition
8.6
, we can find a function
\xi(t)\in L^{2}_{1}(\widehat{Y},i\mathbb{R})
such that

(\delta b^{\prime}(t),\delta\psi^{\prime}(t))=\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}\xi(t)=(-d_{\widehat{Y}}\xi(t),\xi(t)\Psi(t))\text{ on }\{t\}\times Y

for each
t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
. If we write
\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
as

(\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{0},\grad^{1}\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega})\in L^{2}(\widehat{Y},iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S),

then

\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(u\cdot\check{\gamma})=(\grad^{0}\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}),u\cdot\grad^{1}\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma})).

In particular,

\mathcal{D}_{\check{\gamma}(t)}\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}\xi(t))=(0,\xi(t)\cdot\grad^{1}\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}(t)).

Even though
\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}\xi(t)
and
(\delta b^{\prime}(t),\delta\psi^{\prime}(t))
only agree over
\{t\}\times Y
, we still have

\mathcal{D}_{\check{\gamma}(t)}\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\delta b^{\prime}(t),\delta\psi^{\prime}(t))=(0,\xi(t)\cdot\grad^{1}\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\check{\gamma}(t))\text{ on }\{t\}\times Y,

since the perturbation
\mathfrak{q}
is supported on
Y
in the sense of Definition
7.1
. The equation (
13.8
) then implies

\frac{d}{dt}\delta b^{\prime}\equiv 0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}_{t}\times Y.

As
U\in L^{2}
,
-d_{\widehat{Y}}\xi(t)=\delta b^{\prime}(t)\equiv 0
. Now the equation (
13.7
) yields

0=\Delta_{\widehat{Y}}\xi(t)+|\Psi(t)|^{2}\xi(t)=|\Psi(t)|^{2}\xi(t)\text{ on }\{t\}\times Y,

As a result,
U\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times Y
. An elliptic operator of the form (
13.6
) satisfies the unique continuation property, so
U\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
.
∎

13.3.
Spectral Projections

Having discussed the linearized operator on an infinite cylinder
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
, we start to look at a finite interval
I=[t_{1},t_{2}]\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
and consider the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer boundary-value problems. As noted in the beginning of Section
13
, we have to justify that the proof of gluing theorem in
[
KM07
, Section 18,19]
remains valid in our case, in the presence of essential spectra. This subsection is devoted to an abstract formalism, while the application in gauge theory will be explained in Subsection
13.4
. However, the results in these subsections will
not
be used elsewhere in this paper.

As we will work in a slightly abstract setting, define

E_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S\to\widehat{Y}

Take a reference operator
\mathbb{A}_{0}
that acts on sections of
E_{0}
, extending to bounded linear operators

\mathbb{A}_{0}:L^{2}_{j}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})\to L^{2}_{j-1}(\widehat{Y},E_{0}).

for any
j\geq 1
. Moreover, assume that
\mathbb{A}_{0}
is a unbounded self-adjoint operators on
L^{2}
and its spectrum is disjoint from the interval
(-\lambda_{1}/2,\lambda_{1}/2)
:

(13.9)

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathbb{A}_{0})

\displaystyle\subset(-\infty,-\lambda_{1}/2]\cap[\lambda_{1}/2,\infty)\text{ with }

\displaystyle\sigma_{ess}(\mathbb{A}_{0})

\displaystyle=(-\infty,-\lambda_{1}]\cap[\lambda_{1},\infty),

for some
\lambda_{1}>0
as in Proposition
12.1
. One may think of
\mathbb{A}_{0}
as a first-order self-adjoint elliptic differential operator plus a compact perturbation. For convenience, suppose the
L^{2}_{j}
-norm on
C^{\infty}_{c}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
is defined using
\mathbb{A}_{0}
:

\|s\|_{L^{2}_{j}(E_{0})}=\|(1+|\mathbb{A}_{0}|)^{j}s\|_{L^{2}(E_{0})},\forall s\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{Y},E_{0}).

Let
K:C^{\infty}_{c}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})\to C^{\infty}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
be an operator acting on sections of
E_{0}
extending to a compact operator:

K:L^{2}_{j}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})\to L^{2}_{j}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})

for any
j\geq 0
. Assume that
K
is self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
. When the sum
\mathbb{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{A}_{0}+K
is invertible,
L^{2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
is the direct sum of the positive and negative spectral spaces of
\mathbb{A}
:

L^{2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})=H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}}\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}},

and for any
j\geq 0
,

(13.10)

L^{2}_{j}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})=(H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}}\cap L^{2}_{j})\oplus(H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}}\cap L^{2}_{j}).

Let
E\to\widehat{Z}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(-\infty,0]\times\widehat{Y}
be the pull-back bundle of
E_{0}
over the half cylinder and consider the operator:

D_{\mathbb{A}}=\frac{d}{dt}+\mathbb{A}:C^{\infty}(\widehat{Z},E)\to C^{\infty}(\widehat{Z},E).

The next result is a direct consequence of Functional Calculus, cf.
[
KM07
, Theorem 17.1.4]
.

Proposition 13.7
.

Let
\widehat{Z}=(-\infty,0]\times\widehat{Y}
be the half cylinder. Suppose the operator

\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}_{0}+K:L^{2}_{1}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})\to L^{2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})

is invertible, then the operator

D_{\mathbb{A}}\oplus\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}}\circ r:L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{Z},E)\oplus(H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0}))

is also invertible for any
k\geq 1
, where
r:L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E)
is the restriction map at the boundary
\{0\}\times\widehat{Y}
and

\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}^{-}:L^{2}_{k-1/2}\to H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})

is the spectral projection. The subspace
H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}
is precisely the image of
\ker D_{\mathbb{A}}
under
r
.

As
\mathbb{A}
differs from
\mathbb{A}_{0}
only by a compact operator, it is expected that
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}^{-}
forms a “compact” family as
\mathbb{A}
varies. We make this precise in the next proposition.

Proposition 13.8
.

Given an invertible operator
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}_{0}+K
, the difference of their spectral projections

\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}^{-}-\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-}:L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})\to L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})

is compact for any
k\geq 1
, i.e.
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}
and
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}
are
k
-commensurate in the sense of
[
KM07
, Definition 17.2.1]
.

Proof.

We follow the trick from
[
KM07
, Proposition 17.2.4]
. It suffices to show for any bounded sequence
\{w_{i}\}\subset L^{2}_{k-1/2}
, it image under
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}^{-}-\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-}
contains a converging subsequence. In terms of the decomposition (
13.10
), we can deal with entries of
\{w_{i}\}
separately. By the symmetry of
H^{\pm}_{\mathbb{A}}
, we focus on the case when
\{w_{i}\}\subset H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}
. By Proposition
13.7
, there exists sections
\{v_{i}\}\subset L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)
such that

D_{\mathbb{A}}v_{i}=0\text{ and }r(v_{i})=w_{i}.

Apply Proposition
13.7
again for
\mathbb{A}_{0}
to find solutions
\{u_{i}\}\subset L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)
with

D_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}u_{i}=-K(v_{i})\text{ and }\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-}\circ r(u_{i})=0.

Since
D_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}(u_{i}-v_{i})=0
,
r(u_{i}-v_{i})\in H_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-}
. So

\displaystyle(\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}^{-}-\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-})(w_{i})=(1-\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-})(w_{i})=\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}\circ r(v_{i})=\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}\circ r(u_{i}).

One may write the last term explicitly in terms of
v_{i}
using formulae on
[
KM07
, P.299]
:

(13.11)

v_{i}\mapsto\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}\circ r(u_{i})=y_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\int^{0}_{-\infty}e^{t\mathbb{A}_{0}}(-K(v_{i}(t)))^{+}dt.

where
(\cdot)^{+}
denotes the positive part in
H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}
. As this point, approximate
K
by finite rank operators. The operator
v\mapsto y
defined by the expression (
13.11
) is also approximated by finite rank operators in the norm topology, so (
13.11
) is also compact.

Here is the main difference of this proof from that of
[
KM07
, Proposition 17.2.4]
: the operator

v\mapsto\int^{0}_{-\infty}e^{t\mathbb{A}_{0}}(v(t))^{+}dt,\ L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0}),

is not compact as
\mathbb{A}_{0}
has essential spectrum, so the compactness of
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}^{-}-\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-}
really arises from
K
.
∎

With Proposition
13.8
in mind, we are ready to study the boundary value problem on a finite interval.

Proposition 13.9
.

Let
I=[t_{1},t_{2}]_{t}
be a finite interval and
\widehat{Z}=I\times\widehat{Y}
. Given invertible operators
\mathbb{A}_{i}=\mathbb{A}_{0}+K_{i},i=1,2
as compact perturbations of
\mathbb{A}_{0}
, consider the operator

D=\frac{d}{dt}+\mathbb{A}_{0}+K(t):L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{Z},E)

on
\widehat{Z}
and spectral projections

\displaystyle\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}\circ r_{1}

\displaystyle:L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\{t_{1}\}\times\widehat{Y},E_{0}),

\displaystyle\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\circ r_{2}

\displaystyle:L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\{t_{2}\}\times\widehat{Y},E_{0}).

where
K:I\to\Hom(L^{2}_{j},L^{2}_{j}),j\geq 0
is a smooth family of self-adjoint compact operators. Then the operator

P\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D\oplus(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2})

is Fredholm, whose index is equal to the spectrum flow from
\mathbb{A}_{1}
to
\mathbb{A}_{2}
. In particular, the restriction map on the kernel of
D
:

(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2}):\ker D\to H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})

is Fredholm of the same index.

In the sequel, we will abbreviate
H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
into
H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}}
when the regularity of sections is clear from the context.

Proof.

We start with the model case when
K_{1}=K_{2}=K(t)\equiv 0
. The operator

P_{0}=D_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\oplus(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\circ r_{1}\oplus\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\circ r_{2})

is then invertible by direct computation using Functional Calculus. For the general case, note that
D-D_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}
is a compact operator. As for the boundary projections, Proposition
13.8
implies that

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}:H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}

\displaystyle\to H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}^{+}:H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}

\displaystyle\to H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}},

are mutual inverses modulo compact operators, which also holds for the negative projections
\{\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-},\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}^{-}\}
. To compute the index, we use the concatenation trick and compare
P
with the operator on the infinite cylinder:

\frac{d}{dt}+\mathbb{A}_{0}+K^{\prime}(t):L^{2}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y},E)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y},E).

where
K^{\prime}
is a smooth path of compact operators connecting
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
:

K‘(t)\equiv K_{1}\text{ if }t\leq t_{1};\ K’(t)\equiv K_{2}\text{ if }t\geq t_{2}.

Now apply Proposition
13.1
or Theorem
10.2
. If we write
L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)
as a direct sum

C\oplus\ker D

where
C
is the
L^{2}_{k}
-orthogonal complement of
\ker D
, then
P
is cast into a lower triangular metric

(13.12)

\begin{pmatrix}D&0\\
*&(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2})\end{pmatrix}.

As
D|_{C}
is a bijection by
[
KM07
, Proposition 17.1.5]
and the unique continuation property, the other diagonal entry has to be Fredholm of the same index as that of
P
.
∎

Remark 13.10
.

Here is a major difference of our case from
[
KM07
, Proposition 17.2.5]
: the projection map onto the complementary spectral subspaces:

(\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}},\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2}):\ker D\to H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}\oplus H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}

is not compact. To see this, consider the model case when
\mathbb{A}_{1}=\mathbb{A}_{2}=\mathbb{A}_{0}
and
K(t)\equiv 0
, so
\ker D
is parametrized by the image of
(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2})
. Sticking to the positive part, the composition map

\displaystyle H_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\{t_{1}\}\times\widehat{Y},E_{0})

\displaystyle\to H_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\{t_{2}\}\times\widehat{Y},E_{0})

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\mapsto v\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}P^{-1}(0,w,0)\in\ker D

\displaystyle\mapsto\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\circ r_{2}(v).

is simply
e^{-\mathbb{A}_{0}(t_{2}-t_{1})}
acting on
H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}
which has essential spectrum
[0,e^{-\lambda_{1}(t_{2}-t_{1})}]
. As a result, it is never compact.
∎

To circumvent this problem, we have to refine the estimates when the 3-manifold
\widehat{Y}
is not compact. Recall that a Fredholm operator
P
is invertible modulo compact operators. A right (left) parametrix
Q
is a right (left) inverse of
P
modulo compact operators, i.e.

PQ=\Id+\text{ a compact term}.

Such a
Q
is unique up to a compact term and is also a (two-sided) parametrix.

The difference up to a compact term is always insignificant. This motivates the next definition and lemma:

Lemma 13.11
.

Let
H_{i},\ i=1,2
be Hilbert spaces. For any operator
Q:H_{2}\to H_{1}
, define its essential norm as

\|Q\|_{ess}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\inf_{K\text{ compact }}\|Q+K\|_{H_{2}\to H_{1}}.

For any Fredholm operator
P:H_{1}\to H_{2}
with a parametrix
Q
, the perturbed operator
P+F
is Fredholm if
\|FQ\|_{ess}<1
.

Proof.

As
(P+F)Q
and
Q
are Fredholm,
P+F
is Fredholm as well.
∎

Now let us recast Proposition
13.9
into a more convenient form for applications. Recall that the essential spectrum of
\mathbb{A}_{0}
is away from the origin:

\sigma_{ess}(\mathbb{A}_{0})=(-\infty,\lambda_{1}]\cup[\lambda_{1},\infty),

for some
\lambda_{1}>0
.

Proposition 13.12
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
13.9
, the operator
P
is Fredholm. The essential norm of its parametrix
\widetilde{Q}
is bounded by a constant
C_{1}
that depends only on
\lambda_{1}
. The same conclusion applies to the projection map

(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2}):\ker D\to H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\{t_{1}\}\times\widehat{Y},E_{0})\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\{t_{2}\}\times\widehat{Y}\,E_{0}).

and its parametrix
Q
. Moreover, the essential norm of the complementary projection pre-composed with
Q
:

(\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2})\circ Q:H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\xrightarrow{Q}\ker D\to H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\oplus H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}

is bounded above by
e^{-\lambda_{1}|I|}
, where
|I|=|t_{2}-t_{1}|
is the length of
I
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into four steps:

Step
1. Estimate
\widetilde{Q}
. When
K_{1}=K_{2}=K(t)\equiv 0
, we obtain the model operator

P_{0}=D_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\oplus(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\circ r_{1}\oplus\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\circ r_{2}):L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{Z},E)\oplus(H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}).

Let
\widetilde{Q}_{0}=(R,Q_{0})
be the inverse of
P_{0}
with

\displaystyle Q_{0}:H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}

\displaystyle\to L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E),

\displaystyle R:L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{Z},E)

\displaystyle\to L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E).

The norm
\|\widetilde{Q}_{0}\|
is bounded by a constant
C_{1}
independent of the length
|I|
. In the general case, set
\widetilde{Q}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(R,Q_{0}\circ(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}))
with

(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}):H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\to H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}.

Then
\|\widetilde{Q}\|\leq\|\widetilde{Q}_{0}\|
, since we have used
\mathbb{A}_{0}
to define the
L^{2}_{j}
-norm on
C_{c}^{\infty}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
. By Proposition
13.8
, projection maps:

\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{i}}^{\pm}:H^{\pm}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\to H^{\pm}_{\mathbb{A}_{i}},\ \Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{\pm}:H^{\pm}_{\mathbb{A}_{i}}\to H^{\pm}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},i=1,2

are mutual inverses modulo compact operators; so
\widetilde{Q}
is a parametrix of
P
.

Step
2. Estimate
Q
. Using the block form (
13.12
), we write
\widetilde{Q}
as a 2 by 2 matrix:

\begin{pmatrix}Q_{11}&Q_{12}\\
Q_{21}&Q_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

Take
Q\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q_{22}
to be the bottom right entry, then

Q:H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}\oplus H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\to\ker D

is a left parametrix of
(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2})
and

\|Q\|\leq\|\widetilde{Q}\|,

since
C
is
L^{2}_{k}
-orthogonal to
\ker D
in (
13.12
).

Step
3. Estimate the complementary projection.
It suffices to estimate the norm of

M\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2})\circ Q.

First of all, the estimate holds for the model case when
\mathbb{A}_{1}=\mathbb{A}_{2}=\mathbb{A}_{0}
and
K(t)\equiv 0
, by Remark
13.10
. Define

M_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2})\circ Q_{0}.

Now we allow
K(t)\neq 0
, but
\mathbb{A}_{1}=\mathbb{A}_{2}=\mathbb{A}_{0}
. Write
Q^{\prime}
for the parametrix constructed in
Step
2. We have to compare

M^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}},\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}})\circ(r_{1},r_{2})\circ Q^{\prime}.

with the model operator
M_{0}
, and show the difference
M-M_{0}
is compact.

For any
(w_{1},w_{2})\in H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}
, sections
u\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q^{\prime}(w)
and
v\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Q_{0}(w)
obey the following equations respectively:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}D_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}(u)&=-K(t)u\\
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}\circ r_{2}(u)&=w_{1}-k_{1}(w),\\
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-}\circ r_{2}(u)&=w_{2}-k_{2}(w),\end{array}\right.\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}D_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}(v)&=0,\\
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}\circ r_{2}(v)&=w_{1},\\
\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-}\circ r_{2}(v)&=w_{2},\end{array}\right.

where
(k_{1},k_{2})
is a compact operator acting on
H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}\oplus H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}
. It follows that

w\mapsto(Q^{\prime}-Q_{0})(w)=u-v=P_{0}^{-1}(-K(t)Q^{\prime}(w),-k_{1}(w),-k_{2}(w))

is a compact operator.

Step
4. In the most general case, we allow
K(t)\neq 0
and
\mathbb{A}_{1},\mathbb{A}_{2}\neq\mathbb{A}_{0}
. Recall that
Q=Q^{\prime}\circ(\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+},\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-})
, so
M=M^{\prime}\circ(\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+},\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{-})
and

\|M\|_{ess}\leq\|M^{\prime}\|_{ess}=\|M_{0}\|_{ess}\leq e^{-\lambda_{1}|I|}.\qed

Spectral projections are not the most relevant boundary conditions for the main applications in gauge theory, although they serve important intermediate steps.

Proposition 13.13
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
13.7
with
\widehat{Z}=(-\infty,0]\times\widehat{Y}
, suppose
\Pi_{1}
is any linear projection on
L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
whose kernel is a complement of
H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}}
:

(13.13)

\ker(\Pi_{1})\oplus(H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0}))=L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0}).

and let
H_{1}^{-}
be the image of
\Pi_{1}
. Then the operator

D_{\mathbb{A}}\oplus\Pi_{1}\circ r:L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{Z},E)\oplus H_{1}^{-}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

See
[
KM07
, Proposition 17.2.6]
or
[
KM07
, P.340-341]
.
∎

Proposition 13.14

(cf.
[
KM07
]
Proposition 17.2.6)
.

Under the assumption of Proposition
13.12
with
\widehat{Z}=I\times\widehat{Y}
and
I=[t_{1},t_{2}]
, suppose
\Pi_{1}^{+}
and
\Pi_{2}^{-}
are any linear projections on
L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
whose kernels are complements of
H^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}
and
H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}
respectively, i.e. (
13.13
) holds for
(\Pi_{1}^{+},H_{\mathbb{A}_{1}}^{+})
and
(\Pi_{2}^{-},H_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}^{-})
. Let
H_{1}^{-}
and
H_{2}^{+}
be images of
\Pi_{1}^{+}
and
\Pi_{2}^{-}
respectively. Then there exists a constant
T_{0}(\Pi_{1}^{+},\Pi_{2}^{-})>0
such that the operator

D\oplus(\Pi_{1}^{+},\Pi_{2}^{-})\circ(r_{1},r_{2}):L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},E)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{Z},E)\oplus H_{1}^{+}\oplus H_{2}^{-},

is Fredholm when
|I|>T_{0}
.

Proof.

There are two ways to proceed. In the first approach, one may use Proposition
13.13
to construct a parametrix of
D\oplus(\Pi_{1}^{+},\Pi_{2}^{-})
; see Proposition
14.1
below. In the second approach, we use the estimate on essential operator norms from Proposition
13.12
. It suffices to show the restriction map

(\Pi_{1}^{+},\Pi_{2}^{-})\circ(r_{1},r_{2}):\ker D\to H_{1}^{+}\oplus H_{2}^{-}

is Fredholm. We focus on
H_{2}^{+}
and pretend the other boundary does not exist. Write

\Pi^{-}_{2}=\Pi^{-}_{2}\circ\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}+\Pi^{-}_{2}\circ(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}-\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+})+\Pi_{2}^{-}\circ\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}.

The middle term is compact. Since
\Pi_{2}^{-}:H_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}^{-}\to H_{2}^{-}
is an isomorphism of Hilbert spaces, by Proposition
13.12
,

\Pi_{2}^{-}\circ\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}^{-}\circ r_{2}:\ker D\to H_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}^{-}\xrightarrow{\Pi_{2}^{-}}H_{2}^{-}

is Fredholm with parametrix
Q\circ(\Pi_{2}^{-})^{-1}
. To apply Lemma
13.11
, we have to estimate the essential norm of

(\Pi_{2}^{-}\circ\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+})\circ(Q\circ(\Pi_{2}^{-})^{-1})=\Pi_{2}^{-}\circ(\Pi_{\mathbb{A}_{0}}^{+}\circ Q)\circ(\Pi_{2}^{-})^{-1},

which is bounded above by
C(\Pi_{2}^{-})\cdot e^{-\lambda_{1}|I|}<1
if
|I|\gg 1
. The constant
C(\Pi_{2}^{-})
depends only on the operator norms of

\Pi_{2}^{-}:H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\to H_{2}^{-}\text{ and }(\Pi_{2}^{-})^{-1}:H_{2}^{-}\to H^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}.\qed

13.4.
Applications in Gauge Theory

Having developed the abstract theory in Subsection
13.3
, let us explain now how various operators are defined in gauge theory. For each tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}
and a configuration
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, consider the extended Hessian

\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}},

The reference operator
\mathbb{A}_{0}
is taken to be a compact perturbation of
\tilde{\mathbb{A}}
such that the conditon (
13.9
) holds.

Recall that the space
L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})
admits a decomposition for each
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
:

\displaystyle L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})

\displaystyle=L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},i\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathcal{T}_{k-1/2,\mathfrak{a}},

\displaystyle=L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},i\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathcal{J}_{k-1/2,\mathfrak{a}}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{k-1/2,\mathfrak{a}},

on which
\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}}
takes a block form:

\begin{pmatrix}0&\bd_{\mathfrak{a}}^{*}&0\\
\bd_{\mathfrak{a}}&0&0\\
0&0&\Hess_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&y&x\\
0&x^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}

The operators
x,y
are defined as in (
12.1
) and they are compact. Denote the first matrix by
\mathbb{A}
and consider its spectral decomposition:

\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}^{\pm}:L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})\to H^{\pm}_{\mathbb{A}}.

As
\Hess_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}}
acts on
\mathcal{K}_{k-1/2,\mathfrak{a}}
, we also have the spectral decomposition of
\Hess_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}}
:

\mathcal{K}_{k-1/2,\mathfrak{a}}=\mathcal{K}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{+}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{-}.

Define subspaces:

H^{\pm}_{\mathfrak{a}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},i\mathbb{R})\oplus\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\pm}\subset L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0}),

and the projection maps

\Pi_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\pm}:L^{2}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},E_{0})\to H^{\pm}_{\mathfrak{a}},

whose kernels are

\{0\}\oplus\mathcal{J}_{k-1/2,\mathfrak{a}}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\mp}.

The pairs
(\Pi_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\pm},\Pi_{\mathbb{A}}^{\pm})
that satisfy the condition (
13.13
), cf.
[
KM07
, P.316]
. By Proposition
13.14
, the first statement of
[
KM07
, Theorem 17.3.2]
continues to hold in our case, and the proof the gluing theorem from
[
KM07
, Section 17-19]
remains valid. Proposition
13.12
is the replacement of
[
KM07
, Proposition 17.2.5]
in the presence of essential spectra.

Remark 13.15
.

In practice, we will take
\mathfrak{q}
to be an admissible perturbation and
\mathfrak{a}
to be a non-degenerate critical point of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
, in which case
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}=\mathbb{A}
. Moreover,
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
has only finitely many critical points by the compactness theorem. Since only finitely many configurations are involved in the gluing theorem, we have a uniform upper bound on the constant
T_{0}
in Proposition
13.14
, so it does not cause a problem.
∎

Finally, let us compute the spectrum flow from
\Hess_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}}
to
\Hess_{\mathfrak{q},u\cdot\mathfrak{a}}
as an application of Proposition
13.9
.

Lemma 13.16

(cf.
[
KM07
]
Lemma 14.4.6)
.

Consider the cylinder
\widehat{Z}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and the operator
(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}})
defined in Proposition
13.1
with
\mathfrak{b}=u\cdot\mathfrak{a}
and
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
, then

\Ind(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}})=([u]\cup c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))[Y,\partial Y]\in 2\mathbb{Z},\ \forall\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a},u\cdot\mathfrak{a}).

Proof.

We may use Proposition
13.9
and
[
KM07
, Proposition 14.2.2]
to identify this index to the index of an operator on
S^{1}\times\widehat{Y}
. The spin bundle
S^{+}\to S^{1}\times\widehat{Y}
is constructed as

[0,1]\times S/(0,v)\sim(1,u\cdot v).

Using the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index theorem
[
APS75
, Theorem 3.10]
instead, the proof of
[
KM07
, Lemma 14.4.6]
can now proceed with no difficulty. Indeed, over the cylindrical end of
S^{1}\times\widehat{Y}
, the operator is cast into the form (up to a compact term)

\partial_{t}+\sigma(\partial_{s}+D_{\Sigma})=\sigma(\partial_{s}-\sigma\cdot\partial_{t}+D_{\Sigma})\text{ on }S^{1}\times[0,+\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma.

Following the proof of Proposition
11.2
, the spectrum of
(-\sigma\cdot\partial_{t}+D_{\Sigma})
on
S^{1}\times\Sigma
is discrete and symmetric with respect to the origin, so its
\eta
-invariant is zero. Moreover,
(-\sigma\cdot\partial_{t}+D_{\Sigma})
is invertible, so its kernel is trivial.
∎

14.
Linearized Operators on Cobordisms

Having addressed the linearized operators on the product manifold
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
, in this section, we explore the case for a morphism
\mathbb{X}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
in the strict cobordism category
\SCob_{s}
. In this case, we have a relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism

(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}).

By attaching cylindrical ends, we obtain a complete Riemannian manifold

\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigg((-\infty,-1]_{t}\times\widehat{Y}_{1}\bigg)\cup\widehat{X}\cup\bigg([1,\infty)_{t}\times\widehat{Y}_{2}\bigg)

together with a closed 2-form
\omega_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
defined as in (
3.11
). There are two main tasks for this section:

•

define the perturbation space of the Seiberg-Witten equaions on
\mathcal{X}
. This is crucial for the transversality result in Section
16
, cf. Theorem
16.5
;

•

prove that the linearized operator on
\mathcal{X}
is Fredholm.

They are addressed in Subsection
14.1
and
14.2
respectively.

14.1.
Perturbations

Given a morphism
\mathbb{X}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
in the strict
spin^{c}\
cobordism category
\SCob_{s}
, the perturbation
\mathfrak{q}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(Y_{i})
encoded in the definition of
(\mathbb{Y}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})
is admissible by
(P7)
. Take a critial point

\mathfrak{a}_{i}\in\Crit(\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{i},\widehat{Y}_{i}})\subset\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}),

for each
i=1,2
. Pick a smooth configuration
\gamma
on
\mathcal{X}
such that

(14.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\check{\gamma}(t)&\equiv\mathfrak{a}_{1}\text{ if }t<-1/2;\\
\check{\gamma}(t)&\equiv\mathfrak{a}_{2}\text{ if }t>1/2\\
\gamma(t)&\text{is in the temporal gauge when }|t|>1/2,\\
\gamma\big|_{\widehat{X}}&\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}).\end{array}\right.

Now consider the configuration space on
\mathcal{X}
:

\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{(A,\Phi)=\gamma_{0}+(a,\phi):(a,\phi)\in L^{2}_{k}(\mathcal{X},iT^{*}\mathcal{X}\oplus S^{+})\}.

and the gauge group

\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\mathcal{X})=\{u:\mathcal{X}\to S^{1}:u-1\in L^{2}_{k+1}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{C})\}.

The linearized action of
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\mathcal{X})
at
\gamma=(A,\Phi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
is given by:

\displaystyle\bd_{\gamma}:L^{2}_{k+1}(\mathcal{X},i\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\to T_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle\mapsto(-df,f\Phi)

whose
L^{2}
-formal adjoint is

\displaystyle\bd_{\gamma}^{*}:T_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\mathcal{X},i\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle(\delta a,\delta\phi)

\displaystyle\mapsto-d^{*}a+i\re\langle\delta\phi,i\Phi\rangle.

Let us now specify the class of perturbations involved in the Seiberg-Witten equations. Choose a cut-off function
\beta:\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\beta(t)\equiv 1
if
|t|>3
and
\beta(t)\equiv 0
if
|t|<2
. Pick another cut-off function
\beta_{0}:\mathbb{R}_{t}\to\mathbb{R}
supported on
[1,2]_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
, equal to
1
when
t\in[5/4,7/4]
. Now consider the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equation:

(14.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=0,\ \gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}}(\gamma)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X}}(\gamma)+\beta(t)[\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}(\gamma)+\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}_{2}(\gamma)]+\beta_{0}(t)(\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}_{3}(\gamma))+(\rho_{4}(\omega_{3}^{+}),0),

where
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X}}
is the unperturbed Seiberg-Witten map defined by the formula (
3.7
). Here
\mathfrak{p}
denotes the quadruple

\mathfrak{p}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2},\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3})\in\mathcal{P}(Y_{1})\times\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})\times\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})\times\Omega^{2}_{c}([1,2]\times Y_{2},i\mathbb{R}).

where
\mathfrak{q}_{3}\in\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})
is a tame perturbation supported on
Y_{2}
and
\omega_{3}
is an imaginary-valued exact 2-form compactly supported on
[1,2]\times Y_{2}
. The effect of
\omega_{3}
is to deform
\omega_{X}
into
\omega_{X}-\omega_{3}
, so the first equation of (
3.7
) is changed into

\frac{1}{2}\rho_{4}(F_{A^{t}}^{+}-2\omega^{+}_{X})-(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}=-\rho_{4}(\omega_{3}^{+}),

modulo perturbations from
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
’s. In practice, it suffices to consider
\omega_{3}
in the special form:

(14.3)

\omega_{3}=d_{\mathcal{X}}(\beta_{0}(t)f_{3}dt)=-\beta_{0}(t)dt\wedge d_{Y_{2}}f_{3}.

for a compactly supported smooth function
f_{3}:[1,2]_{t}\times Y_{2}\to i\mathbb{R}
.

Within the space of all compactly supported smooth functions on
[1,2]_{t}\times Y_{2}
, we choose a countable subset that is dense in
C^{\infty}
-topology and form a Banach space as in Theorem
8.17
:

\mathcal{P}_{\form}.

The space
\mathcal{P}_{\form}
is dense in
C^{\infty}_{c}([1,2]_{t}\times Y_{2},i\mathbb{R})
, and we define
\omega_{3}
by the formula (
14.3
) with
f_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{\form}
. In all, the quadruple
\mathfrak{p}
takes value in a Banach space

\mathfrak{p}=(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2},\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3})\in\mathcal{P}(Y_{1})\times\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})\times\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})\times\mathcal{P}_{\form}.

Here
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{2}
are encoded in the cylindrical ends of
\mathcal{X}
; only the last two terms

(\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3})

give rise to the actual perturbation in (
14.2
), allowing us to achieve transversality in Section
16
. Note that

\beta_{0}(t)\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}_{3}(\gamma)\text{ and }(\rho_{4}(\omega_{3}^{+}),0)

are both supported in the compact region
[1,2]_{t}\times Y_{2}
. Finally, we form the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
by taking the quotient space:

(14.4)

\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}}(\gamma)=0:\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})\}/\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\mathcal{X}),

which is in fact independent of the subscript
k
, due to the exponential decay of the local energy functional, cf. Theorem
9.3
.

14.2.
Linearized Operators

Similar to the case for
\widehat{Z}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
, the linearization of
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}}
together with
\bd_{\gamma}^{*}
forms a Fredholm operator. In particular, the cokernel is finite dimensional.

Proposition 14.1
.

For any
i=1,2
, let
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
be a smooth non-generate critical point of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{i}}
in
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})
. Then for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
, the operator

(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}}):L^{2}_{k}(\mathcal{X},iT^{*}\mathcal{X}\oplus S^{+})\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\mathcal{X},i\mathbb{R}\oplus i\Lambda^{+}\mathcal{X}\oplus S^{-})

is Fredholm.

Definition 14.2
.

The moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
is called regular, if the operator
(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}})
is surjective at any solution
[\gamma]\in\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
,
∎

Proof of Proposition
14.1
.

It suffices to deal with the case for the reference configuration
\gamma=\gamma_{0}
and when
(\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3})=0
. As
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is non-degenerate, the operator on the infinite cylinder

D_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{d}{dt}+\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{q}_{i},\mathfrak{a}_{i}}:L^{2}_{k}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}_{i},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}_{i}\oplus S)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}_{i},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}_{i}\oplus S)

is invertible for
i=1,2
. Denote the inverse by
Q_{i}
. Unlike Theorem
10.2
, the cut-off functions involved in the parametrix patching argument are more sophisticated, as we explain now. There are three of them:

\beta_{1},\ \beta_{2}\text{ and }\beta_{X}\text{ with }\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\beta_{X}\equiv 1\text{ and }\beta_{X}\text{ compactly supported}

Over the region
\{s\leq 2\}\subset\mathcal{X}
, choose a partition of unity
\{\beta_{1}^{\prime},\beta_{2}^{\prime},\beta_{X}\}
subordinate to the open cover
U_{1}\cup U_{2}\cup U_{X}
:

\begin{overpic}[F2.png]
\put(46.0,15.0){$X$}
\put(-2.0,15.0){$(-\infty,-1]\times\widehat{Y}_{1}$}
\put(72.0,15.0){$[1,\infty)\times\widehat{Y}_{2}$}
\put(16.0,5.0){$t=$}
\put(26.0,5.0){\small$-2$}
\put(36.0,5.0){\small$-1$}
\put(47.0,5.0){\small$0$}
\put(57.0,5.0){\small$1$}
\put(66.0,5.0){\small$2$}
\put(8.0,46.0){\small$s=2$}
\put(15.0,36.0){\small$0$}
\put(12.0,26.0){\small$-2$}
\put(60.0,23.0){\color[rgb]{1,0,0}$U_{X}$}
\put(21.0,39.0){\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}$U_{1}$}
\put(71.0,39.0){\color[rgb]{0,0,1}$U_{2}$}
\end{overpic}

Figure 2
.
An open cover of
\{s\leq 2\}

Over the region
\{s\geq 2\}
,
\beta_{X}\equiv 0
and
\beta_{i}(s,t)=\beta_{i}^{T}(t),i=1,2
where
\{\beta_{1}^{T},\beta_{2}^{T}\}
is a partition of unity on the real line
\mathbb{R}_{t}
subordinate to the cover

\mathbb{R}_{t}=(-\infty,T]\cup[-T,\infty),

such that
|d\beta_{i}^{T}|\leq 4/T
. The value of
\beta_{i}
in the transition area
\{1\leq s\leq 2\}
is filled in by interpolation. To be more precise, pick a partition of unity
\{\alpha^{L},\alpha^{U}\}
on
\mathbb{R}_{s}
such that
\alpha^{U}(s)\equiv 1
when
s\geq 2
and
\alpha^{U}(s)\equiv 0
when
s\leq 1
. Set

\beta_{i}=\alpha^{L}(s)\beta_{i}^{\prime}+\alpha^{U}(s)\beta_{i}^{T}(t),\ i=1,2.

Finally, we take

Q=\tilde{\beta}_{1}Q_{1}\beta_{1}+\tilde{\beta}_{2}Q_{2}\beta_{2}+\tilde{\beta}_{X}Q_{X}\beta_{X},

with
\tilde{\beta}_{i}
constructed in a similar manner. Here we require that
\tilde{\beta}_{i}\equiv 1
on
\text{supp}\ \beta_{i}
so that
\tilde{\beta}_{i}\beta_{i}=\beta_{i}
. The same holds for
(\tilde{\beta}_{X},\beta_{X})
; and also
\text{supp}\ \tilde{\beta}_{X}
is compact.

The parametrix
Q_{X}
is given by a local patching argument as usual. By taking
T\gg 0
, one verifies that
Q
is indeed a parametrix for the operator
(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}})
.
∎

Part V
Transversality

The primary goal of this part is to prove the key transversality result: Theorem
16.1
, which states that admissible perturbations on
(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, in the sense of Definition
13.3
, exist and are in fact generic. Because the perturbation space
\mathcal{P}(Y)
that we consider are supported on the truncated 3-manifold
Y=\{s\leq 0\}
, only a weak separating property is satisfied, cf. Theorem
8.20
. As a result, a stronger unique continuation property is required in order to achieve transversality.

Section
15
is devoted to the proof of unique continuation properties, which uses the Carleman estimates from
[
Kim95
]
. In Section
16
, we prove Theorem
16.1
as well as its analogue for a general morphism
\mathbb{X}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
in the
\SCob_{s}
, cf. Theorem
16.5
.

15.
Unique Continuation

15.1.
Statements

In this section, we prove the unique continuation properties of the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations (
13.1
), which are crucial for the proof of Theorem
16.1
. The main results are listed as follows:

•

the non-linear version: Theorem
15.1
;

•

the linearized version: Theorem
15.2
; and

•

the irreducibility of spinors: Theorem
15.3
.

These theorems are summarized in the first subsection, while the rest of section is devoted to their proofs. Let us start with the non-linear version of unique continuation:

Theorem 15.1
.

Let
I=(t_{1},t_{2})_{t}
be an open finite interval. Consider a tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}
supported on the truncated 3-manifold
Y=\{s\leq 0\}\subset\widehat{Y}
and the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations on
\widehat{Z}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}I\times\widehat{Y}
:

(15.1)

0=\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}}(\gamma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z}}(\gamma)+\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}(\gamma).

If two solutions
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
are gauge equivalent on the slice
\{t_{0}\}\times Y
at some
t_{0}\in I
, i.e there exists a gauge transformation
u\in\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y})
such that

u(\gamma_{1}|_{\{t_{0}\}\times\widehat{Y}})=\gamma_{2}|_{\{t_{0}\}\times\widehat{Y}}\text{ on }Y,

then
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are gauge equivalent over the whole manifold
\widehat{Z}
.

The analogous result for closed 3-manifolds is
[
KM07
, Proposition 7.2.1]
. The main difference here is that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are
not
assumed to be gauge equivalent on the whole time slice
\{t_{0}\}\times\widehat{Y}
; thus, the proof of
[
KM07
, Proposition 7.2.1]
does not apply directly here.

Theorem
15.1
will follow from the strong unique continuation of the Seiberg-Witten equations if
\mathfrak{q}=0
. The problem arises from the tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}
, which gives rise to non-local operators. We will provide a toy model in the next subsection to clarify this point, cf. Remark
15.5
. It is essential here that the region
\{t_{0}\}\times Y
over which
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
agree contains the support of
\mathfrak{q}
.

Before we proceed any further, let us state the linearized version of Theorem
15.1
and the version that concerns the irreducibility of spinors.

Theorem 15.2

(The Linearized Version)
.

Let
I=(t_{1},t_{2})_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
be an open interval. Consider a tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}
supported on the truncated 3-manifold
Y=\{s\leq 0\}\subset\widehat{Y}
and a smooth solution
\gamma
to the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equation (
15.1
) on the 4-manifold
\widehat{Z}=I\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. Suppose a smooth tangent vector at
\gamma

V(t)=(\delta c(t),\delta b(t),\delta\psi(t))\in L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},iT^{*}\widehat{Z}\oplus S)

lies in the kernel of the linearized Seiberg-Witten map:

(15.2)

0=\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}}(V),

or equivalently, it solves the equation (
13.2
). If
V
is generated by the linearized gauge action on
\{t_{0}\}\times Y
at some
t_{0}\in I
, i.e. there exists a smooth function
\xi\in L^{2}_{k+1/2}(\widehat{Y},i\mathbb{R})
such that

(\delta b(0),\delta\psi(0))=\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}\xi\text{ on }\{t_{0}\}\times Y.

then
V
is generated by the linearized gauge action on the whole manifold
\widehat{Z}
, i.e. there exists a smooth function
\xi^{\prime}\in L^{2}_{k+1}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R})
such that

V=\bd_{\gamma}\xi^{\prime}\text{ on }\widehat{Z}.

Theorem 15.3

(Irreducibiliy of Spinors)
.

Let
I=(t_{1},t_{2})_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}
be an open interval. For any tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}
and a solution
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
to the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations (
15.1
) on the 4-manifold
\widehat{Z}=I\times\widehat{Y}
, if the spinor

\Phi\equiv 0\text{ on }\{t_{0}\}\times Y,

for some
t_{0}\in I
, then
\Phi\equiv 0
on
\widehat{Z}
.

The proofs of Theorem
15.1
-
15.3
will not be used elsewhere in this paper. They will dominate the rest of the section.

15.2.
A Motivating Problem

To better explain the ideas and point out the difference from the standard theory
[
KM07
, Section 7]
, let us first discuss a motivating problem that concerns the
\bar{\partial}
-operator on the complex plane. Let

f:\mathbb{C}_{z}\to\mathbb{C}

be a holomorphic function and
z=t+is
be the complex coordinate of the domain. It is well-known that if
f
vanishes along the interval
\{0\}\times[0,1]_{s}
, then
f\equiv 0
over
\mathbb{C}_{z}
.

We investigate a class of perturbations of the
\bar{\partial}-
operator. The equation
\bar{\partial}f=0
can be formally cast into an evolution equation:

\partial_{t}f=-D(f)

where
D(f)=i\partial_{s}f
is a self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathbb{C})
(although we do not assume
f(t)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathbb{C})
for any time slice
\{t\}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}
). Consider a smooth function
K_{1}:\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}
with

\text{supp}\ K_{1}\subset[0,1]_{s}\times[0,1]_{s}

and form the convolution operator

\displaystyle K:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle\to C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto K(f)(s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}K_{1}(s,s^{\prime})f(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}.

Then
D_{K}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D+K
is a compact perturbation of
D
, not necessarily self-adjoint anymore. More generally, let
V:\mathbb{C}_{z}\to\mathbb{C}
be any smooth function and consider the equation

(15.3)

\partial_{t}f=-D_{K}(f)-V\cdot f\text{ on }\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}.

The potential
V
can be viewed as a time-dependent perturbation of
D_{K}
.

Proposition 15.4
.

Suppose
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}_{z},\mathbb{C})
is a solution to the perturbed
\bar{\partial}
-equation (
15.3
) and
f(z)=0
for any
z\in\{0\}\times[0,1]_{s}
, then
f\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{C}_{z}
.

Remark 15.5
.

If we only assume
f\equiv 0
on
\{0\}\times[\epsilon,1]_{s}
for some small
\epsilon>0
, then for some kernel
K_{1}
and potential
V
, the conclusion fails. Indeed, set
f(t,s)\equiv g(s)
and
V\equiv 0
. Let
g
be a cut-off function such that

g(s)\equiv 0,\forall s\geq\epsilon\text{ and }g(s)\equiv 1,\forall s<\epsilon/2.

Then one can find
K_{1}
with
K_{1}*g=-D(g)=-i\partial_{s}g
, so
g\in\ker D_{K}
.
∎

The problem here is that the convolution operator
K
is not local: even if a function
g:[0,1]_{s}\to\mathbb{C}
is supported on a small interval
[0,\epsilon]\subset[0,1]_{s}
,
K(g)=K_{1}*g
might be non-vanishing on a much larger region. This is the analogue of the tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}
in the Seiberg-Witten equations.

The proofs of Theorem
15.1
-
15.3
are modeled on that of Proposition
15.4
, which involves Carleman estimates, as we discuss in the next subsection.

15.3.
Carleman Estimates

There are two classical ways to prove a strong unique continuation property like Proposition
15.4
. The first follows Agmon and Nirenberg
[
AN67
]
and relies on a differential inequality. This is the approach adopted in the book
[
KM07
, Section 7]
. In this paper, we follow the second strategy and base our works on Carleman estimates
[
Car39
]
. The primary result that we consult is
[
Kim95
, Theorem 1]
.

Let us first state a result in an abstract Hilbert space.

Proposition 15.6
.

Let
H
be a Hilbert space and
L_{i}:H\to H,\ i=1,2
be (unbounded) self-adjoint operators on
H
satisfying the relation

(15.4)

(L_{1}+rL_{2}+\alpha)^{2}-rL_{2}\geq 0

for any
r>0
and
\alpha>\alpha_{0}(H,L_{1},L_{2})
; or equivalently,

(15.5)

\|(L_{1}+rL_{2}+\alpha)v\|_{H}^{2}-\re\langle v,(rL_{2})v\rangle\geq 0\ \forall v\in D(L_{1})\cap D(L_{2}).

Here,
\alpha_{0}>0
is a fixed large number depending only on
H
,
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
.

Suppose
w:[0,r_{0}]_{r}\to D(L_{1})\cap D(L_{2})
is a smooth function such that

•

for a constant
C_{0}>0
, the following estimate holds for any
r\in(0,r_{0}]
:

(15.6)

\|(\partial_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\cdot L_{1}+L_{2})w(r)\|_{H}\leq C_{0}\|w(r)\|_{H};

•

w(r)
vanishes at the origin to the infinite order, i.e

(\partial_{r}^{n}w)(0)=0

for any
n\geq 0
. In practice, we will only need the property that

(15.7)

\|w(r)\|_{H},\|\partial_{r}w(r)\|_{H}=\mathcal{O}(r^{n})\text{ as }r\to 0,

for any
n\geq 1
.

Then
w\equiv 0
.

With loss of generality, we assume
r_{0}=1
and let
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ln r\in(-\infty,0]
. Then the inequality (
15.6
) becomes

(15.8)

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\partial_{x}+L_{1}+e^{x}L_{2})w(x),

\displaystyle\|g(x)\|_{H}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}e^{x}\|w(x)\|_{H},\ \forall x\in(-\infty,0].

The key ingredient is the Carleman estimate. We follow the idea from
[
AB80
]
. For any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, consider the weight function
\varphi:(-\infty,0]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
implicitly determined by the relation
-\varphi(x)+\exp(-\epsilon\varphi(x))=x
, so
\varphi(x)\sim-x
and

(15.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{1+\epsilon e^{-\epsilon\varphi(x)}}\in(-1,-\frac{1}{2}),

(15.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon^{2}e^{-\epsilon\varphi(x)}}{(1+\epsilon e^{-\epsilon\varphi(x)})^{3}}\geq C_{1}\epsilon^{2}\cdot e^{2\epsilon x},

for a constant
C_{1}>0
. In what follows, we will always treat
\epsilon\in(0,1)
as a fixed constant.

Proposition 15.7

(Carleman Estimates,
[
Kim95
]
Theorem 1)
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
15.6
, for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, there is a constant
C(\epsilon)>0
such that for any
\tau>2\alpha_{0}
and
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}((-\infty,0),D(L_{1})\cap D(L_{2}))
, we have

\tau\int_{(-\infty,0)}\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)+\epsilon x}u(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx\leq C(\epsilon)\int_{(-\infty,0)}\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)}(\partial_{x}+L_{1}+e^{x}L_{2})u(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx.

This estimate is uniform in
\tau
.

Proof of Proposition
15.6
.

Fix some
x_{0}<0
. To apply Carleman estimates, choose a cut-off function
\chi:(-\infty,0]_{x}\to[0,1]
such that
\chi(x)\equiv 1
when
x<x_{0}
and
\chi(0)=0
. Set
u(x)=\chi(x)w(x)
. The function
u(x)
is not compactly supported on
(-\infty,0)
, but its decay is faster than any exponential function as
x\to-\infty
, by (
15.7
). In this case, Proposition
15.7
still applies, cf. Remark
15.8
; so

\displaystyle\frac{\tau}{2C(\epsilon)}\int_{(-\infty,x_{0}]}

\displaystyle\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)+\epsilon x}w(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx\leq\frac{\tau}{2C(\epsilon)}\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)+\epsilon x}u(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx,

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)}(\partial_{x}+L_{1}+e^{x}L_{2})u(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx,

\displaystyle\leq\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)}g(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx+\int_{[x_{0},0]}\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)}[\partial_{x},\chi(x)]w(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx,

\displaystyle(\text{by }\eqref{E20.16})

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)+x}w(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx+C_{2}e^{\tau\varphi(x_{0})}\int_{[x_{0},0]}\|w\|_{H}^{2}dx,

where
C_{2}=\|\partial_{x}\chi\|_{\infty}^{2}
. The upshot is that this inequality holds for any
\tau\gg 1
, so when
\tau>4C_{0}C(\epsilon)
, we use the rearrangement argument to derive that

\frac{\tau}{2}\int_{(-\infty,x_{0}]}\|e^{\tau\varphi(x)+\epsilon x}w(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx\leq 2C(\epsilon)C_{2}e^{\tau\varphi(x_{0})}\int_{[x_{0},0]}\|w\|_{H}^{2}dx.

Let
\tau\to\infty
. We conclude that
w(x)\equiv 0
when
x<x_{0}
. Since
x_{0}<0
is arbitrary,
w\equiv 0
on
(-\infty,0]
.
∎

To complete the proof of Proposition
15.6
, it remains to prove Carleman estimates.

Proof of Proposition
15.7
.

It is essentially the same argument as
[
Kim95
, Theorem 1]
. We record the proof here because a slight modification will be made in our actual applications. Set
v(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}e^{\tau\varphi(x)}u(x)
, then

e^{\tau\varphi(x)}(\partial_{x}+L_{1}+e^{x}L_{2})e^{-\tau\varphi(x)}v(x)=(\partial_{x}+L_{1}+e^{x}L_{2}+\tau(-\partial_{x}\varphi(x)))v(x).

Define
L(x)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L_{1}+e^{x}L_{2}+\tau(-\partial_{x}\varphi(x))
and compute

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|(\partial_{x}+L(x))v(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|\partial_{x}v(x)\|_{H}^{2}+\|L(x)v(x)\|_{H}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{(-\infty,0]}2\re\langle\partial_{x}v(x),L(x)v(x)\rangle_{H}dx.

Using the fact that
L(x):H\to H
is a self-adjoint operator, we integrate by parts:

(15.11)

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0]}2\re\langle\partial_{x}v(x),L(x)v(x)\rangle_{H}=-\int_{(-\infty,0]}\re\langle v(x),(\partial_{x}L(x))v(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0]}\re\langle v(x),e^{x}(-L_{2})v(x)\rangle+\tau\int_{(-\infty,0]}\langle v(x),(\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(x))v(x)\rangle

\displaystyle(\text{by }\eqref{E20.4})\geq

\displaystyle\int_{(-\infty,0]}\re\langle v(x),e^{x}(-L_{2})v(x)\rangle+C_{1}\epsilon^{2}\tau\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|e^{\epsilon x}v(x)\|_{H}^{2}.

Set
\alpha=\tau(-\partial_{x}\varphi)
. If
\tau>2\alpha_{0}
, then by (
15.9
),
\alpha=\tau(-\partial_{x}\varphi)>\tau/2>\alpha_{0}.
Now we use the relation (
15.4
) to conclude that

\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|(\partial_{x}+L(x))v(x)\|_{H}^{2}dx\geq C_{1}\epsilon^{2}\tau\int_{(-\infty,0]}\|e^{\epsilon x}v(x)\|_{H}^{2}

for any
\tau>2\alpha_{0}
and
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.
∎

Remark 15.8
.

When
u(x):(-\infty,0)\to D(L_{1})\cap D(L_{2})
is not compactly supported and yet
u(0)=0
, we have to verify that the boundary term in (
15.11
) vanishes:

\lim_{x\to-\infty}\re\langle v(x),L(x)v(x)\rangle.

Then one may assume that
\|u(x)\|_{H},\ \|\partial_{x}u(x)\|_{H}
and
\|(L_{1}+e^{x}L_{2})u(x)\|_{H}
decay faster than any exponential functions as
x\to-\infty
. In Proposition
15.6
, this is guaranteed by (
15.8
) and (
15.7
).
∎

15.4.
Applications

In this subsection, we give a few examples of
(H,L_{1},L_{2})
for which the assumption (
15.5
) is fulfilled and derive Proposition
15.4
from the abstract Proposition
15.6
. We will work out the Seiberg-Witten equations in the next subsection.

Lemma 15.9
.

If self-adjoint operators
L_{1},L_{2}:H\to H
anti-commute, i.e.

\{L_{1},L_{2}\}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L_{1}L_{2}+L_{2}L_{1}=0,

then the condition (
15.5
) holds.

Proof.

We rewrite the left hand side of (
15.5
) as

\|(L_{1}+(1-\frac{1}{2\alpha})rL_{2}+\alpha)v\|_{H}^{2}+(1-(1-\frac{1}{2\alpha})^{2})\|(rL_{2})v\|_{H}^{2}+\frac{\re\langle L_{1}v,(rL_{2})v\rangle}{\alpha}\geq 0\text{ if }\alpha>\frac{1}{4}.

The last term vanishes because
\{L_{1},L_{2}\}=0.

∎

Example 15.10
.

The first example is the Dirac operator on
\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
where
\Sigma=\partial Y
is a union of 2-tori endowed with a flat metric. We choose a
spin^{c}\
connection
A
on
\mathbb{C}_{z}
such that

A=\frac{d}{dt}+\frac{d}{ds}+\check{B}

for a fixed
spin^{c}\
connection
\check{B}
on the surface
\Sigma
.

Using the polar coordinate
(r,\theta)
on the complex plane, the Dirac operator
D_{A}^{+}
can be written as

D_{A}^{+}=\rho_{4}(dr)(\partial_{r}+\rho_{3}(rd\theta)\cdot(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{\theta}+D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}))

where
D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}
is the Dirac operator associated to
\check{B}
on the surface. Unlike
\rho_{4}(rd\theta)
,

\rho_{3}(rd\theta)=\rho_{4}(dr)^{-1}\cdot\rho_{4}(rd\theta)=-\rho_{4}(dr\wedge rd\theta)

is a constant bundle map. Proposition
15.6
applies to the operator
\rho_{4}^{-1}(dr)\cdot D_{A}^{+}
with

L_{1}^{\mathbb{D}}=\rho_{3}(rd\theta)\cdot\partial_{\theta},\ L_{2}^{\mathbb{D}}=\rho_{3}(rd\theta)D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}.

and
H=L^{2}(S^{1}\times\Sigma,S^{+})
. Indeed, by Lemma
15.9
,
\{L_{1}^{\mathbb{D}},L_{2}^{\mathbb{D}}\}=0
.
∎

Example 15.11
.

The second example concerns the self-dual operator

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}(X,i\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{+}(X,i\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\mapsto d^{+}b,

on the 4-manifold
X=\mathbb{C}_{z}\times\Sigma
. Using the polar coordinate at the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}_{z}
, we regard
b
as an 1-form on

X^{\prime}=[0,r_{0})_{r}\times S^{1}\times\Sigma.

Suppose
b
does not contain the
dr
-component and write

b(r)=b_{1}(r)(rd\theta)+b_{2}(r)

with
b_{1}(r)\in H_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}(S^{1}\times\Sigma,\mathbb{R})
and
b_{2}(r)\in H_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}(S^{1}\times\Sigma,T^{*}\Sigma)
. As the metric on
X^{\prime}
is given by

dr^{2}+(rd\theta)^{2}+g_{\Sigma},

the equation
d^{+}b=0
is equivalent to that

\partial_{r}\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}(r)\\
b_{2}(r)\end{pmatrix}+\bigg[\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&*_{\Sigma}\partial_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&*_{\Sigma}d_{\Sigma}\\
-*_{\Sigma}d_{\Sigma}&0\end{pmatrix}\bigg]\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}(r)\\
b_{2}(r)\end{pmatrix}=0.

To apply Proposition
15.6
, set
H=H_{1}\oplus H_{2}
and

L_{1}^{\mathbb{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&L_{3}\end{pmatrix},\ L_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&L_{4}\\
L_{4}^{*}&0\end{pmatrix},

with
L_{3}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}*_{\Sigma}\partial_{\theta}:H_{2}\to H_{2}
and
L_{4}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}*_{\Sigma}d_{\Sigma}:H_{2}\to H_{1}
. To verify the condition (
15.5
), we calculate for each
v=(b_{1},b_{2})\in H
that

\displaystyle\|(L_{1}^{\mathbb{S}}+rL_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}+\alpha)v\|_{H}^{2}-\langle v,rL_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}v\rangle

\displaystyle=\|\alpha b_{1}+rL_{4}b_{2}\|_{H_{1}}^{2}+\|rL_{4}^{*}b_{1}+(L_{3}+\alpha)b_{2}\|_{H_{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+(2\alpha+1)\|b_{1}\|_{H_{1}}^{2}\geq 0\text{ if }\alpha>-\frac{1}{2}.

In this case, Lemma
15.9
is not applicable because the anti-commutator
\{L_{1}^{\mathbb{S}},L_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}\}\neq 0
.
∎

In the proof of Proposition
15.4
below, we will work with operator
L_{1},L_{2}
that are not self-adjoint on
H
. Nevertheless, the abstract Proposition
15.6
still applies, since we can verify the first step of (
15.11
) directly: this is the only place the self-adjointness was used.

Proof of Proposition
15.4
.

Let
I=[0,1]_{s}
. For any
r\geq 0
, consider the contour
\Gamma_{r}=\Gamma_{r}^{(1)}+\Gamma_{r}^{(2)}+\Gamma_{r}^{(3)}+\Gamma_{r}^{(4)}
with

\displaystyle\Gamma_{r}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\{r\}\times I,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{r}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\{i+re^{i\theta}:0\leq\theta\leq\pi\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{r}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\{-r\}\times I,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{r}^{(4)}=

\displaystyle\{re^{i\theta}:\pi\leq\theta\leq 2\pi\},

and define

\displaystyle v_{1}(r)

\displaystyle=f|_{\Gamma_{r}^{(1)}\coprod\Gamma_{r}^{(3)}}\in H_{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}(I\coprod(-I),\mathbb{C}),

\displaystyle v_{2}(r)

\displaystyle=f|_{\Gamma_{r}^{(2)}\coprod\Gamma_{r}^{(4)}}\in H_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\coprod[\pi,2\pi]_{\theta},\mathbb{C}).

where
(-I)
stands for the orientation reversal of
I
. Finally, set

w(r)=(w_{1}(r),w_{2}(r))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(v_{1}(r),\sqrt{r}v_{2}(r))\in H\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}H_{1}\oplus H_{2}.

Our assumptions imply that the function
w:[0,1)\to H
vanishes to the infinite order at the origin. To apply Proposition
15.6
, we look for the differential equation that governs
w(r)
. As the function
f
solves the perturbed
\bar{\partial}
-equation, we have

(15.12)

\partial_{r}w(r)+\big(\frac{1}{r}L_{1}+L_{2}\big)w(r)=h(r)

with

L_{1}=(0,i\partial_{\theta}-\frac{1}{2})\text{ and }L_{2}=(i\partial_{s},0)\text{ on }H=H_{1}\oplus H_{2}.

The error term
h(r)
in (
15.12
) is determined by the convolution operator
K
and the smooth potential
V
, so the assumption (
15.6
) is satisfied in our case.

Neither
L_{1}
nor
L_{2}
is a self-adjoint operator on
H
, but we still have

(15.13)

\re\langle(\frac{1}{r}L_{1}+L_{2})w(r),\partial_{r}w(r)\rangle=\re\langle w(r),(\frac{1}{r}L_{1}+L_{2})\partial_{r}w(r)\rangle

which justifies the equality (
15.11
) in the proof of Proposition
15.7
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\langle(\frac{1}{r}L_{1}+L_{2})w(r),\partial_{r}w(r)\rangle-\langle w(r),(\frac{1}{r}L_{1}+L_{2})\partial_{r}w(r)\rangle=\frac{i}{2r}\int_{[0,\pi]\coprod[\pi,2\pi]}\partial_{\theta}|v_{2}(r,\theta)|^{2}d\theta

is purely imaginary. As the relation

2\re\langle L_{1}v,L_{2}v\rangle=0,\forall v\in D(L_{1})\cap D(L_{2})

still holds in our case, the proof of Lemma
15.9
remains valid. Now we use Lemma
15.9
and Proposition
15.6
to complete the proof.
∎

15.5.
The Seiberg-Witten Equations

Having discussed some toy problems, we are now ready to prove the strong unique continuation property for the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations, by combining Example
15.10
and
15.11
.

Proof of Theorem
15.1
.

With loss of generality, assume
I=[-1,1]
and
t_{0}=0
. It suffices to show that
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are gauge equivalent in an open neighborhood of
\{0\}\times Y
, then one may use induction to extend this neighborhood to the whole space
\widehat{Z}=I\times\widehat{Y}
.

To imitate the proof of Proposition
15.4
, consider the closed 3-manifold
\mathcal{Y}_{r}=\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(3)}
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{r\}\times Y,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\{re^{i\theta}:0\leq\theta\leq\pi\}\times\Sigma,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(3)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(-\{-r\}\times Y),

\displaystyle\forall r

\displaystyle\in[0,1].

Here
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(3)}
is the orientation reversal of
\{-r\}\times Y
. Let
B_{0}
be the reference
spin^{c}\
connection on
\widehat{Y}
, so
B_{0}
agrees with the
\mathbb{R}_{s}
-invariant connection

\frac{d}{ds}+\check{B}

on the cylindrical end
[-1,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
. Set
\gamma_{0}=(B_{0},0)
.

Extend the gauge transformation
u
constantly in the time direction and replace
\gamma_{1}
by
u(\gamma_{1})
. Construct gauge transformations
u_{i},\ i=1,2
such that
u_{i}\equiv\Id
on
\{0\}\times\widehat{Y}
and
\gamma_{i}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}u_{i}(\gamma_{i})
is in the temporal gauge (the
dt
-component vanishes). Consider the difference

\delta_{i}(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\gamma_{i}^{\prime}|_{\{t\}\times\widehat{Y}}-\gamma_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{Y},iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S)

Formally,
\delta_{i}
is subject to an evolution equation:

\partial_{t}\delta_{i}(t)+L_{2}^{Y}\delta_{i}(t)+\delta_{i}(t)\#\delta_{i}(t)+\mathfrak{q}(\delta_{i}(t)+\gamma_{0})=c.

where
\#
is a symmetric bilinear form that involves only point-wise multiplications. Here
c
is a constant error term determined by
\gamma_{0}
and

L_{2}^{Y}=\begin{pmatrix}*_{3}d_{\widehat{Y}}&0\\
0&D_{B_{0}}\end{pmatrix}.

Now take the difference
\delta(t)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\delta_{2}(t)-\delta_{1}(t)
. Over the space
[-1,1]_{t}\times Y
, we have

(15.14)

\partial_{t}\delta(t)+L^{Y}_{2}(\delta(t))=h_{1}(t)\in C^{\infty}(Y,iT^{*}Y\oplus S)

and
\|h_{1}(t)\|_{L^{2}(Y)}\leq C\|\delta(t)\|_{L^{2}(Y)}
for a uniform constant
C>0
. Moreover,

\partial_{t}^{n}\delta(0)\equiv 0\text{ on }Y\text{ for any }n\geq 0.

When
n=0
, this follows from the assumption that
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
on
\{0\}\times Y
. When
n\geq 1
, this is a consequence of the equation (
15.14
) and its higher time derivatives. As a result, all derivatives of
\delta
vanish on
\{0\}\times Y
.

Set
H_{1}=L^{2}(Y,iT^{*}Y\oplus S)
and define

v_{1}(r)=(\delta(r)|_{Y},\delta(-r)|_{Y})\in H_{1}\oplus H_{1}.

Then
\partial_{r}^{n}v_{1}(0)=0
for any
n\geq 0
.

To deal with the middle part
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}
, consider the polar coordinate at
0\in\mathbb{C}_{z}
and restrict
\delta
to a section of

iT^{*}X^{\prime}\oplus S\to X^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[0,1]_{r}\times[0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\{s\geq 0\}\times\Sigma.

The section
\delta
is not necessarily in the radial temporal gauge: the
dr
-component of
\delta
only vanishes when
\theta=0,\pi
. One has to construct gauge transformations
u^{\prime}_{i}:X^{\prime}\to S^{1}
on
X^{\prime}
such that
u_{i}^{\prime}|_{\{0\}\times[0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma}\equiv\Id
and
u_{i}^{\prime}(\gamma_{i}^{\prime})
is the radial temporal gauge. Then we define

v_{2}(r)=u_{2}^{\prime}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime})(r)-u_{1}^{\prime}(\gamma_{1}^{\prime})(r)\in H_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}L^{2}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma,i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\Sigma\oplus S).

Then the path
v_{2}(r)
is subject to the equation

\partial_{r}v_{2}(r)+\bigg(\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}L_{1}^{\mathbb{S}}&0\\
0&L_{1}^{\mathbb{D}}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}L_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}&0\\
0&L_{2}^{\mathbb{D}}\end{pmatrix}\bigg)v_{2}(r)=h_{2}(r)\in H_{2}.

and
\|h_{2}(r)\|_{H_{2}}\leq C\|v_{2}(r)\|_{H_{2}}
for a constant
C>0
. The Seiberg-Witten equations are not perturbed on
X^{\prime}
, so the error term
h_{2}(r)
involves only point-wise multiplications with
v_{2}(r)
. Operators
L_{i}^{\mathbb{S}}
and
L_{i}^{\mathbb{D}},i=1,2
are defined as in Example
15.10
and
15.11
.

As all derivatives of
\delta
vanish on
(0,0)\times\Sigma
,
\partial_{r}^{n}v_{2}(0)=0
for any
n\geq 0
.

Finally, let
H=(H_{1}\oplus H_{1})\oplus H_{2}
and define

w(r)=(w_{1}(r),w_{2}(r))\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(v_{1}(r),\sqrt{r}v_{2}(r))\in H.

Now the path
w:[0,1)_{r}\to H
is subject to the equation

(15.15)

\partial_{r}w(r)+(\frac{1}{r}L_{1}+L_{2})w(r)=(h_{1}(r),-h_{1}(-r),\sqrt{r}h_{2}(r)).

with

L_{1}=(0,0,\begin{pmatrix}L_{1}^{\mathbb{S}}&0\\
0&L_{1}^{\mathbb{D}}\end{pmatrix}-\frac{1}{2}),L_{2}=(L_{2}^{Y},-L_{2}^{Y},\begin{pmatrix}L_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}&0\\
0&L_{2}^{\mathbb{D}}\end{pmatrix}).

To apply Proposition
15.6
, we have to verify:

•

the positivity condition (
15.5
);

•

the symmetry condition (
15.13
); note that neither
L_{1}
nor
L_{2}
is self-adjoint.

At this point, we have reduced the problem to some formal properties of
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
and
w(r)
. We will treat the form component and the spinor component of (
15.15
) separately. The verification of (
15.5
) and (
15.13
) will dominate the rest of the proof.

Step
1. The Form Component and the Self-Dual operators. In this case, the positivity condition (
15.5
) follows from the same argument as in Example
15.11
and Proposition
15.4
. It can be checked separately on each of
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(i)},\ 1\leq i\leq 3
. As for (
15.13
), we focus on the common boundary of
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}
. Suppose the form components of
v_{1}(r)
and
v_{2}(r)
are given respectively by

\displaystyle v_{1}|_{\{r\}\times(-1,0]_{s}\times\Sigma}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow a_{1}ds+a_{2},

\displaystyle a_{1}(r)\in C^{\infty}((-1,0]_{s}\times\Sigma,i\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle a_{2}(r)\in C^{\infty}((-1,0]_{s}\times\Sigma,iT^{*}\Sigma),

\displaystyle v_{2}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow b_{1}(rd\theta)+b_{2},

\displaystyle b_{1}(r)\in C^{\infty}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma,i\mathbb{R}),

\displaystyle b_{2}(r)\in C^{\infty}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma,iT^{*}\Sigma).

Near the boundary of
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(1)}
, we have

(*_{3}d_{Y})\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}(r)\\
a_{2}(r)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&*_{\Sigma}d_{\Sigma}\\
-*_{\Sigma}d_{\Sigma}&*_{\Sigma}\partial_{s}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}(r)\\
a_{2}(r)\end{pmatrix}.

Then we calculate (the operator
L_{2}^{\mathbb{S}}
is ignored here as it is always self-adjoint):

\displaystyle\langle(*_{3}d_{Y})v_{1},(\partial_{r}v_{1})\rangle_{\{r\}\times Y}-\langle v_{1},(*_{3}d_{Y})(\partial_{r}v_{1})\rangle_{\{r\}\times Y}

\displaystyle=\langle*_{\Sigma}a_{2}(r,0),(\partial_{r}a_{2})(r,0)\rangle_{(r,0)\times\Sigma}.

\displaystyle\langle\frac{1}{r}L_{1}^{\mathbb{S}}w_{2},(\partial_{r}w_{2})\rangle_{H_{2}}-\langle w_{2},\frac{1}{r}L_{1}^{\mathbb{S}}(\partial_{r}w_{2})\rangle_{H_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\langle*_{\Sigma}b_{2}(r,0),(\partial_{r}b_{2})(r,0)\rangle_{(r,0)\times\Sigma}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{r}\underbrace{\int_{[0,\pi]_{\theta}}\partial_{\theta}\langle*_{\Sigma}b_{2},b_{2}\rangle_{\Sigma}}_{=0}+\cdots.

It remains to verify that
a_{2}(r,0)=b_{2}(r,0)
on
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}
. Suppose the restriction of the form component of
\delta
on
X^{\prime}=[0,1]_{r}\times[0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma
is
fdr+c_{1}(rd\theta)+c_{2}
with

\displaystyle f(r),c_{1}(r)\in C^{\infty}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma,i\mathbb{R}),\ c_{2}(r)\in C^{\infty}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma,iT^{*}\Sigma).

It is clear that
a_{2}
and
c_{2}
agree along the common boundary of
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}
. Moreover,

f(r,\theta)\equiv 0\text{ if }\theta=0\text{ or }\pi.

To put
\delta
into radial temporal gauge, we applied further gauge transformations, so
(b_{1},b_{2})
is related to
\delta
by the formulae:

\displaystyle b_{1}(r)

\displaystyle=c_{1}(r)-\frac{1}{r}\int_{0}^{r}(\partial_{\theta}f)(r^{\prime})dr^{\prime},

\displaystyle b_{2}(r)

\displaystyle=c_{2}(r)-\int_{0}^{r}(d_{\Sigma}f)(r^{\prime})dr^{\prime}.

As a result,
a_{2}(r,s)|_{s=0}=b_{2}(r,\theta)|_{\theta=0}
. This equality does not a priori hold for
a_{1}
and
b_{1}
, but it is not needed in the proof.

Step
2. The Spinor Component and the Dirac operators. The proof of (
15.13
) proceeds in the same way as in
Step
1. We focus on the positivity condition (
15.5
). Suppose the spinor components of
v_{i}(r),\ 1\leq i\leq 3
are given respectively by

\displaystyle v_{1}|_{\{r\}\times Y}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow\Phi_{1}(r)\in C^{\infty}(Y,S),

\displaystyle v_{2}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow\Phi_{2}(r)\in C^{\infty}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma,S),

\displaystyle v_{3}|_{\{r\}\times Y}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow\Phi_{3}(r)\in C^{\infty}(Y,S).

We focus on sections

(\Phi_{1}(r),\Phi_{3}(r),\sqrt{r}\Phi_{2}(r))\in L^{2}(Y,S)\oplus L^{2}(Y,S)\oplus L^{2}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma,S)

and operators:

\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&L_{1}^{\mathbb{D}}-\frac{1}{2}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}D_{B_{0}}&0&0\\
0&-D_{B_{0}}&0\\
0&0&L_{2}^{\mathbb{D}}\end{pmatrix}.

Unlike Example
15.10
,
L^{\mathbb{D}}_{1}
is not self-adjoint in this case. In general,

2\re\langle L^{\mathbb{D}}_{1}v,L^{\mathbb{D}}_{2}v\rangle_{L^{2}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma)}=\int_{\{\theta\}\times\Sigma}\langle v,D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}v\rangle\ \bigg|_{\theta=0}^{\theta=\pi}\neq 0,\ v\in L^{2}([0,\pi]_{\theta},S).

Let
v=\sqrt{r}\Phi_{2}(r)
and follow the proof of Lemma
15.9
:

\displaystyle\|(L_{1}^{\mathbb{D}}+rL_{2}^{\mathbb{D}}+(\alpha-\frac{1}{2}))\sqrt{r}\Phi_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma)}-\re\langle\sqrt{r}\Phi_{2},(rL_{2}^{\mathbb{D}})\sqrt{r}\Phi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{2r^{2}}{2\alpha-1}\re\langle L_{1}^{\mathbb{D}}\Phi_{2},L_{2}^{\mathbb{D}}\Phi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{2\alpha-1}\bigg(\int_{\{\pi\}\times\Sigma}\langle\Phi_{2},D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}\Phi_{2}\rangle-\int_{\{0\}\times\Sigma}\langle\Phi_{2},D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}\Phi_{2}\rangle\bigg).

Just as in
Step
1, sections
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
have the same boundary value along
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(1)}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}
:

\Phi_{1}(r,s)\big|_{s=0}=\Phi_{2}(r,\theta)\big|_{\theta=0}.

Therefore, it remains to verify the inequality:

\|(rD_{B_{0}}+\alpha)\Phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}-\re\langle\Phi_{1},(rD_{B_{0}})\Phi_{1}\rangle\geq\frac{r^{2}}{2\alpha-1}\int_{\{0\}\times\Sigma}\langle\Phi_{1},D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}\Phi_{1}\rangle.

The left hand side can be rewritten as

(1-\frac{1}{2\alpha-1})\|(rD_{B_{0}}+\frac{(2\alpha-1)^{2}}{4\alpha-4})\Phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}+\frac{r^{2}}{2\alpha-1}\|D_{B_{0}}\Phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}+\frac{4\alpha^{2}-6\alpha+1}{8\alpha-8}\|\Phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}.

Using the Weitzenböck formula
[
KM07
, (4,15)]
, the last two terms are bounded below by

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{2\alpha-1}\bigg(\|\nabla_{B_{0}}\Phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}+\int_{\{0\}\times\Sigma}\langle\Phi_{1},D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}\Phi_{1}\rangle+\int_{Y}\frac{s}{4}|\Phi_{1}|^{2}+\langle\Phi_{1},\frac{1}{2}\rho_{3}(F_{B_{0}^{t}})\Phi_{1}\rangle\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\alpha-1}{2}\|\Phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}\geq\frac{r^{2}}{2\alpha-1}\int_{\{0\}\times\Sigma}\langle\Phi_{1},D_{\check{B}}^{\Sigma}\Phi_{1}\rangle+\frac{\alpha-\alpha_{0}}{2}\|\Phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Y)}.

Then we take
\alpha>\alpha_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}e^{100}\max\{\|s\|_{\infty},\|F_{B_{0}^{t}}\|_{\infty},1\}
.

The common boundary
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(3)}
is dealt with similarly. Hence, the positivity condition (
15.5
) holds when
\alpha>\alpha_{0}
. Now we use Proposition
15.6
and
15.7
to complete the proof.
∎

15.6.
Irreducibility of Spinors

We accomplish the proof of Theorem
15.3
in this subsection, following the idea above. The spinor part of the equation (
15.1
) is cast into the form

\frac{d}{dt}\Psi(t)+D_{B(t)}\Psi(t)+\mathfrak{q}^{1}(B(t),\Psi(t))=0.

where
\mathfrak{q}^{1}
is the spinor part of the perturbation
\mathfrak{q}=(\mathfrak{q}^{0},\mathfrak{q}^{1})
. As
\mathfrak{q}^{1}(B(t),0)\equiv 0
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{q}^{1}(B(t),\Psi(t))\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|\mathfrak{q}^{1}(B(t),\Psi(t))-\mathfrak{q}^{1}(B(t),0)\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\|\mathcal{D}_{(B(t),r\Psi(t))}\mathfrak{q}^{1}(\Psi(t))\|_{2}dr\leq C\|\Psi(t)\|_{L^{2}(Y)},

for a constant
C>0
and any
t\in[t_{0}-\epsilon,t_{0}+\epsilon]
. Now the proof of Theorem
15.1
can proceed with no difficulty.

15.7.
The Linearized Version

In this subsection, we accomplish the proof of Theorem
15.2
. To some extent, it suffices to “linearize” each step of the proof of Theorem
15.1
. Again, assume
I=[-1,1]_{t}
and
t_{0}=0
.

\xi^{(1)}(t)=\xi-\int_{0}^{t}\delta c(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R}),

and set
V_{1}=V-\bd_{\gamma}\xi^{(1)}
. This new section
V_{1}
is smooth, and

\displaystyle V_{1}(t)

\displaystyle=(0,\delta b_{1}(t),\delta\psi_{1}(t))\in L^{2}_{k}(Z,iT^{*}Z\oplus S),

\displaystyle V_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\{0\}\times Y.

As
\gamma
solves the non-linear equation (
15.1
),
\textbf{d}_{\gamma}f^{(1)}
is a solution to the linear equation (
15.2
), and so is
V_{1}
. The equation (
13.2
) is formally an evolutionary equation on
I\times Y
:

(15.16)

\frac{d}{dt}\begin{pmatrix}\delta b_{1}(t)\\
\delta\psi_{1}(t)\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}*_{3}d_{Y}&0\\
0&D_{B_{0}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\delta b(t)\\
\delta\psi(t)\end{pmatrix}=\eta(t)\begin{pmatrix}\delta b(t)\\
\delta\psi(t)\end{pmatrix},t\in\mathbb{R}.

where
\eta(t):L^{2}(Y)\to L^{2}(Y)
is a family of bounded linear operators determined by
\check{\gamma}(t)
.

To borrow the proof of Theorem
15.2
, we focus on
\mathcal{Y}_{r}^{(2)}
. Using polar coordinates, we write

V_{1}(r)=(\delta c_{1}^{\prime}(r),\delta b_{1}^{\prime}(r),\delta\psi_{1}^{\prime}(r))\in C^{\infty}(X^{\prime},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}([0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma)\oplus S),

on
X^{\prime}=[0,1]_{r}\times[0,\pi]_{\theta}\times\Sigma\subset\mathbb{H}^{2}_{+}\times\Sigma
. To put
V_{1}(r)
into radial temporal gauge, consider the function
