f^{(2)}(r)=-\int_{0}^{r}\delta c_{1}^{\prime}(r^{\prime})dr^{\prime}\text{ on }X^{\prime}.

Then
f^{(2)}(r,\theta)\equiv 0
when
\theta=0,\pi
, and the section
V_{1}-\textbf{d}_{\gamma}f^{(2)}
solves the linear equation (
15.2
) on
X
. The proof of Theorem
15.1
is now applicable. We conclude that

(15.17)

\displaystyle V_{1}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv 0\text{ on }I\times Y

\displaystyle V_{1}-\textbf{d}_{\gamma}f^{(2)}

\displaystyle\equiv 0\text{ on }X^{\prime}.

We extend
f^{(2)}
by zero over the product
I\times Y
. One might worry that
f^{(2)}
does not form a smooth function on the union

(I\times Y)\bigcup X^{\prime},

as we pointed out in
Step
1 in the proof of Theorem
15.2
. However, once the unique continuation property is established, the smoothness of
f^{(2)}
follows from (
15.17
) and the smoothness of
V_{1}
. As a result,

V_{1}=\textbf{d}_{\gamma}f^{(2)}\text{ on }(I\times Y)\bigcup X^{\prime}.

By induction, we can extend the region where this equality holds. This completes the proof of Theorem
15.2
.

16.
Transversality

With all machinery developed so far, we are ready to prove the transversality result on the cylinder
\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
in this section. Here is the main result:

Theorem 16.1
.

For any relative
spin^{c}\
manifold
(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
satisfying constraints in the strict cobordism category
\Cob_{s}
, one can find an admissible perturbation
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, in the sense of Definition
13.3
. Here
\mathcal{P}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is the Banach space of tame perturbations constructed Subsection
8.5
.

Pick an admissible perturbation
\mathfrak{q}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
for each relative
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}
on
Y
. By putting them altogether, we obtain an object
\mathbb{Y}=(Y,\psi,g_{Y},\omega,\mathfrak{q})
in the category
\Cob_{s}
: the property
(P7)
is fulfilled. In this case, the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{[\gamma]}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b})
defined in Section
13
will become a smooth manifold, and the Floer homology of
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
will be defined in Part
VI
.

Theorem
16.1
is a formal consequence of the unique continuation properties, Theorem
15.1
-
15.3
and the separating properties of cylinder functions, Theorem
8.20
. The transversality result for a general morphism
\mathbb{X}:(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
in the category
\SCob_{s}
is proved in Subsection
16.3
, cf. Theorem
16.5
.

16.1.
Transversality for the 3-Dimensional Equations

Consider the Banach space of perturbations
\mathcal{P}
and a tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}=\grad f\in\mathcal{P}
. We start with the first condition
(E1)
in Definition
13.3
which concerns the 3-dimensional equation

\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\mathfrak{a})=0,

Recall from Definition
12.2
that a critical point
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+f
is called non-degenerate if the extended Hessian at
\mathfrak{a}

\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}}

is invertible. In fact, this is a generic condition for a perturbation
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}
.

Theorem 16.2

(cf.
[
KM07
]
Theorem 12.1.12)
.

There is a residue (and in particular non-empty) subset of
\mathcal{P}
such that for every
\mathfrak{q}
in this subset, any critical point
\mathfrak{a}\in\Crit(\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega})
is non-degenerate. For such a perturbation,
\Crit(\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega})
comprises a finite collection of gauge orbits.

Proof.

The proof follows the same argument as in
[
KM07
, Section 12.5]
with one slight modification, as we explain now. Suppose for some
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}
and
\mathfrak{a}\in\Crit{\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}}
, the tangent vector
v=(0,\delta b,\delta\psi)\neq 0
lies in the kernel of
\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}}
:

(16.1)

(0,\delta b,\delta\psi)\in\ker\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{a}}.

We have to show that
v
is separated by a cylinder function. To apply Proposition
8.6
. we verify that
v
is not generated by the infinitesimal gauge action on
Y
. Suppose on the contrary that

(16.2)

(\delta b,\delta\psi)=\bd_{\mathfrak{a}}\xi\text{ on }Y

for some
\xi\in L^{2}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y},i\mathbb{R})
, then by the unique continuation property of tangent vectors, Theorem
15.2
, for a possibly different function
\xi^{\prime}\in L^{2}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y},\mathbb{R})
, the equation (
16.2
) holds on
\widehat{Y}
:

(\delta b,\delta\psi)=\bd_{\mathfrak{a}}\xi^{\prime}.

By (
16.1
),
\bd_{\mathfrak{a}}^{*}(\delta b,\delta\psi)=0
, so
(\delta b,\delta\psi)
is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to the subspace
\mathcal{J}_{k,\mathfrak{a}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{k,\mathfrak{a}}
. This implies that
v=0
, which a contradiction. Alternatively, we may apply the linearized version of
[
KM07
, Theorem 7.2.1]
on the 4-manifold

S^{1}\times\widehat{Y},

which possesses a cylindrical end
S^{1}\times[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
. Now we use Proposition
8.6
to find a cylinder function
f\in\Cylin(Y)
supported on
Y\subset\widehat{Y}
such that

df(v)\neq 0.

The rest of the proof then follows
[
KM07
, Section 12.5]
.
∎

16.2.
Transversality on Cylinders

Suppose a tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}_{1}=\grad f_{1}
in the residue subset of Theorem
16.2
has been chosen. Then the critical set of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+f_{1}
consists of a finite collection of gauge orbits; let their representatives be

\mathfrak{a}_{i},\ 1\leq i\leq r.

We wish to find a closed Banach subspace
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{P}
such that for any generic
\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with
\|\mathcal{P}\|\ll 1
, the sum

\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}_{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{2}

is an admissible perturbation. The Banach subspace
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
that we consider is

(16.3)

\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}:\mathfrak{q}_{2}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=0,\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}^{1}\mathfrak{q}_{2}=0,\forall i=1,\cdots,r\},

so the perturbation
\mathfrak{q}_{2}
vanishes to the first order at each representative
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
. The subspace
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is clearly closed inside
\mathcal{P}
. Let us first verify the property
(E1)
for
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}_{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{2}
.

Lemma 16.3

(
[
KM07
]
Lemma 15.1.2)
.

There exists some
\eta>0
such that for any
\mathfrak{q}_{2}=\grad_{2}f_{2}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
with
\|\mathfrak{q}_{2}\|_{\mathcal{P}}<\eta
, the critical set of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+(f_{1}+f_{2})
agrees with that of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{1}=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+f_{1}
. As a result, the first condition
(E1)
of Definition
13.3
continues to hold for the sum
\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{q}_{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{2}
.

In particular, for any
\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, the critical points of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in the quotient configuration space
\mathcal{B}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
are still given by
[\mathfrak{a}_{i}],1\leq i\leq r
and

\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}\grad(\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+f_{1})=\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}\grad(\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+f_{1}+f_{2}),\ 1\leq i\leq r.

So each
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is still non-degenerate in the sense of Definition
12.2
. Here
[\mathfrak{a}_{i}]
is the image of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
in
\mathcal{B}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Proof of Lemma.

Suppose on the contrary that there is a sequence of tame perturbations
\mathfrak{q}_{2}^{(j)}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and a sequence of configurations
\beta_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
such that

\|\mathfrak{q}_{j}\|_{\mathcal{P}}\to 0,\ (\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{1}+\mathfrak{q}_{2}^{(j)})(\beta_{j})=0

and each
\beta_{j}
is not gauge equivalent to any of
\mathfrak{a}_{i},1\leq i\leq r
. By Proposition
9.7
, a subsequence of
\{\beta_{j}\}
converges to some
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
up to gauge. Fix
0<\epsilon\ll 1
and let
\mathcal{O}_{i}(\epsilon)
be the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}_{k+1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. When
j\gg 1
, each
\beta_{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{i}(\epsilon)
, and one may use gauge transformations to put
\beta_{j}
into the Coulomb gauge slice at
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
, i.e.

\bd_{\mathfrak{a}_{i}}^{*}(\beta-\mathfrak{a}_{i})=0.

Then

(16.4)

\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{1}(\beta_{j})-\grad\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{1}(\mathfrak{a}_{i})=-(\mathfrak{q}_{2}^{(j)}(\beta_{j})-\mathfrak{q}_{2}^{(j)}(\mathfrak{a}_{j})).

As
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is non-degenerate as a critical point of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}^{1}
, the
L^{2}_{k-1/2}
-norm of the left hand side is bounded below by

c\|\beta_{j}-\mathfrak{a}_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{k+1/2,\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}

for some
c>0
. On the other hand, as
\mathfrak{q}_{2}^{(j)}\to 0
in
\mathcal{P}
, the
C^{2}
-norm of
\mathfrak{q}
over the bounded neighborhood
\mathcal{O}_{i}(\epsilon)
converges to zero, by Corollary
8.19
:

\sup_{\gamma\in\mathcal{O}_{i}(\epsilon)}\|\mathcal{D}^{2}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{q}_{2}^{(j)}\|\to 0\text{ as }j\to\infty.

As a result, the
L^{2}_{k-1/2}
-norm of the right hand side of (
16.4
) is bounded above by

\|\beta_{j}-\mathfrak{a}_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{k-1/2,\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}^{2}\leq\|\beta_{j}-\mathfrak{a}_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{k+1/2,\mathfrak{a}_{i}}}^{2}\leq\epsilon\|\beta_{j}-\mathfrak{a}_{i}\|_{L^{2}_{k+1/2,\mathfrak{a}_{i}}},

when
j\gg 1
, which yields a contradiction if
\epsilon<c
.
∎

Theorem
16.1
now follows from the strong unique continuation property Theorem
15.1
-
15.3
together with Lemma
13.5
. The proof is modeled on
[
KM07
, Section 15]
. In what follows, we will only point out the necessary changes to be made.

Proof of Theorem
16.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b}\in\Crit(\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega})
be critical points of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
and
\widehat{Z}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
be the infinite cylinder. Following the scheme of
[
KM07
, Section 15]
and notations from Subsection
13.1
, it suffice to show for any
\mathfrak{q}_{2}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and any solution
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b})
to the perturbed equation

0=\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z}}+\widehat{\mathfrak{q}},

the operator

(16.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\times L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S)

\displaystyle(\delta\mathfrak{q},V)\mapsto\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}(\gamma)+(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}})(V)

is surjective. The section
\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}(\gamma)
lies in
L^{2}_{k-1}
as the underlying path
\check{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
decay exponentially to either
\mathfrak{a}
or
\mathfrak{b}
as
t\to\pm\infty
and
\delta\mathfrak{q}
vanishes at
\mathfrak{a}
and
\mathfrak{b}
to the first order.

Suppose first that
\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}=0
in (
16.5
), then (
16.5
) becomes a Fredholm operator by Proposition
13.1
, and its cokernel is finite dimensional. It remains to show that for any section

U=(\delta c^{\prime}(t),\delta b^{\prime}(t),\delta\psi^{\prime}(t))\in L^{2}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}\oplus S)

that is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to the image of
(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\widehat{Z},\mathfrak{q}})
, there exist some
\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}\in\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
such that

(16.6)

\langle\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}(\gamma(t)),U\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times\widehat{Y})}\neq 0.

We first explain how to achieve (
16.6
) for a generalized cylinder function
f:\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\to\mathbb{R}
:

(16.7)

\int_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}}\langle\grad f(\check{\gamma}),U(t)\rangle_{L^{2}(\widehat{Y})}\neq 0.

By the unique continuation properties, Theorem
15.1
,
15.2
and
15.3
, the underlying path
\check{\gamma}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{C}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
satisfies the following properties

•

for any
t_{1}\neq t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
,
\check{\gamma}(t_{1})
and
\check{\gamma}(t_{2})
are not gauge equivalent over
Y
;

•

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
,
\check{\gamma}(t)
is not gauge equivalent to
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
on
Y
for any
1\leq i\leq r
; moreover,
\check{\gamma}(t)
is irreducible on
Y
;

•

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
, its derivative
\partial_{t}\check{\gamma}(t)
is not generated by the infinitesimal gauge action over
Y
.

As for the section
U
in the cokernel, by Lemma
13.5
, we have

•

\delta c^{\prime}(t)\equiv 0
;

•

for some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
,
U(t_{0})=(0,\delta b^{\prime}(t_{0}),\delta\psi^{\prime}(t_{0}))
are not generated by the infinitesimal gauge action over
Y
.

Take a large constant
T>0
such that
t_{0}\in[-T,T]
. To apply Theorem
8.20
, let the compact subset
K
be the image of

\{\mathfrak{a}_{i}:1\leq i\leq r\}\bigcup\{\check{\gamma}(t):t\in[-T,T]\}

in the quotient configuration space
\mathcal{B}^{*}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. Then we can find a finite collection of cylinder functions
\{f_{j},\ 1\leq j\leq l\}
defined using embeddings
\iota_{j}:S^{1}\times D^{2}\hookrightarrow Y
such that the map

\Xi^{\prime}_{*}=(f_{1},\cdots,f_{l}):\mathcal{B}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\to\mathbb{R}^{l}

gives an embedding of
K
and
\Xi^{\prime}(U(t_{0}))\neq 0
. Choose a smooth function

g^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}

supported in a small neighborhood
\overline{\Omega}
of
\Xi^{\prime}([\check{\gamma}(t_{0})])
with the following additional properties

•

\Xi^{\prime}([\mathfrak{a}_{i}])\not\in\Omega,\ \forall 1\leq i\leq r;

•

(\Xi^{\prime}\circ\check{\gamma})^{-1}(\overline{\Omega})
is a small connected interval
[t_{0}-\epsilon_{1},t_{0}+\epsilon_{2}]
around
t_{0}
; to achieve this, we take
T\gg 1
;

•

lastly, the integral

(16.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{t}}dg^{\prime}(\Xi^{\prime}_{*}(U(t)))dt\neq 0.

The last property would be impossible if for some constant
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Xi_{*}^{\prime}(U(t))=\alpha\Xi_{*}^{\prime}(\partial_{t}\check{\gamma}_{t})
for any
t\in[t_{0}-\epsilon,t_{0}+\epsilon]
. However, this cannot hold for the whole real line; otherwise one may draw a contradiction from equations (
13.2
) and (
13.8
). Then we can achieve (
16.8
) by taking a different time slice
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{t}
and possibly a different
\Xi^{\prime}
.

As a result, the inequality (
16.7
) is achieved for the composition:

f\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}g^{\prime}\circ\Xi^{\prime}:\mathcal{B}_{k-1/2}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\to\mathbb{R},

Note that
f\equiv 0
in some
L^{2}_{k-1/2}
-neighborhood of
\{[\mathfrak{a}_{i}]:1\leq i\leq r\}
, so
\grad f
satisfies the constraints in (
16.3
). By the density of the Banach space
\mathcal{P}
, we can approximate
\grad f
by an element
\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}
in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and the inequality (
16.6
) holds for this approximation.

The rest of the proof follows the same line of argument as in
[
KM07
, Proposition 15.1.3]
.
∎

16.3.
Transversality on 4-Manifolds in General

Recall the set up from Section
14
. For a morphism
\mathbb{X}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
, the Seiberg-Witten equations
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}}=0
on the complete Riemannian 4-manifold
\mathcal{X}
is perturbed by a quadruple

\mathfrak{p}=(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2},\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3}).

While
(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2})
are encoded in the objects
\mathbb{Y}_{1}
and
\mathbb{Y}_{2}
, the pair

(\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3})\in\mathcal{P}(Y_{3})\times\mathcal{P}_{\form}

is the actual perturbation that allows us to achieve transversality.

Definition 16.4
.

The quadruple
\mathfrak{p}
is said to be admissible if

•

each
\mathfrak{q}_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(Y_{i}),i=1,2
is admissible in the sense of Definition
13.3
;

•

for any
spin^{c}\
cobordism
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\widehat{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
(with a prescribed planar metric
g_{X}
), the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
is regular in the sense of Definition
14.2
. Here
\mathfrak{a}_{i}\in\Crit(\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{i},\widehat{Y}_{i}})
is a critical point of the perturbed Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{i},\widehat{Y}_{i}}
on
\widehat{Y}_{i}
,
i=1,2
. ∎

Theorem 16.5
.

Under above assumptions, for any fixed admissible perturbations
(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2})
on
\widehat{Y}_{1}
and
\widehat{Y}_{2}
respectively, there is a residue subset of
\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})\times\mathcal{P}_{\form}
such that for every pair
(\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3})
in this subset, the quadruple
\mathfrak{p}
is admissible.

Proof.

Following the proof of Theorem
16.1
, it suffices to verify that the operator

(16.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})\times\mathcal{P}_{\form}\times L^{2}_{k}(\mathcal{X},iT^{*}\mathcal{X}\oplus S^{+})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}_{k-1}(\mathcal{X},i\mathbb{R}\oplus i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})\oplus S-)

\displaystyle(\delta\mathfrak{q}_{3},\delta\omega_{3},V)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}})V+\beta_{0}(t)\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}_{3}(\gamma)+\rho_{4}(\delta\omega_{3}^{+}),

is surjective, for any solution
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
to the perturbed equation
\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}}=0
. We begin with
(\delta\mathfrak{q}_{3},\delta\omega_{3})=0
, then (
16.9
) becomes a Fredholm operator by Proposition
14.1
. Suppose
U\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X},i\mathbb{R}\oplus i\Lambda^{+}\mathcal{X}\oplus S^{-})
is
L^{2}
-orthogonal to the image of
(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}})
. it remains to find
(\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}_{3},\delta\omega_{3})
such that

(16.10)

\langle U,\beta_{0}(t)\delta\widehat{\mathfrak{q}}_{3}(\gamma)+\rho_{4}(\delta\omega_{3}^{+})\rangle_{L^{2}}\neq 0.

Let
I=[1,2]_{t}
and write

U=(\delta\xi,\delta\omega,\delta\phi)\text{ with }\delta\xi\in L^{2}(\mathcal{X},i\mathbb{R}).

The same argument as in the proof of Lemma
13.5
implies that
\delta\xi\equiv 0
. The inner product (
16.10
) is supported on the compact submanifold

\widehat{Z}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}I\times\widehat{Y}_{2},

over which the formal adjoint of
(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}})
is cast into the form (
13.6
). If instead we write

U(t)=(0,\delta b(t),\delta\psi(t))\in L^{2}_{1}(\widehat{Z},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\widehat{Y}_{2}\oplus S)\text{ on }I\times\widehat{Y}_{2},

then we are back to the cylindrical case. Here we have used the bundle map

(\rho_{3},\rho_{4}(dt))

to identify
iT^{*}\widehat{Y}_{2}\oplus S
with
i\mathfrak{su}(S^{+})\oplus S^{-}
over
\widehat{Z}
.

However, Lemma
13.5
does not apply directly here, so we argue as follows. If there exists some
t_{0}\in\text{supp}\beta_{0}\subset[1,2]
such that
U(t_{0})
is separated by some cylinder function
f
, then we set
\delta\omega_{3}=0
and proceed as in the proof of Theorem
15.1
.

If not, then by the proof of Lemma
13.5
, for any
t\in[5/4,7/4]
, there exists some function
\xi(t)\in L^{2}_{1}(\widehat{Y},i\mathbb{R})
such that

(\delta b(t),\delta\psi(t))=\bd_{\check{\gamma}(t)}\xi(t)\text{ on }\{t\}\times Y_{2}.

Moreover,

(16.11)

\frac{d}{dt}d_{Y_{2}}\xi(t)\equiv 0\text{ and }\Delta_{Y_{2}}\xi(t)+\xi(t)|\Psi(t)|^{2}=0\text{ on }[5/4,7/4]\times Y_{2}.

Recall that
\delta\omega_{3}=-\beta_{0}(t)dt\wedge d_{Y_{2}}f_{3}
for a compactly supported function
f_{3}:I\times Y_{2}\to i\mathbb{R}
, so

\rho_{4}(\delta\omega_{3}^{+})=\rho_{3}(d_{Y_{2}}(\beta_{0}(t)f_{3})).

If
U
is orthogonal to
\rho_{4}(\delta\omega_{3}^{+})
for any
\delta\omega_{3}\in\mathcal{P}_{\form}
, then
\Delta_{Y_{2}}\xi(t)\equiv 0
. By (
16.11
),
U(t)\equiv 0
on
[5/4,7/4]\times Y_{2}
. By unique continuation,
U\equiv 0
on the whole manifold
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Part VI
Floer Homology

Let
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\in\SCob_{s}
be an object in the strict
spin^{c}\
cobordism category, as defined in Section
3
. The underlying 3-manifold
Y
of
\mathbb{Y}
is compact, connected and oriented, whose boundary is identified with a disjoint union of 2-tori
\Sigma
by the diffeomorphism
\psi:\partial Y\to\Sigma
. The quintuple
\mathbb{Y}=(Y,\psi,g_{Y},\omega,\{\mathfrak{q}\})
also dictates a cylindrical metric
g_{Y}
and a closed 2-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(Y,i\mathbb{R})
.
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in\Spincr(Y)
is a relative
spin^{c}\
structure of the 3-manifold
Y
.

The primary goal of this part is to define the functor

\HM_{*}:\SCob_{s}\to\mathcal{R}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}

which assigns the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
for each object
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\in\SCob_{s}
, generalizing the construction of Kronheimer-Mrowka for closed 3-manifolds.

So far we have addressed two fundamental problems in order to define the functor
\HM_{*}
:

•

the compactness issue; see Theorem
6.1
for the unperturbed equations and Theorem
9.5
for the perturbed ones;

•

the transversality issue; see Theorem
16.1
for the case of cylinders and Theorem
16.5
for morphisms in
\SCob_{s}
.

Although the proof of the gluing theorem is omitted in this paper, it follows from the standard procedure in
[
KM07
, Section 17-19]
, as noted in Subsection
13.4
.

Now the construction of monopole Floer homology becomes straightforward by following the standard argument. Part
VI
is organized as follows. In Section
17
, we explain the basic construction using
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficient. Section
18
is devoted to the canonical grading as well as the canonical mod
2
grading of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
.

In Section
19
, we address the orientation issue, which allows us to define the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
using
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficient. The key ingredient is the notion of relative orientations, which compare the orientations of two Fredholm operators using the excision principle, cf. Theorem
19.2
and Definition
A.2
. The proof is postponed to Appendix
A
.

17.
The Basic Construction:
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficient

In this section, we define the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
for each object
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\in\SCob_{s}
using
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficient. For the most general case, we have to use a Novikov ring
\mathcal{R}_{2}
. To work with the field
\mathbb{F}_{2}
of two elements, we will pass to a subcategory of
\SCob_{s}
in which case a monotonicity condition is required.

17.1.
Novikov Rings

Let us first explain the construction of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
using a Novikov ring

\mathcal{R}_{2}=\{\sum_{n_{i}}a_{i}q^{n_{i}}:\ a_{i}\in\mathbb{F}_{2},\ n_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\ \lim_{i}n_{i}=-\infty\},

which is a complete topological group. Each element of
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is a Laurent series in a formal variable
q
with possibly infinitely many terms in negative degrees. For any object
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\in\SCob_{s}
, the perturbation
\mathfrak{q}=\grad f
encoded in the quintuple
\mathbb{Y}
is admissible in the sense of Definition
13.3
. Let
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
be the set of critical points of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}=\mathcal{L}_{\omega}+f
in the quotient configuration space
\mathcal{B}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, then
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is a finite set by Theorem
16.2
. Then the chain group
C_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is freely generated by
\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
over
\mathcal{R}_{2}
:

C_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})=\bigoplus_{[\mathfrak{a}]\in\mathfrak{C}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})}\mathcal{R}_{2}\cdot[\mathfrak{a}].

with differential
\partial
defined as

(17.1)

\partial[\mathfrak{a}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])\\
\dim\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])=0\end{subarray}}[\mathfrak{b}]\cdot\#\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])\cdot q^{-\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{q}}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}];z)}.

The unparameterized moduli space
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{M}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])/\mathbb{R}_{t}
is defined as in (
13.5
). The topological energy
\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{q}}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}];z)
for a homotopy class of paths
z\in\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
equals twice the drop of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
along
\gamma

2(\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\mathfrak{a})-\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\mathfrak{b}))

if
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is a lift of
z
with
\gamma(0)=\mathfrak{a}
and
\gamma(1)=\mathfrak{b}
. This expression is suggested by Proposition
9.1
. To ensure the sum in (
17.1
) is convergent in
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, we need a finiteness result:

Lemma 17.1
.

For any
C>0
, there are only finitely many homotopy classes of paths
z\in\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
such that
\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{q}}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}],z)<C
and
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
is non-empty. Moreover, each
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
is compact if its dimension equals zero.

To show
\partial^{2}=0
, we follow the standard argument and look at the compactification of moduli spaces
\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
when
\dim=1
. Readers are referred to
[
KM07
, Section 22]
for the details. The monopole Floer homology of
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is then defined as the homology of the chain complex
(C_{*}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}),\partial)
:

\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}H_{*}((C_{*}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}),\partial)).

To make
\HM_{*}
into a functor:

\HM_{*}:\SCob_{s}\to\mathcal{R}_{2}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod},

we assign for each morphism
\mathbb{X}:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\to\mathbb{Y}_{2}
a chain map:

m(\mathbb{X};g_{X},\mathfrak{p}):(C_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1}),\partial_{1})\to(C_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}),\partial_{2})

which relies on a planar metric
g_{X}
of the strict cobordism
X:Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
and a quadruple

\mathfrak{p}=(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2},\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3})\in\mathcal{P}(Y_{1})\times\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})\times\mathcal{P}(Y_{2})\times\mathcal{P}_{\form}.

Here
\mathfrak{p}
is required to be admissible in the sense of Definition
16.4
. While
(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2})
are encoded in the objects
(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\mathbb{Y}_{2})
,
(\mathfrak{q}_{3},\omega_{3})
are the actual perturbations to the Seiberg-Witten equations on the complete Riemannian 4-manifold
\mathcal{X}
. Now define

(17.2)

m(\mathbb{X};g_{X},\mathfrak{q})[\mathfrak{a}_{1}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spinc(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})\\
\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})=0\end{subarray}}[\mathfrak{a}_{2}]\cdot\#\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})\cdot q^{-\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})},

where
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is a lift of
[\mathfrak{a}_{i}]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y}_{i})
in
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
for
i=1,2
. The moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
is defined as in (
14.4
) with the admissible quadruple
\mathfrak{q}
as perturbations. The topological energy is given by the formula

(17.3)

\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}2\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1})-2\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{2}}(\mathfrak{a}_{2})+C(A_{0},\omega_{X})

where
A_{0}
is a background
spin^{c}\
connection on
\widehat{X}
such that the restriction
A_{0}|_{\widehat{Y}_{i}}
is the reference connection on
\widehat{Y}_{i}
that defines the Chern-Simons-Dirac functional
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{i}}
for
i=1,2
. The constant
C(A_{0},\omega_{X})
is given concretely by

(17.4)

C(A_{0},\omega_{X})=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\widehat{X}}F_{A^{t}_{0}}\wedge F_{A^{t}_{0}}-\int_{\widehat{X}}F_{A^{t}_{0}}\wedge\omega_{X},

as suggested by (
5.2
). To make sense of the expression (
17.2
), we need another finiteness result:

Lemma 17.2
.

For any
C>0
, any pair of critical points
([\mathfrak{a}_{1}],[\mathfrak{a}_{2}])\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y}_{1})\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y}_{2})
and any admissible quadruple
\mathfrak{p}
, there are only finitely many relative
spin^{c}\
cobordisms
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
such that
\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})<C
and
\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
is non-empty. Moreover, each moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
is compact if its dimension equals zero.

Lemma
17.1
and Lemma
17.2
follow from the Compactness Theorem
9.5
and its analogue for a general cobordism. Readers are referred to
[
KM07
, Corollary 31.2.5]
for more details; their proofs are omitted here. By analyzing the moduli space
\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
with
\dim=1
, we conclude that
m(\mathbb{X};g_{X},\mathfrak{q})
is a chain map by the standard argument. The chain maps induced from different auxiliary data
(g_{X},\mathfrak{q})
are all chain homotopic to each other, so the resulting maps on the homology are independent of
(g_{X},\mathfrak{q})

\HM(\mathbb{X})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[m(\mathbb{X};g_{X},\mathfrak{p})]:\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}),

To show that
\HM
defined this way is a functor and satisfies the composition law in Theorem
1.5
, we follow
[
KM07
, Section 26]
.

17.2.
Monotonicity

To define the monopole Floer homology using
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficient, it is necessary to pass to a subcategory of
\SCob_{s}
, as we explain in this subsection.

Definition 17.3
.

An object
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})=(Y,\psi,g_{Y},\omega,\mathfrak{q},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\in\SCob_{s}
is called monotone if the period class
[\omega]\in H^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
is proportional to the image of
c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
in
\im(H^{2}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R})
:

[\frac{\omega}{\pi i}]=\alpha\cdot c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{R})\text{ for some }\alpha\in\mathbb{R}.

In addition,
(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is called

•

positively monotone if
\alpha<1
;

•

balanced if
\alpha=1
;

•

negatively monotone if
\alpha>1
.∎

In light of Lemma
3.8
, under the monotonicity assumption, we have

\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(u\cdot\gamma)-\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\gamma)=2(1-\alpha)\pi^{2}[u]\cup c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}),

for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
. In particular,
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
becomes a real valued functional if
(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is balanced. One necessary condition of monotonicity is that
\mu=0
. The construction described below will work in general for any monotone objects, but let us focus on the special case when the period class
[\omega]=0\in H^{2}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
and the form
\overline{\omega}
defined in
(P6)
vanishes, for the sake of simplicity; so

\omega=\omega_{\lambda}=\chi_{1}(s)ds\wedge\lambda.

In this case,
(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is always positively monotone, since
\alpha=0
.

Under this assumption, the chain group
C_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}};\mathbb{F}_{2})
is a finite dimensional
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector space:

C_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}};\mathbb{F}_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{[\mathfrak{a}]\in\mathfrak{C}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})}\mathbb{F}_{2}\cdot[\mathfrak{a}].

with differential defined by

(17.5)

\partial[\mathfrak{a}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}});[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])\\
\dim\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])=0\end{subarray}}[\mathfrak{b}]\cdot\#\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])

In light of Lemma
17.1
, to make sense of this expression, we need an upper bound on the topological energy
\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{q}}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}];z)
:

Lemma 17.4
.

For any
[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}]\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{q}}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}];z)<C,

for any homotopy classes of paths
z\in\pi_{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}),[\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])
with
\dim\widecheck{\mathcal{M}}_{z}([\mathfrak{a}],[\mathfrak{b}])=0
.

As for a morphism
\mathbb{X}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
with
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}=\omega_{\lambda}
,
\overline{\omega}_{X}
is a compactly supported 2-form (see
•
‣
(Q5)
) on
X
. We require that the class defined in
(Q6)
vanishes:
[\omega_{X}]_{cpt}=0\in H^{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
. This time the chain map
m(\mathbb{X};g_{X},\mathfrak{q})
is defined as

\displaystyle m(\mathbb{X};g_{X},\mathfrak{q}):C_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1};\mathbb{F}_{2})

\displaystyle\to C_{*}(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2};\mathbb{F}_{2})

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{a}_{1}]

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spinc(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})\\
\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})=0\end{subarray}}[\mathfrak{a}_{2}]\cdot\#\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2}).

Again, we need a upper bound on
\mathcal{E}_{top}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
to ensure the sum in the expression above is finite:

Lemma 17.5
.

Under above assumptions, for any pair of critical points
([\mathfrak{a}_{1}],[\mathfrak{a}_{2}])\in\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\times\mathfrak{C}(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
, any planar metric
g_{X}
and any admissible quadruple
\mathfrak{p}
, there is a constant
C>0
such that

\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{p}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})<C

which holds for any
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
with
\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})=0
.

Lemma
17.4
and
17.5
follow directly from a general statement relating the dimension with the topological energy
\mathcal{E}_{top}
. In Proposition
17.6
below, we will think of a homotopy class of paths as a relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism, following the ideas in Subsection
3.5
.

Proposition 17.6
.

Under above assumptions, for any relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{top}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}_{2})-\mathcal{E}_{top}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})=-4\pi^{2}\big(\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}_{2})-\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})\big)

In particular, the topological energy
\mathcal{E}_{top}^{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
is independent of the choice of
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
if
\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})=0
.

Proof.

Suppose
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime}=\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\otimes L
for a relative complex line bundle in the class
[L]\in H^{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
. In terms of (
17.3
) and (
17.4
), we compute the difference of the topological energy

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{top}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}_{2})-\mathcal{E}_{top}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})

\displaystyle=C(A_{0}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime}),\omega_{X})-C(A_{0}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}),\omega_{X})

\displaystyle=-2\pi^{2}[L]\cup(c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})+c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime}))[X,\partial X]

\displaystyle=-4\pi^{2}[L]\cup(c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})+[L])[X,\partial X].

where
c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})
and
\ c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime})
are understood as elements in
H^{2}(X,[-1,1]\times\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
. On the other hand, pick an arbitrary non-vanishing section
\Phi_{0}
of

S^{+}|_{\partial X}\to\partial X.

Any relative
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
dictates an identification of
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}|_{\partial X}
with a standard
spin^{c}\
structure on the boundary
\partial X
, so it makes sense to define the relative Euler number
e(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X};\Phi_{0})[X,\partial X]
for any non-vanishing section
\Phi_{0}
of the spin bundle
S^{+}\to\partial X
. In particular,

\big(e(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime};\Phi_{0})-e(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X};\Phi_{0})\big)[X,\partial X]=[L]\cup(c_{1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})+[L])[X,\partial X].

In Proposition
18.6
below, we will associate a homotopy class of non-vanishing sections
[\Phi_{0}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2})]
to any pair
(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
such that

(17.6)

e(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X};\Phi_{0}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathfrak{a}_{2}))[X,\partial X]=\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})

for any
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
. In fact, (
17.6
) follows from the Index Axiom
(A-I)
of the canonical grading of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. Another approach is to show

\big(e(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime};\Phi_{0})-e(\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X};\Phi_{0})\big)[X,\partial X]=\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}^{\prime},\mathfrak{a}_{2})-\dim\mathcal{M}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})

for any non-vanishing section
\Phi_{0}
directly using the excision principle. This completes the proof of Proposition
17.6

∎

Finally, one has to verify that
m(\mathbb{X};g_{X},\mathfrak{q})
is a chain map and a generic homotopy of auxiliary data
(g_{X},\mathfrak{q})
gives rise to a chain homotopy of
m(\mathbb{X};g_{X},\mathfrak{q})
. The argument is not different from that of
[
KM07
, Section 25]
.

18.
Canonical Gradings

In this section, we introduce the canonical grading of the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. It is more natural to think of the grading set of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})

\Xi^{\pi}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})

as the space of unit-length relative spinors on
\widehat{Y}
modulo gauge transformations, identified also as a subset of homotopy classes of oriented relative 2-plane fields on
Y
. In particular,

\Xi^{\pi}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})=\Xi^{\pi}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})

if
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}
come down to the same
spin^{c}\
structure on
Y
.

The main result of this section is Proposition
18.6
, which characterizes the canonical grading in terms of the Index Axiom
(A-I)
and the Normalization Axiom
(A-II)
. They are inspired by the following index computation for a closed Riemannian 4-manifold
X
:

\dim\mathcal{M}(X,\mathfrak{s}_{X})=e(\mathfrak{s}_{X})[X]

where
\mathcal{M}(X,\mathfrak{s}_{X})
is the Seiberg-Witten moduli space and
e(\mathfrak{s}_{X})
is the Euler class of the spin bundle
S_{X}^{+}\to X
. The canonical mod 2 grading will be discussed in Subsection
18.2
.

18.1.
Homotopy Classes of Oriented Relative 2-Plane Fields

For a closed
3
-manifold
Y
, recall that the three flavors of monopoles Floer homology:

\widecheck{\HM}_{\bullet}(Y),\ \widehat{\HM}_{\bullet}(Y),\ \overline{\HM}_{\bullet}(Y)

defined in the book
[
KM07
]
are graded by the homotopy classes of oriented 2-plane fields over
Y
. The analogous statement continues to hold in our case, using
relative
oriented 2-plane fields instead, as we explain now. The following lemma from
[
KM07
]
explains the relationship between 2-plane fields and
spin^{c}\
structures:

Lemma 18.1

(
[
KM07
]
Lemma 28.1.1)
.

On an oriented Riemannian 3-manifold
Y
, there is a bijection between

(i)

oriented
2
-plane fields
\xi
;

(ii)

1-forms
\theta
of length
1
; and

(iii)

isomorphism classes of pairs
(\mathfrak{s},\Psi)
comprising a
spin^{c}\
structure and a unit-length spinor
\Psi
.

Over the infinite cylinder
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
, we defined in (
2.6
) a preferred
\mathbb{R}_{s}
-translation invariant solution

\gamma_{*}=(B_{*},\Psi_{*})

to the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations
(
3.6
)
. The perturbation is provided by a covariantly constant 2-form

\omega_{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mu+ds\wedge\lambda

The correspondence in Lemma
18.1
then identifies

(18.1)

\text{the unit length 1-form }\theta_{*}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}i*_{3}\frac{\omega_{*}}{|\omega_{*}|}\leftrightarrow\text{the unit length spinor }\frac{\Psi_{*}}{|\Psi_{*}|},

Indeed, as
\gamma_{*}
solves the equations (
3.6
),
(\Psi_{*}\Psi_{*}^{*})_{0}=\rho_{3}(*_{3}\omega_{*})
, so

\mathbb{C}\Psi_{*}\text{ and }\mathbb{C}(\Psi_{*})^{\perp}

are
i
and
-i
eigenspaces of
\rho_{3}(\theta_{*})
respectively. In particular, (
18.1
) determines a preferred oriented 2-plane fields
\xi_{*}
on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
by Lemma
18.1
. Now we return to a 3-manifold
\widehat{Y}
with cylindrical ends and state a relative version of Lemma
18.1
.

Definition 18.2
.

An oriented 2-plane field
\xi
on
\widehat{Y}
is called
relative
if
\xi
agrees with
\xi_{*}
over the cylindrical end
[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
. Similarly, we define

•

relative
1-forms and

•

relative
spinors

using
\theta_{*}
and
\Psi_{*}/|\Psi_{*}|
as the models along the end
[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
.
∎

Lemma 18.3
.

For any object
\mathbb{Y}\in\Cob_{s}
, let
\widehat{Y}
be the extended 3-manifold with cylindrical ends. Then there is a bijection between:

(i)

oriented relative
2
-plane fields
\xi
;

(ii)

1-forms relative
\theta
of length
1
; and

(iii)

isomorphism classes of pairs
(\mathfrak{s},\Psi)
consisting of a
spin^{c}\
structure
\mathfrak{s}
with
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})|_{\Sigma}=0\in H^{2}(\Sigma,\mathbb{Z})
and a unit-length spinor
\Psi
that is gauge equivalent to a relative spinor.

Remark 18.4
.

In the last description, the identification of
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}|_{\Sigma}
is not specified and a gauge transformation does not necessarily lie in the identity component when restricted to
\Sigma
.
∎

For each relative
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in\Spincr(Y)
, let
\Xi(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
be the space of unit-length relative spinors on
\widehat{Y}
. The index set for the monopole Floer homology
\HM_{*}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
will be

(18.2)

\Xi^{\pi}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\pi_{0}(\Xi(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))/H^{1}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})

where
H^{1}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{Z})=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{G}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))
acts on
\pi_{0}(\Xi(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))
by gauge transformations. The last description in Lemma
18.3
suggests that

\Xi^{\pi}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\cong\Xi^{\pi}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})

if
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}
come down to the same
spin^{c}\
structure on
Y
. In this way,
\Xi^{\pi}(Y,\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is identified with a subset of homotopy classes of oriented relative 2-plane fields.

Now let us introduce the axioms that characterize the canonical grading of
\HM_{*}(\mathbb{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
.

Definition 18.5
.

For any configuration
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
and any tame perturbation
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{P}(Y)
, the pair
\mathfrak{c}=(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
is called
non-degenerate
if the extended Hessian
\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q}}
is invertible.
∎

For any non-degenerate pair
\mathfrak{c}=(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
, we will assign an element

\gr(\mathfrak{c})\in\pi_{0}(\Xi(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})).

which descends to a map

(18.3)

\gr^{\pi}:(\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\times\mathcal{P})/\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})\dashrightarrow\Xi^{\pi}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}),[\mathfrak{c}]\mapsto[\gr(\mathfrak{c})],

on the “non-degenerate locus” of the quotient space. To state the axioms that characterize the grading function
\gr
, consider a relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism

(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}).

We defined the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{k}(\mathfrak{a}_{1},\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{a}_{2})
in Section
14
, when
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is a critical point of
\hbox to0.0pt{$-$\hss}\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{i},Y_{i}}
for
i=1,2
. However, if we are interested only in the linear theory, one may take
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{2}
to be any configurations. Pick a reference configuration
\gamma
on
\mathcal{X}
satisfying conditions (
14.1
). Then the linearized operator:

(18.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\bd_{\gamma}^{*},\mathcal{D}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{F}_{\mathcal{X},\mathfrak{p}}):L^{2}_{1}(\mathcal{X},iT^{*}\mathcal{X}\oplus S^{+})\to L^{2}(\mathcal{X},i\mathbb{R}\oplus i\Lambda^{+}\mathcal{X}\oplus S^{-})

\displaystyle\text{ with }\mathfrak{p}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\mathfrak{q}_{2},0,0)

is Fredholm, by Proposition
14.1
, provided that
\mathfrak{c}_{i}=(\mathfrak{a}_{i},\mathfrak{q}_{i})
is non-degenerate for
i=1,2
. Any such choices of
\gamma
will provide the same operator
\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
up to compact terms, so the underlying path
\gamma
is omitted from our notations.

Now we are ready to state the axioms that characterize the grading function
\gr
.

(A-I)

(Index Axiom) The Fredholm index of
\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
equals the relative Euler number:

e(S^{+};\Psi_{1},\Psi_{*}/|\Psi_{*}|,\Psi_{2})[X,\partial X]\in\mathbb{Z}.

where
\Psi_{i}
is a unit-length relative spinor on
\widehat{Y}_{i}
representing
\gr(\mathfrak{c}_{i})
. Since
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{*}/|\Psi_{*}|
and
\Psi_{2}
form a unit-length spinor of
S^{+}
on the boundary

\partial X=(-Y_{1})\cup[-1,1]_{t}\times\Sigma\cup Y_{2},

the relative Euler class

e(S^{+};\Psi_{1},\Psi_{*}/|\Psi_{*}|,\Psi_{2})\in H^{4}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
of this spinor is well-defined.

(A-II)

(Normalization Axiom) Suppose
\mathfrak{a}=(B,\Psi)\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
is a configuration such that

(V1)

\Psi
is nowhere vanishing;

(V2)

\Psi\equiv\Psi_{*}
on
[0,+\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
, where
\Psi_{*}
is the standard spinor on
\mathbb{R}_{s}\times\Sigma
;

(V3)

for any
\tau\geq 1
, define the rescaled configuration
\mathfrak{a}(\tau)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(B,\tau\Psi)
; then the extended Hessian
\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{a}(\tau)}
at
\mathfrak{a}(\tau)
is always invertible for any
\tau\geq 1
.

We define that

\gr(\mathfrak{c})=[\Psi/|\Psi|]\in\pi_{0}(\Xi(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))\text{ if }\mathfrak{c}=(\mathfrak{a},0).

Note that
\mathfrak{a}(\tau)
lies in a different configuration space obtained by rescaling the boundary date
(\lambda,\mu)
.

(A-III)

(Equivariance Axiom) The grading function

\gr:\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\times\mathcal{P}\dashrightarrow\pi_{0}(\Xi(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))

is equivariant under the action of
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
meaning that

\gr(u\cdot\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})=[u]\cdot\gr(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})

for any non-generate pair
(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
and
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
.

The Index Axiom
(A-I)
can not determine the grading function
\gr
completely. On the other hand, the Equivariance Axiom
(A-III)
is redundant, since it follows from
(A-I)
(A-II)
. It is added to justify the quotient map
\gr^{\pi}
in (
18.3
). Here is the main result of this section:

Proposition 18.6
.

There exists a unique grading function

\gr:\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\times\mathcal{P}\dashrightarrow\Xi(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})

satisfying axioms
(A-I)
(A-II)
(A-III)
.

The proof of Proposition
18.6
will dominate the rest of this subsection. It relies on two additional lemmas. On the one hand, we have to show the desired configurations in the Normalization Axiom
(A-II)
exist at least for some special metrics on
Y
.

Lemma 18.7
.

For any
3
-manifold
Y
with
\partial Y\cong\Sigma
, there exists some cylindrical metric
g_{Y}
and a configuration
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
that satisfies all constraints in Axiom
(A-II)
.

On the other hand, we have to show that Axioms
(A-I)
and
(A-II)
are consistent.

Lemma 18.8
.

For any relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}),
suppose non-generate pairs
\mathfrak{c}_{i}=(\mathfrak{a}_{i},0),i=1,2
are given as in
(A-II)
, then

\Ind\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})=e(S^{+};\frac{\Psi_{1}}{|\Psi_{1}|},\frac{\Psi_{*}}{|\Psi_{*}|},\frac{\Psi_{2}}{|\Psi_{2}|})[X,\partial X],

where
\Psi_{i}\in\Gamma(\widehat{Y}_{i},S)
is the spinor component of
\mathfrak{a}_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})
.

Proof of Lemma
18.8
.

This lemma is in the spirit of
[
KM97
, Theorem 3.3]
and we follow the argument therein. When
X_{1}
is a closed Riemannian 4-manifold, the index formula:

\dim\mathcal{M}(X_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X_{1}})=e(S^{+})[X_{1}]

is a consequence of the Atiyah-Singer Index Theorem and
[
KM07
, Lemma 28.2.3]
. Using the excision principle, this allows us to reduce Lemma
18.8
to the special case when

e(S^{+};\Psi_{1},\Psi_{*},\Psi_{2})[X,\partial X]=0.

At this point, choose a reference configuration
\gamma=(A,\Phi)
on
\mathcal{X}
such that the spinor
\Phi
is non-vanishing everywhere, and

\gamma|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{+}\times\Sigma}=(A_{*},\Phi_{*})

is the standard configuration on the planar end. By rescaling the spinor
\Phi
, we define

\gamma(\tau)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(A,\tau\Phi).

which lies a different configuration space on
\mathcal{X}
. As the pair
\mathfrak{c}_{i}(\tau)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\mathfrak{a}_{i}(\tau),\mathfrak{q}_{i}=0),i=1,2
are non-degenerate for any
\tau\geq 1
by assumption
(A-II)(V3)
, the linearized operator at
\gamma(\tau)
gives rise to a continuous family of Fredholm operators:

\mathcal{Q}(\tau)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1}(\tau),\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2}(\tau)).

The proof of
[
KM97
, Lemma 3.11 & Corollary 3.12]
is valid here, as
\mathfrak{q}_{i}=0,i=1,2
. As a result,
\mathcal{Q}(\tau)
is invertible when
\tau\gg 1
; so

\Ind\mathcal{Q}(1)=\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\Ind\mathcal{Q}(\tau)=0.\qed

Proof of Lemma
18.7
.

Following the proof of Lemma
18.8
, one can easily show the extended Hessian
\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{a}(\tau)}
is invertible when
\tau\gg 1
for any fixed configuration
\mathfrak{a}=(B,\Psi)
satisfying properties
(A-II)(V1)
and
(A-II)(V2)
, but we have to pick a good metric on
\widehat{Y}
so that this range is
[1,+\infty)
.

If
Y_{1}
is a closed 3-manifold, one may instead rescale the metric:

Y_{1}(\tau)=(Y_{1},\tau^{2}g_{Y_{1}}).

and regard
\mathfrak{a}
as a configuration on the pull-back
spin^{c}\
structure on
Y(\tau)
. The Seiberg-Witten theory does not tell the difference between:

(Y(\tau),\mathfrak{a})\text{ and }(Y,\mathfrak{a}(\tau)),

so for
\tau_{0}\gg 1
,
(Y(\tau_{0}),\mathfrak{a})
satisfies constraints
(A-II)(V1)
(A-II)(V3)
in Axiom
(A-II)
.

In our case, instead of rescaling the whole manifold

\widehat{Y}=Y\cup[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma,

we rescale the compact region
Y
and insert a long cylinder:

\widehat{Y}(\tau)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}Y(\tau)\cup[0,R(\tau)]_{s}\times\Sigma\cup[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma.

The metric of
[0,R(\tau)]_{s}\times\Sigma
interpolates the metrics
\tau^{2}g_{\Sigma}
and
g_{\Sigma}
at boundary. We make this interpolation mild enough by taking
R(\tau)\gg 1
. The extension of
\mathfrak{a}
over the cylinder
[0,R(\tau)]_{s}\times\Sigma
:

(B^{\prime},\Psi^{\prime})

must interpolate
(B_{*},\Psi_{*})
at boundary in a mild way. One may use the oriented relative 2-plane field
\xi_{*}
and construct the spinor
\Psi^{\prime}
using Lemma
18.1
. Now
[
KM97
, Lemma 3.11]
applies, and all constraints in
(A-II)
are satisfied by

(\widehat{Y}(\tau_{0}),\tilde{\mathfrak{a}})

when
\tau_{0}\gg 1
, where
\tilde{\mathfrak{a}}
is the extension of
\mathfrak{a}
on
\widehat{Y}(\tau_{0})
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
18.6
.

The proof is modeled on that of
[
KM07
, Subsection 28.2]
which can now proceed with no difficulties. We first deal with the existence of
\gr
and divide the proof in six steps.

Step
1. Construction. Fix a reference relative
spin^{c}\
3-manifold
(\widehat{Y}_{0},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{0})
. Let
\mathfrak{c}_{0}=(\mathfrak{a}_{0},0)
be a non-generate pair constructed by Lemma
18.7
, then the value
\gr(\mathfrak{c})
is determined by
(A-II)
. Take
\Psi_{0}
as a unit-length relative spinor on
\widehat{Y}_{0}
that represents
\gr(\mathfrak{c})
.

By
[
KM07
, Proposition 28.1.2]
, any two relative
spin^{c}\
manifolds
(\widehat{Y}_{0},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{0})
and
(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})
admit a relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})

(18.5)

(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y}_{0},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{0})\to(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})

The Index Axiom
(A-I)
then determines a unique homotopy class
[\Psi_{1}]
of unit-length relative spinors on
\widehat{Y}_{1}
such that

\Ind\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{0},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{1})=e(S^{+};\Psi_{0},\Psi_{*}/|\Psi_{*}|,\Psi_{1})[X,\partial X].

As noted in Remark
3.13
, an isomorphism

\varphi_{1}:(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})|_{\widehat{Y}_{i}}\cong(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})

is always encoded in a relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism. Define
\gr(\mathfrak{c}_{1})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(\varphi_{1})_{*}[\Psi_{1}]\in\pi_{0}(\Xi(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1}))
.

Step
2.
\gr
is well-defined. Suppose there is another relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism

(18.6)

(\widehat{X}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X_{1}}):(\widehat{Y}_{0},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{0})\to(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1}),

then we reverse the orientation of
(\widehat{X}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X_{1}})
and form the composition:

(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})\#_{(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})}((-\widehat{X}_{1}),\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{-X_{1}}):(\widehat{Y}_{0},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{0})\to(\widehat{Y}_{0},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{0}).

By Lemma
18.8
and the additivity of Fredholm indices and relative Euler classes, the values of
\gr(\mathfrak{c}_{1})
defined using either (
18.5
) or (
18.6
) are equal.

Step
3. Axiom
(A-I)
holds for
\gr
. The proof is similar to
Step
2. Instead of (
18.6
), given any
spin^{c}\
cobordism
(\widehat{X}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X_{2}}):(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
, we take the pre-composition with (
18.5
):

(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})\#_{(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})}(\widehat{X}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X_{2}}):(\widehat{Y}_{0},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{0})\to(\widehat{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}).

The rest of the argument is unchanged.

Step
4. Axiom
(A-II)
holds for
\gr
. This is by Lemma
18.8
.

Step
5. Uniqueness. This is clear from
Step
1.

Step
6. Axiom
(A-III)
. There are two ways to proceed. In
Step
1, one may change the isomorphism
\phi_{1}
by an automorphism of
(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})
, i.e a gauge transformation
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
. As a result, the grading function
\gr
is gauge equivariant.

In the second approach, we verify the following fact: for the product manifold
X=[-1,1]_{t}\times Y
and
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}_{t}\times\widehat{Y}
,

(18.7)

\Ind\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}},u\cdot\mathfrak{c})=e(S^{+};\Psi,\Psi_{*},u\cdot\Psi)[X,\partial X].

for any non-generate pair
\mathfrak{c}
and any gauge transformation
u\in\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
such that
u\equiv 1
on
[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
. Here
\Psi
is a relative spinor on
\widehat{Y}
representing
\gr(\mathfrak{c})
. The identity (
18.7
) now follows from Lemma
13.16
.
∎

18.2.
Canonical Mod 2 Gradings

Now we focus a single relative
spin^{c}\
3-manifold
(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
. In order to define the Euler characteristic of the monopole Floer homology

\chi(\HM_{*}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}))

we need a mod 2 reduction of the canonical grading
\gr^{\pi}
. For each non-generate pair
\mathfrak{c}=(\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{q})
, in the sense of Definition
18.5
, we will assign a number

(18.8)

\grt(\mathfrak{c})\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z},

characterized by the following axioms:

(B-I)

(Reduction Axiom) Let
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X})=[-1,1]_{t}\times(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
be the product
spin^{c}\
manifold. For any
\mathfrak{c}_{1},\mathfrak{c}_{2}
non-generate, we have

\grt(\mathfrak{c}_{1})-\grt(\mathfrak{c}_{2})=\text{Ind}\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})\mod 2,

(B-II)

(Invariance Axiom) The mod 2 grading function

\grt:\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\times\mathcal{P}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

is invariant under the action of
\mathcal{G}_{k+1}(\widehat{Y})
.

Again, the Invariance Axiom
(B-II)
is redundant, as it follows from
(B-I)
. One may fix the value
\grt(\mathfrak{c}_{1})
for one particular pair
\mathfrak{c}_{1}
and decide the other value
\grt(\mathfrak{c}_{2})
using the Reduction Axiom
(B-I)
, so such a mod 2 grading function
\grt
clearly exists. It is not unique, as the value of
\grt(\mathfrak{c}_{1})
is arbitrary.

This ambiguity is fixed
simultaneously
for all relative
spin^{c}\
structures
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}\in\Spincr(Y)
, once
a homological orientation
of
(Y,\partial Y)
is chosen, as explained in
[
MT96
]
, which is also reminiscent of the case of 4-manifolds as treated in
[
KM07
, Subsection 24.8]
. Since this story has been standard nowadays, we only give a brief sketch here.

One may alternatively think of
\grt(\mathfrak{c})
as an orientation of the extended Hessian

\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{c}}.

As
\mathfrak{c}
is non-generate, an orientation of this invertible operator
\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{c}}
is equivalent to a choice of signs in
\{\pm 1\}
. However, this standpoint allows us to extend the domain of
\grt
to the whose space
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\times\mathcal{P}
. Indeed,
\{\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{c}}\}
forms a continuous family of Fredholm operators, and as such gives rise to a determinant line bundle over the base:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}\cong\det\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\times\mathcal{P}.}

The real line bundle
L
is trivial as
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})\times\mathcal{P}
is contractible. To orient
L
, it suffices to orient one particular fiber
L_{\mathfrak{c}}
; we choose the one at
\mathfrak{c}=(\mathfrak{a},0)
such that
\mathfrak{a}
agrees with the standard configuration:

(B_{*},\Psi_{*})

on the cylindrical end
[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
. As explained in the proof of Proposition
12.1
, the extended Hessian
\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{a}}
in this case is cast into the form

\sigma(\partial_{s}+\widehat{D}_{\kappa_{*}})

on the cylindrical end
[0,\infty)_{s}\times\Sigma
, where

(18.9)

\widehat{D}_{\kappa_{*}}:L^{2}_{1}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R}\oplus i\mathbb{R}\oplus T^{*}\Sigma\oplus S)\to L^{2}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R}\oplus i\mathbb{R}\oplus T^{*}\Sigma\oplus S)

is an invertible self-adjoint elliptic operator. For the precise expression, see
[
Wan20a
, Subsection 7.4]
. Let
H^{\pm}
be the
(\pm)
-spectral subspaces of
\widehat{D}_{\kappa_{*}}
. Instead of
\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{a}}
, we consider the operator with a spectral boundary projection:

(18.10)

\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{a}}\oplus\Pi^{-}\circ r:L^{2}_{k}(Y,i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}Y\oplus S)\to L^{2}_{k-1}(Y,i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}Y\oplus S)\oplus(H^{-}\cap L^{2}_{k-1/2}).

on the truncated 3-manifold
Y=\{s\leq 0\}
. At this point, we can further deform
\mathfrak{a}
so that
\Psi\equiv 0
, in which case

\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{a}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-d&0\\
-d^{*}&*d&0\\
0&0&D_{B_{0}}\end{pmatrix}\text{ on }Y

for a reference
spin^{c}\
connection
B_{0}
, and

\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{a}}=\sigma(\partial_{s}+\hat{D}_{0})\text{ with }D_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}D_{\form}&0\\
0&D_{\check{B}_{*}}^{\Sigma}\end{pmatrix}

in the collar
(-1,0]_{s}\times\Sigma
. Here

D_{\form}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&-*_{\Sigma}d_{\Sigma}\\
0&0&-d_{\Sigma}^{*}\\
*_{\Sigma}d_{\Sigma}&-d_{\Sigma}&0\end{pmatrix}:L^{2}_{1}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R}\oplus i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\Sigma)\to L^{2}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R}\oplus i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}\Sigma)

is a self-adjoint operator with kernel
H^{0}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R})\oplus H^{0}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R})\oplus H^{1}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R})
and

D_{\check{B}_{*}}^{\Sigma}:L^{2}_{1}(\Sigma,S)\to L^{2}(\Sigma,S)

is the Dirac operator on the surface, which is complex linear. Consider the projection map

\Pi_{\form}=\Pi_{1}\oplus\Pi_{\form}^{-}:L^{2}(\Sigma)\to H^{1}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R})\oplus H^{-}_{\form}.

where
\Pi_{\form}
is the projection map onto the negative spectral subspace of
D_{\form}
and
\Pi_{1}
is the projection onto
H^{1}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R})\subset\ker D_{\form}
.

Lemma 18.9
.

The kernel and the cokernel of the operator:

(18.11)

\begin{pmatrix}0&-d\\
-d^{*}&*d\end{pmatrix}\oplus(\Pi_{\form}\circ r):L^{2}_{1}(\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}Y)\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}Y)\oplus H^{1}(\Sigma,i\mathbb{R})\oplus H^{-}_{\form}.

are isomorphic to
H^{0}(Y;i\mathbb{R})\oplus H^{1}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{R})
and
H^{0}(Y,\partial Y;\mathbb{R})\oplus H^{1}(Y;i\mathbb{R})
respectively. In particular, an orientation of (
18.11
) is equivalent to a homological orientation of
(Y,\partial Y)
.

Finally, to relate the operator (
18.10
) with (
18.11
), we have to deform the boundary projection
\Pi^{-}
in (
18.10
). Notice that the operator
\widehat{D}_{\kappa_{*}}
in (
18.9
) relies on the standard spinor
\Psi_{*}
. The deformation is then made by taking

\Psi_{*}\mapsto\tau\Psi_{*},\tau\to 0.

In the limit,
\widehat{D}_{\kappa_{*}}
will recover
\hat{D}_{0}
, which is no longer invertible. At this point, one has to examine the deformation of spectral projections very carefully, which is independent of relative
spin^{c}\
structures. In this way, an orientation of (
18.11
) gives rise to an orientation of
L
.

19.
Floer Homology with
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficient

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the Novikov ring of Laurent series with integral coefficients

\mathcal{R}=\{\sum_{n_{i}}a_{i}q^{n_{i}}:\ a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z},\ n_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\ \lim_{i}n_{i}=-\infty\}.

To define the monopole Floer homology over
\mathcal{R}
, we have to orient moduli spaces in a consistent way. Since the space
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}})
does not contain any reducible configurations, the strategy used in
[
KM07
, Section 20]
does not work directly here. Moreover, our cobordism maps are induced from oriented 4-manifold with corners. It is not crystal clear what is meant to be a homology orientation in this case.

We will address this problem using an analytic approach. The main result of this section is Theorem
19.2
, which leads to the replacement of homology orientations in Definition
19.4
. The proof of Theorem
19.2
relies on the notion of relative orientations that compares the determinant line bundles of two Fredholm operators in the excision principle. We will develop the relevant theory in Appendix
A
and accomplish the proof of Theorem
19.2
in Subsection
A.10
. The construction of the functor

\HM_{*}:\SCob_{s,b}\to\mathcal{R}\text{-}\mathrm{Mod}

is explained in Subsection
19.3
.

19.1.
Determinant Line Bundles and Direct Sums

To start, let us recall the basic theory of determinant line bundles of Fredholm operators from
[
KM07
, Section 20.2]
. Given two real Hilbert spaces
E
and
F
, consider a continuous family of Fredholm operators

\mathbb{A}_{z}:E\to F,\ z\in\mathcal{Z},

parametrized by a topological space
\mathcal{Z}
.
The determinant line bundle
of this family is a real line bundle over
\mathcal{Z}

\det\mathbb{A}\to\mathcal{Z}

such that the fiber
\det\mathbb{A}_{z}
at each
z\in\mathcal{Z}
is identified with

\Lambda^{\max}\ker\mathbb{A}_{z}\otimes(\Lambda^{\max}\coker\mathbb{A}_{z})^{*}.

When the determinant line bundle
\det\mathbb{A}\to\mathcal{Z}
is orientable, denote the 2-element set of orientations by

\Lambda(\mathbb{A})\text{ or }\Lambda(\det\mathbb{A}).

Example 19.1
.

Let
\mathbb{A}_{*}:E\to F
be a reference Fredholm operator and
\mathcal{Z}
be the space of all compact operators:

\mathcal{Z}=\{z:E\to F:z\text{ compact}\}.

Then the family
\{\mathbb{A}_{z}=\mathbb{A}_{*}+z:z\in\mathcal{Z}\}
is parametrized by a contractible space
\mathcal{Z}
. An orientation of
\mathbb{A}_{*}
is meant to be an orientation of this contractible family. Denote the 2-element set of orientations by

\Lambda(\mathbb{A}_{*})\text{ or }\Lambda(\det\mathbb{A}_{*}).\qed

Given two families of operators
\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathcal{Z}
parametrized by the same space, we form a new family by taking the point-wise direct sum of Fredholm operators

\mathbb{A}_{z}=\mathbb{A}_{z}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}_{z}:E^{\prime}\oplus E^{\prime\prime}\to F^{\prime}\oplus F^{\prime\prime}.

Then there is a natural isomorphism of real line bundles constructed in
[
KM07
, P.379]
:

(19.1)

q:\det\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\otimes\det\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}\to\det\mathbb{A}.

Suppose
\alpha_{z}^{\prime}
and
\alpha_{z}^{\prime\prime}
are elements in
\Lambda^{\max}\ker\mathbb{A}^{\prime}_{z}
and
\Lambda^{\max}\ker\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}_{z}
respectively, while
\beta_{z}^{\prime}
and
\beta_{z}^{\prime\prime}
are corresponding elements in
\Lambda^{\max}\coker\mathbb{A}^{\prime}_{z}
and
\Lambda^{\max}\coker\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}_{z}
. Then the bundle map
q
is locally defined (up to a positive scalar) by the formula:

\displaystyle\big(\alpha^{\prime}_{z}\otimes(\beta^{\prime}_{z})^{*}\big)\otimes\big(\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{z}\otimes(\beta^{\prime\prime}_{z})^{*}\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto(-1)^{r}(\alpha^{\prime}_{z}\wedge\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{z})\otimes(\beta^{\prime}_{z}\wedge\beta^{\prime\prime}_{z})^{*}\text{ where }

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\dim\coker\mathbb{A}^{\prime}_{z}\times\text{Ind}(\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}_{z}).

The sign
(-1)^{r}
is added here to ensure that the bundle map
q
is continuous as the base point
z
varies in
\mathcal{Z}
. Moreover, the bundle map
q
becomes associative when we consider the direct sum of three families of operators parametrized by the same space
\mathcal{Z}
.

For any 2-element set
\Lambda
, let
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
act on
\Lambda
by involutions. For any
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
with
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
action, we form their product set

\Lambda_{1}\Lambda_{2}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Lambda_{1}\times_{\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda_{2}.

As a result, by passing to the 2-element sets of orientations, the bundle map
q
descends to an associative multiplication, denoted also by
q
:

q:\Lambda(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\times\Lambda(\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime})\to\Lambda(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}),

or an isomorphism preserving the
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-action:

q:\Lambda(\mathbb{A}^{\prime})\Lambda(\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime})\xrightarrow{\cong}\Lambda(\mathbb{A}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{A}^{\prime\prime}).

19.2.
Homology Orientations

Having discussed the abstract properties of determinant line bundles, let us explain now the primary application in gauge theory. Given a morphism
\mathbb{X}:(\mathbb{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\mathbb{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
in the strict cobordism category
\SCob_{s}
, consider non-degenerate pairs (in the sense of Definition
18.5
)

\mathfrak{c}_{i}=(\mathfrak{a}_{i},\mathfrak{q}_{i})\in\mathcal{C}_{k}(\widehat{Y}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{i})\times\mathcal{P}(Y_{i}),i=1,2.

By looking at the linearized Seiberg-Witten map and the linearized gauge fixing equation on the complete Riemannian 4-manifold
\mathcal{X}
, we obtained in
(
18.4
)
a Fredholm operator
\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
for any relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism
(\widehat{X},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}):(\widehat{Y}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})\to(\widehat{Y}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
. Define

\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Lambda(\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2}))

for any non-degenerate pairs
\mathfrak{c}_{1},\mathfrak{c}_{2}
and any
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
. The 2-element set
\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
is understood in the sense of Example
19.1
. Since the different choices of the reference configuration
\gamma
will give rise to the same operator
\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
up to compact terms,
\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
is independent of the choice of
\gamma
.

Our goal is to identify these 2-element sets
\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
in a canonical way for all relative
spin^{c}\
cobordisms
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
. As a result, if the orientation is fixed for one particular
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}
, then it automatically fixes the choice for any other relative
spin^{c}\
cobordisms.

Recall that
\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
is a torsor over
H^{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
.

Theorem 19.2
.

For any isomorphism classe of relative line bundles
[L]\in H^{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
, there exists a natural bijection

e_{L}:\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})\to\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\otimes L,\mathfrak{c}_{2}),

for any
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
satisfying the following two properties:

(U1)

e_{L_{1}}\circ e_{L_{2}}=e_{L_{1}\otimes L_{2}}
;

(U2)

the collection
\{e_{L}\}
is compatible with the concatenation map
q
meaning that the diagram

(19.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{12},\mathfrak{c}_{2})\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{23},\mathfrak{c}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{13},\mathfrak{c}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{12}\otimes L_{12},\mathfrak{c}_{2})\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{23}\otimes L_{23},\mathfrak{c}_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{13}\otimes L_{13},\mathfrak{c}_{3})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{L_{12}}\otimes e_{L_{23}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{L_{13}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

is commutative for any relative
spin^{c}\
cobordisms:

\displaystyle(\widehat{X}_{12},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{12}):(Y_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1})

\displaystyle\to(Y_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2}),

\displaystyle(\widehat{X}_{23},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{23}):(Y_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{3})

\displaystyle\to(Y_{3},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{3}).

Here
(\widehat{X}_{13},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{13})=(\widehat{X}_{12}\#\widehat{X}_{23},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{12}\#\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{23})
is the concatenation of relative cobordisms and
L_{13}=L_{12}\#L_{23}
is the concatenation of relative line bundles.

Remark 19.3
.

The proof of Theorem
19.2
is constructive: we will construct each
e_{L}
explicitly and verify properties
(U1)
(U2)
by hands. The key ingredient is the notion of relative orientations, which allows us to reduce the problem from a non-compact manifold
\mathcal{X}
to a closed 4-manifold. In the latter case, we know how to construct
e_{L}
, since the Dirac operator and the self-dual operator are now decoupled. The relevant theory is developed in Appendix
A
. The proof of Theorem
19.2
will be accomplished in Subsection
A.10
.
∎

The horizontal maps
q
in the diagram (
19.2
) require some further explanations. Take non-degenerate pairs
\mathfrak{c}_{i}
on
\widehat{Y}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq 3
. Instead of
\mathcal{Q}
, we look at operators on
\widehat{X}_{ij}
with spectral projections:

(19.3)

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{c}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{ij},\mathfrak{c}_{j})\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}D_{ij}\oplus(\Pi^{+}_{\mathbb{A}_{i}},\Pi^{-}_{\mathbb{A}_{j}})\circ(r_{i},r_{j}),1\leq i<j\leq 3,

understood in the sense of Proposition
13.9
and Subsection
13.4
adapted to the case of general cobordisms. In particular,
\Pi^{\pm}_{\mathbb{A}_{i}}
are spectral projections of the extended Hessians at
\mathfrak{c}_{i}
:

\widehat{\Hess}_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}:L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Y}_{i},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}Y_{i}\oplus S)\to L^{2}_{k}(\widehat{Y}_{i},i\mathbb{R}\oplus iT^{*}Y_{i}\oplus S),\ 1\leq i\leq 3.

The 2-element set
\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{12},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
can be defined using
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{c}_{i},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{ij},\mathfrak{c}_{j})
instead. As explained in
[
KM07
, P. 384]
, there is a canonical bundle isomorphism defined using the map
(
19.1
)
,

(19.4)

q:\det\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{12},\mathfrak{c}_{2})\otimes\det\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{c}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{23},\mathfrak{c}_{3})\to\det\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{13},\mathfrak{c}_{3}).

which descends to an associative multiplication:

q:\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{12},\mathfrak{c}_{2})\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{2},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{23},\mathfrak{c}_{3})\to\Lambda(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{13},\mathfrak{c}_{3}).

Our construction of homology orientations is based upon Theorem
19.2
.

Definition 19.4
.

Following the notations in Theorem
19.2
, for any triple
(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\mathbb{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
, define the 2-element set of
