\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

with
V_{3}
defined as in (
A.15
). Again we have to verify the sum

a_{1}^{\prime}+a_{5}^{\prime}+a_{l}^{\prime}+a_{r}^{\prime}\equiv 0\mod 2

where

\displaystyle a_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=r_{01;34}+r_{12;34}=r_{11;34}+r_{02;34}=a_{5}^{\prime},

\displaystyle a_{l}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Ind D_{13}\Ind D_{14}+(\Ind D_{13}+\Ind D_{14})\Ind D_{03}+\Ind D_{03},

\displaystyle a_{r}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Ind D_{13}\Ind D_{14}+(\Ind D_{13}+\Ind D_{14})\Ind D_{04}+\Ind D_{04}.

If we set
c=\Ind D_{i3}-\Ind D_{i4},\ i\in\{0,1,2\}
, then

a_{1}^{\prime}+a_{5}^{\prime}+a_{l}^{\prime}+a_{r}^{\prime}\equiv c^{2}+c\equiv 0\mod 2.

The last step is to show that the matrix
V_{2}
is homotopic to

V_{3}^{-1}\circ V_{1}\circ V_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{L}&\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{0}&0&\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{-\phi_{R}}\\
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{0}&\phi_{L}&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{-\phi_{R}}&0\\
0&\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\phi_{R}}&\phi_{L}&\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{0}\\
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\phi_{R}}&0&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{0}&\phi_{L}\end{pmatrix}\begin{array}[]{l}:L^{2}_{k}(E_{13}\oplus E_{14}\oplus E_{03}\oplus E_{24})\to\\
\qquad\qquad L^{2}_{k}(E_{14}\oplus E_{13}\oplus E_{04}\oplus E_{23}).\end{array}

The homotopy is again constructed by “rotating” the four entries colored red and the other four entries colored blue. The proofs of Proposition
A.3
and
A.12
are now completed.

A.9.
Proof of Proposition
A.7

We claim that the construction in Proposition
A.12
still works in this general setup, if
\mathscr{L}
is an even class of operators in the sense of Definition
A.6
. If we stick to operators
\widehat{R}=(P,R,Q)\in\widehat{\mathscr{R}}
with
P=\emptyset
, then the proof of Proposition
A.12
remains valid, since it does not see the difference.

In the general case, let

\widehat{R}_{j}=(P_{j},R_{j},Q_{j})\in\widehat{\mathscr{R}},j=3,4.

We wish to compare
p(\widehat{R}_{3},\widehat{R}_{4})
with
p(R_{3},R_{4})
. To illustrate, we focus on the special case when
P_{3}=Q_{3}=\emptyset
and verify the following digram is commutative:

(A.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(D_{13})\Lambda(P_{4})\Lambda(D_{24})\Lambda(Q_{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(P_{4})\Lambda(D_{13})\Lambda(Q_{4})\Lambda(D_{23})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(D_{13})\Lambda(P_{3}\oplus D_{24}\oplus Q_{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(P_{4}\oplus D_{14}\oplus Q_{4})\Lambda(D_{23})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(D_{13}\oplus P_{4}\oplus D_{24}\oplus Q_{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(P_{4}\oplus D_{14}\oplus Q_{4}\oplus D_{23}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id\otimes\bar{p}(R_{3},R_{4})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id\otimes q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q\otimes\Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{p}(R_{3},\widehat{R}_{4})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{13;24}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{14;23}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-1)^{r}U_{*}}

The second square comes from the digram (
A.9
) with
R_{4}
replaced by
\widehat{R}_{4}
; so

U=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&0&0\\
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\phi_{L}}&0&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{-\phi_{R}}&0\\
0&0&0&-1\\
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\phi_{R}}&0&\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{\phi_{L}}&0\end{pmatrix}

and

r=(1+\Ind D_{23})\Ind(L_{2}\#\widehat{R}_{4})=(1+\Ind D_{23})(\Ind P_{4}+\Ind D_{23}+\Ind Q_{3}).

One may verify that the first square of (
A.19
) also is commutative, using diagrams like (
A.14
) and (
A.18
). The computation boils down to

\Ind P_{4}\cdot(\Ind D_{13}+\Ind D_{23})\equiv 0\mod 2,

so the assumption that
\mathscr{L}
is even is crucially here. In general, one has to verify that a digram like (
A.19
) commutes for arbitrary
\widehat{R}_{3},\widehat{R}_{4}\in\widehat{\mathscr{R}}
. This reduces the problem from
\widehat{\mathscr{R}}
to the smaller family
\mathscr{R}
: it suffices to verify axiom
(C-I)
-
(C-IV)
for
(\mathscr{L},\mathscr{R})
, but this is done in Proposition
A.12
. Details are left for the readers.

Finally, to verify the additional property (
A.3
), we set

\widehat{R}_{3}=(\emptyset,R_{3},\emptyset),\ \widehat{R}_{4}=(P_{3},R_{3},Q_{3}),

in the diagram (
A.19
). Then we use the fact that the top arrow
\bar{p}(R_{3},R_{3})=\Id
to conclude.

A.10.
Proof of Theorem
19.2

Having developed the abstract theory of relative orientations, let us explain its application in gauge theory and prove Theorem
19.2
. Consider a strict cobordism
X:Y_{1}\to Y_{2}
, let

Y=\partial X=(-Y_{1})\cup([-1,1]\times\Sigma)\cup Y_{2}.

We regard
Y
as a compact oriented 3-manifold by smoothing the corners.

For any relative
spin^{c}\
cobordism
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})
, let the operator

L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}

be the restriction of the Fredholm operator
\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})
on
X
and
R_{*}
be the restriction on the complement
\mathcal{X}\setminus X
, then

\mathcal{Q}(\mathfrak{c}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X},\mathfrak{c}_{2})=L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}\#R_{*}.

Let
\mathscr{L}=\{L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}:\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})\}
be the space of all such operators. The underlying manifold of
L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}
is always the compact 4-manifold
X
. As for the other class
\mathscr{R}
, let
X_{3}
be any smooth 4-manifold with boundary
(-Y)

such that
X\#X_{3}
is a closed oriented manifold and
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}|_{\partial X}
extends to a
spin^{c}\
structure
\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{3}
on
X\#X_{3}
. Define
R_{3}
to be the linearized Seiberg-Witten map together with the linearized gauge fixing equation on
X\#X_{3}
restricted on
X_{3}
. As a result

L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}\#R_{3}

is the linearized operator at some configuration for the closed
spin^{c}\
manifold
(X\#X_{3},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\#\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{3})
. Set
\mathscr{R}=\{R_{*}\}\cup\{\text{all possible }(X_{3},R_{3})\}
. Our goal is to construct the natural bijection

e_{E}:\Lambda(L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}\#R_{*})\to\Lambda(L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\otimes E}\#R_{*}),

for each relative line bundle
[E]\in H^{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
. (Here we changed the notation for a line bundle to avoid confusion). Using the set of bijections
\{p(R_{3},R_{4})\}
in Proposition
A.3
or
A.12
, we can define
e_{E}
using any compact piece
(X_{3},R_{3})
instead:

e_{E}:\Lambda(L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}\#R_{3})\to\Lambda(L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\otimes E}\#R_{3}).

It is constructed as follows. The linearized operator at a reducible configuration on
X_{3}\#R_{3}
is

(d^{*}\oplus d^{+})\oplus D_{A}^{+}

The second operator is complex linear, while the first one is independent of the line bundle
[E]\in H^{2}(X,\partial X;\mathbb{Z})
, so
e_{E}
is defined by the commutative digram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(d^{*}\oplus d^{+})\Lambda(D_{A}^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(d^{*}\oplus d^{+})\Lambda(D_{A^{\prime}}^{+})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}\#R_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda(L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\otimes L}\#R_{3}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Id\otimes h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{E}}

where
h:\Lambda(D_{A}^{+})\to\Lambda(D_{A^{\prime}}^{+})
preserves the complex orientations. Notice that
\{e_{E}\}
is independent of the compact piece
(X_{3},R_{3})
by our construction of
\{p(R_{3},R_{4})\}
.

Now the first property
(U1)
of Theorem
19.2
follows from Axiom
(C-IV)
.

As for
(U2)
, it suffices to address the special case when either
[E_{12}]=0
or
[E_{23}]=0
. Technically, we have to work with the operators
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
defined in (
19.3
), which involve manifolds with boundary and spectral projections. We can enlarge the family
\mathscr{R}
to incorporate such operators, so it is not a problem.

At this point, we conclude using the additional property (
A.3
) in Proposition
A.7
by setting either
P_{3}=\emptyset
or
Q_{3}=\emptyset
. The assumption is verified by the next lemma.

Lemma A.18
.

The class of operators
\mathscr{L}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{L_{\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}}:\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{X}\in\Spincr(X;\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{s}}_{2})\}
is even in the sense of Definition
A.6
.

Proof of Lemma.

By the excision principle, it suffices to verify the condition (
A.2
) for a special operator
(X_{3},R_{3})\in\mathscr{R}
. In particular, we take
(X_{3},R_{3})
to be a compact piece.
∎

Appendix B
Some Formulae of Taubes

In this appendix, we summarize some formulae of the Seiberg-Witten equations from Taubes’ paper
[
Tau96
, Section 2]
. Although the primary applications of
[
Tau96
]
focus on symplectic 4-manifolds, it is well-known that some of them generalize to any Riemannian 4-manifolds. This observation forms the basis of the finiteness result in Section
20
. For the sake of completeness, we record their statements and prove Lemma
20.3
.

Given an oriented Riemannian 4-manifold
X
, consider the Seiberg-Witten equations on
X
perturbed by a self-dual 2-form
\omega^{+}\in\Gamma(X,i\Lambda^{+}X)
:

(B.1)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\rho_{4}(F_{A^{t}}^{+})-(\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}

\displaystyle=\rho_{4}(\omega^{+}),

(B.2)

\displaystyle D_{A}^{+}\Phi

\displaystyle=0.

The 2-form
\omega^{+}
is not assumed to be closed, and
X
is not necessarily compact. Set

F\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\frac{1}{2}F_{A^{t}}\in\Omega^{2}(X,i\mathbb{R}).

Then the curvature tensor
F_{A}|_{S^{+}}\in\Gamma(X,i\Lambda^{2}X\otimes\End(S^{+}))
can be written as

(B.3)

F_{A}|_{S^{+}}=F\otimes\Id_{S^{+}}+\mathscr{U},

where
\mathscr{U}
is the traceless part of
F_{A}|_{S^{+}}
and is independent of the
spin^{c}\
connection
A
. By the Weitzenböck formula
[
KM07
, (4.14)]
, if
(A,\Phi)
solves (
B.1
)(
B.2
), then

(B.4)

\frac{1}{2}\Delta_{A}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|^{2}\Phi=-\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})\Phi-\frac{s}{4}\Phi.

Our goal is to find explicit formulae for
d^{*}F,\ \Delta|F|^{2}\text{ and }\Delta|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}.

Lemma B.1
.

For any solution
(A,\Phi)
to the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations (
B.1
)(
B.2
), we have

d^{*}F=2d^{*}F^{+}=2d^{*}F^{-}=i\im\langle\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle+2d^{*}\omega^{+}.

Proof.

Since
F
is a closed 2-form,
dF^{-}=-dF^{+}
and
d^{*}F^{-}=d^{*}F^{+}.

To compute
d^{*}F^{+}
, we pick a local orthonormal framing
\{e_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4}
such that
\nabla_{e_{i}}e_{k}=0
at
x\in X
. Moreover, we exploit the formula from
[
LM89
, Lemma 5.13]
:

d^{*}F^{+}=-(\nabla_{e_{i}}F^{+})(e_{i},\cdot).

By the curvature equation (
B.1
), we have

\displaystyle(F^{+}-\omega^{+})(e_{i},e_{k})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\tr(\rho_{4}(F^{+}-\omega^{+})\rho_{4}(e_{i})\rho_{4}(e_{k}))=-\frac{1}{4}\tr((\Phi\Phi^{*})_{0}\rho_{4}(e_{i})\rho_{4}(e_{k})).

Now we use the Dirac equation (
B.2
) to compute:

\displaystyle I\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}

\displaystyle-\nabla_{e_{i}}(F^{+}-\omega^{+})(e_{i},e_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\tr([(\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi)\Phi^{*}+\Phi(\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi)^{*}-\re\langle\Phi,\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi\rangle\otimes\Id_{S^{+}}]\rho_{4}(e_{i})\rho_{4}(e_{k})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\tr(\rho_{4}(e_{i})\rho_{4}(e_{k})(\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi)\Phi^{*})+\frac{1}{4}\tr(\Phi\underbrace{(\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi)^{*}\rho_{4}(e_{i})}_{=0}\rho_{4}(e_{k}))+\frac{1}{2}\re\langle\Phi,\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi\rangle.

For the first term, we commute
\rho_{4}(e_{i})
and
\rho_{4}(e_{k})
to derive:

\displaystyle I=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\tr(\rho_{4}(e_{k})\underbrace{\rho_{4}(e_{k})(\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi)}_{=0}\Phi^{*})+\frac{1}{2}\tr(\rho_{4}(e_{k})\rho_{4}(e_{k})(\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi)\Phi^{*})+\frac{1}{2}\re\langle\Phi,\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\langle\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi,\Phi\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\re\langle\Phi,\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot i\im\langle\Phi,\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi\rangle.

We conclude that
2d^{*}F^{+}=2d^{*}\omega^{+}+i\im\langle\Phi,\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi\rangle\cdot\omega_{k}
, where
\{\omega_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4}
are co-vectors dual to
\{e_{i}\}
.
∎

Now we are read to compute the Hodge Laplacian of the curvature 2-form
F
.

Proposition B.2
.

For any solution
(A,\Phi)
to the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations (
B.1
)(
B.2
), we have

(d+d^{*})^{2}F+|\Phi|^{2}F=\langle\nabla_{A}\Phi\wedge\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle+2dd^{*}\omega^{+}+I(\Phi,\Phi),

where
\langle\nabla_{A}\Phi\wedge\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle
denotes the imaginary valued 2-form

\sum_{i,j}\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{j}\cdot\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}^{A}\Phi\rangle=2i\sum_{i<j}\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{j}\cdot\im\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}^{A}\Phi\rangle,

and
I(\Phi,\Phi)=\sum_{i<k}\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{k}\cdot i\im\langle\Phi,\mathscr{U}(e_{i},e_{k})\Phi\rangle
is a symmetric bilinear form.

Proof.

Since
dF=0
, it suffices to compute
dd^{*}F
. We exploit the formula from
[
LM89
, Lemma 5.13]
:

d\nu=\omega_{i}\wedge\nabla_{e_{i}}\nu

for any
\nu\in\Omega^{*}(X,i\mathbb{R})
.
In particular, set
\nu=i\im\langle\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle
:

\displaystyle d\nu

\displaystyle=\omega_{i}\wedge\nabla_{e_{i}}(\omega_{k}\otimes i\im\langle\Phi,\nabla_{e_{k}}\Phi\rangle)=\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{k}\cdot(i\im\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi\rangle+i\im\langle\Phi,\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi\rangle)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i<k}\omega_{i}\wedge\omega_{k}\cdot(2i\im\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}^{A}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{k}}^{A}\Phi\rangle+i\im\langle\Phi,F_{A}(e_{i},e_{k})\Phi\rangle)

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{A}\Phi\wedge\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle-|\Phi|^{2}F+I(\Phi,\Phi).

At the last step, we used the decomposition

F_{A}|_{S^{+}}=\frac{1}{2}F_{A^{t}}\otimes\Id_{S^{+}}+\mathscr{U}
from (
B.3
).
∎

Finally, we address the Laplacian of
|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}
. Note that

\frac{1}{2}\Delta|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|\Hess_{A}\Phi|^{2}=\re\langle(\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A})\nabla_{A}\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle.

We start with an explicit formula for the commutator
\Delta_{A}\nabla_{A}-\nabla_{A}\Delta_{A}
where
\Delta_{A}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\nabla_{A}^{*}\nabla_{A}
.

Lemma B.3
.

For any
spin^{c}\
connection
A
and any spinor
\Phi
, we have an identity:

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{A}\nabla_{A}\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\nabla_{A}(\Delta_{A}\Phi),\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle-\Ric(e_{i},e_{j})\re\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\re\langle(d^{*}_{A}F_{A})\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle-2\re\langle F_{A}(e_{i},e_{j})\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi\rangle.

Remark B.4
.

The last two terms can be recognized as follows:

\displaystyle d_{A}^{*}F_{A}

\displaystyle=d^{*}F\otimes\Id_{S^{+}}+d^{*}_{A_{0}}\mathscr{U},

\displaystyle\re\langle F_{A}(e_{i},e_{j})\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\re\langle\mathscr{U}(e_{i},e_{j})\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi\rangle+2\sum_{i<j}F(e_{i},e_{j})\cdot i\im\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\re\langle\mathscr{U}(e_{i},e_{j})\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi\rangle-\langle F,\langle\nabla_{A}\Phi\wedge\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle\rangle.

Note that
d^{*}_{A_{0}}\mathscr{U}
is independent of the reference
spin^{c}\
connection
A_{0}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

Let
\{e_{i}=\partial_{i}\}
in a normal coordinate at
x\in X
and
\{\omega_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq 4}
be the co-vectors dual to
\{e_{i}\}
. Then

\displaystyle\nabla_{A}\Phi

\displaystyle=\omega_{i}\otimes\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,

\displaystyle\Delta_{A}\nabla_{A}\Phi

\displaystyle=-\omega_{i}\otimes\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi-\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\omega_{i}\otimes\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi

\displaystyle=-\omega_{i}\otimes\nabla_{e_{i}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi-2\omega_{i}\otimes F_{A}(e_{j},e_{i})\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi

\displaystyle\qquad+\omega_{i}\otimes(d^{*}_{A}F_{A})(e_{i})\Phi-\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\omega_{i}\otimes\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,

\displaystyle\Delta_{A}\Phi

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi-\langle\nabla_{e_{j}}\omega_{i},\omega_{j}\rangle\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,

\displaystyle\nabla_{A}\Delta_{A}\Phi

\displaystyle=-\omega_{i}\otimes\nabla_{e_{i}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi-\omega_{k}\otimes\langle\nabla_{e_{k}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\omega_{i},\omega_{j}\rangle\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,

Now take inner products with
\nabla_{A}\Phi
. To find the Ricci curvature, use relations:

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\omega_{i},\omega_{k}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{j}}e_{k},e_{i}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\nabla_{e_{k}}\nabla_{e_{j}}\omega_{i},\omega_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\nabla_{e_{k}}\nabla_{e_{j}}e_{j},e_{i}\rangle,

and
\nabla_{e_{j}}e_{k}=\nabla_{e_{k}}e_{j}
in a normal neighborhood.
∎

Proposition B.5
.

For any solution
(A,\Phi)
to the perturbed Seiberg-Witten equations, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Delta|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|\Hess_{A}\Phi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|^{2}|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|\langle\nabla_{A}\Phi,\Phi\rangle|^{2}+\frac{s}{4}|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\langle F,\langle\nabla_{A}\Phi\wedge\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle\rangle+J_{1}+J_{2}

where

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle=-2\langle\mathscr{U}(e_{i},e_{j})\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi\rangle-\Ric(e_{i},e_{j})\re\langle\nabla_{e_{i}}\Phi,\nabla_{e_{j}}\Phi\rangle-\re\langle\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})\nabla_{A}\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle,

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle=\re\langle(d_{A_{0}}^{*}\mathscr{U})\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle+\re\langle 2d^{*}\omega^{+}\otimes\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 110.35576pt-\re\langle\rho_{4}(\nabla\omega^{+})\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle-\frac{1}{4}\re\langle ds\otimes\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle.

In particular,
|J_{1}|\leq C|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}
and
|J_{2}|\leq C|\nabla_{A}\Phi||\Phi|
for some function
C:X\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
depending only on
(g_{X},\omega^{+})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.3
and Remark
B.4
, it suffices to compute

\re\langle(d^{*}F)\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle\text{ and }\re\langle\nabla_{A}\Delta_{A}\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle.

For the first term, we apply Lemma
B.1
:

\displaystyle\re\langle(d^{*}F)\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=-|\im\langle\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle|^{2}+\re\langle 2d^{*}\omega^{+}\otimes\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle.

For the second term, we apply
\Delta_{A}
to (
B.4
) to compute

\displaystyle\nabla_{A}\Delta_{A}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|^{2}\nabla_{A}\Phi+\re\langle\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle\Phi=\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})\nabla_{A}\Phi-\rho_{4}(\nabla\omega^{+})\Phi-\frac{s}{4}\nabla_{A}\Phi-\frac{1}{4}ds\otimes\Phi.

To conclude, take the inner product with
\nabla_{A}\Phi
:

\displaystyle\re\langle\nabla_{A}\Delta_{A}\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle+\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|^{2}|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}+|\re\langle\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\re\langle\rho_{4}(\omega^{+})\nabla_{A}\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle-\re\langle\rho_{4}(\nabla\omega^{+})\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle-\frac{s}{4}|\nabla_{A}\Phi|^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\re\langle ds\otimes\Phi,\nabla_{A}\Phi\rangle.\qed

Finally, let us state the corresponding results for 3-manifolds from which one can easily deduce Lemma
20.3
.

Proposition B.6
.

Let
(Y,g_{Y})
be any Riemannian 3-manifold,
\Rm_{Y}
be the curvature tensor and
\omega\in\Omega^{2}(Y,i\mathbb{R})
be a closed 2-form. For any solution
(B,\Psi)
to the 3-dimensional Seiberg-Witten equations (
3.6
), write

F=\frac{1}{2}F_{B^{t}}\in\Omega^{2}(Y,i\mathbb{R}).

Then we have

d^{*}F=i\im\langle\Psi,\nabla_{B}\Psi\rangle+d^{*}\omega,

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Delta|\nabla_{B}\Psi|^{2}+

\displaystyle|\Hess_{B}\Psi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|^{2}|\nabla_{B}\Psi|^{2}+|\langle\nabla_{B}\Psi,\Psi\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\langle F,\langle\nabla_{B}\Psi\wedge\nabla_{B}\Psi\rangle\rangle+J_{1}(\nabla_{B}\Psi,\nabla_{B}\Psi)+J_{2}(\Psi,\nabla_{B}\Psi).

where
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
are certain bilinear maps depending only on
R_{Y}
,
\omega
and their first derivatives. In particular, if

\|\Rm_{Y}\|_{L^{\infty}_{1}},\|\omega\|_{L^{\infty}_{1}}<\infty,

then

|J_{1}(\nabla_{B}\Psi,\nabla_{B}\Psi)|\leq C|\nabla_{B}\Psi|^{2}\text{ and }|J_{2}(\nabla_{B}\Psi,\Psi)|\leq C|\nabla_{B}\Psi||\Psi|,

for some constant
C>0
.

Proposition
B.6
follows from its 4-dimensional analogue: Lemma
B.1
and Proposition
B.5
.
